
--- COMMENT ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471-0137 AND DOCKET ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 ---
Clean Source August 2024 
 
 
Feature Article: 
 
Survival of the Fittest, Critical Cleaning, and EPA Amended TSCA Rules - Coping with 
Change 
 
Barbara Kanegsberg and Ed Kanegsberg, BFK Solutions LLC 
 
Note: Analysis and comments reﬂect the status as of August 31, 2024. Speciﬁcally, a ﬁnal rule for 
MC has been issued and proposed rules for PCE, TCE and nPB (1-BP) have been published. These 
analyses and comments are self-funded by BFK Solutions.  
 
“Survival of the ﬁttest” is a tenet of Charles Darwin’s evolution of species. Many United 
States manufacturers requiring critical product cleaning must develop survival strategies 
and tactics. One immediate source of this existential crisis are new and impending 
regulations from U.S. EPA Amended TSCA. The regulations restrict or even prohibit use of 
certain eWective, aggressive chlorinated and brominated solvents in vapor degreasing and 
related activities. The most recent update is the proposed rule to manage n-propyl bromide 
(nPB). Manufacturers have only until September 23 to comment. Be aware that EPA refers 
not to nPB but to 1-bromopropane (1-BP). Manufacturers who want to survive should 
peruse the proposed 1-BP rule.  Manufacturers who use or who have suppliers of 
components or product with cleaning processes that depend on Methylene Chloride (MC), 
Perchloroethylene (PCE) or Trichloroethylene (TCE) should peruse the 1-BP rule. The 
proposed rule provides possible paths forward for manufacturers. Understanding all the 
rules, all the diWerent EPA strategies, will enhance your ability as a manufacturer to be 
among the “survival of the ﬁttest.” What does “ﬁttest” mean? In Darwinian terms, ﬁttest 

does not mean the strongest; ﬁttest does not mean the most intelligent. Fittest means the 
“most adaptable to change.”  How might manufacturers adapt to changes impelled by EPA 
regulations? How much change is needed? What are the costs?  In this article we discuss 
the proposed nPB (1-BP) rule AND compare it with the ﬁnal rule for MC and the proposed 
rules for TCE and PCE. As principals at BFK Solutions, we intend to submit our comments to 
the 1-BP (nPB) docket. 
 
nPB  (1-BP)  
On August 8, 2024, the Proposed Rule, “1-Bromopropane (1–BP); Regulation Under the Toxic 
Substances Control Act (TSCA)”, was published in the Federal Register (1). Under the 
proposed rule, everyone using nPB must change their cleaning processes or certify that 
their current processes meet EPA requirements. The primary concern with nPB, and with 
the other three solvents under consideration is worker exposure, not environmental 
releases.  
 
Staying below the worker exposure limit will be much more diWicult than meeting an OSHA 
standard. The reason is that a few years back, the U.S. Congress passed what is usually 
referred to as “Lautenberg”(2) with bipartisan support. Under Lautenberg, EPA was tasked 
with eliminating “unreasonable risk” to not only to the environment but also to workers. EPA 
has established an Existing Chemical Exposure Level (ECEL). The ECEL is very diWerent from 
limits set by OSHA or AGCIH (3) (Table 1).  For a few, particularly those using nPB sealed, 
reduced pressure solvent systems, survival adaptation may be no more than providing a 
required “self-certiﬁcation” that they currently meet EPA requirements for worker exposure. 
However, meeting those requirements is likely to be very challenging for most users.  
 
Table 1: Exposure levels for halogenated solvents 
 
 

nPB would be allowed in vapor degreasers. The ECEL for N-propyl bromide is 0.05 ppm.  EPA 
asserts that a level of 0.05 ppm could be achieved. Frankly, based on our experience and 
because most manufacturers use nPB open-top degreasers, we ﬁnd this blanket assertion 
to be – what’s the word? Curious, aspirational, perhaps unrealistic. The assertion that ECEL 
can be reached in most situations is not realistic. Certainly, there could be a few situations 
where the EPA assertion is viable. For example, in a small R&D group or a manufacturer with 
very low product throughput, a small open-top vapor degreaser could be placed in an area 
remote from employees and used once or twice a day for no more than 1 to 2 minutes. 
 
Further, until recently, nPB was not listed as a hazardous air pollutant (HAP) and therefore 
did not have to be used in NESHAP compliant degreasers with increased freeboard. Spray 
wands are often used to access all surfaces of the part being cleaned. This means the 
employee is standing over the vapor degreaser and may spray solvent relatively close to 
their face. If the process is not automated, there will be high employee exposure. The levels 
achieved must be even lower than 0.05 ppm, because the proposed rule calls for an action 
level of 0.03 ppm. If the EPA has found examples of employee monitoring indicating 
successful control with open top degreasers, it would be enlightening for that data were to 
be made publicly available.  
 
We expect current nPB processes to be discontinued in most applications. Substitute 
processes would involve process change and/or chemical substitution. 
 
Sealed, reduced pressure cleaning systems 
Vapor degreasing in nPB ought not be prohibited. Assertions of compliance with an 
exceedingly low ECEL sets up false hope. The nPB proposed rule, and rules for MC, TCE, and 
PCE, should be revised to encourage and support cleaning process technology. The dream 
of solvent substitution as a solution is just that – a dream. Manufacturers use halogenated 
solvents not through ignorance or laziness but because they have the physical and 
chemical properties to clean eWectively. 
 
Rather than targeting individual chemicals one at a time, pathways to success ought to be 
suggested by EPA and encouraged by EPA. Manufacturers who make the signiﬁcant 
ﬁnancial investment in capital equipment, employee education, process development and 
process validation/veriﬁcation should be encouraged and rewarded. EPA should work with 
other agencies to develop incentivization programs.  
 
nPB and similar solvents can be used in sealed, reduced pressure cleaning machines (often 
referred to as airless/airtight systems). For decades (probably since the 1980s) such 
systems have been successful to minimize releases of volatile chemicals. Their use should 
be encouraged and rewarded by EPA. We implore EPA to develop and post a category for 
such systems, as opposed to using the incorrect and misleading terms likely “sealed, 
enclosed, or closed-loop.” EPA might consider such systems as examples of BACT (Best 
Available Controlled Technology); or EPA might prefer another acronym.  
 
EPA needs an accurate, encompassing deﬁnition for such systems, one that includes 
diWerences in design.  We refer Clean Source readers to our January 2024 article (4). One 
reason reduced pressure cleaning machines have not been recognized as adequate may be 
that those who manufacture or supply such systems seem to foster only their technology is 

acceptable. BFK Solutions has ZERO desire to head up an ASTM or ISO committee on the 
topic! Here is our fervent plea to providers of such systems: Stop (verbally) bashing each 
other over the head! Please stop saying only your technology works! In our observations, 
many similar technologies are acceptable. Help the EPA make critical product cleaning safe 
and eWective. 
 
Disparate regulatory approaches 
Assessments and rule development under EPA amended TSCA have proceeded during the 
same timeframe and presumably using the same approaches. The rules/proposed rules for 
MC, PCE, TCE and nPB ought to look relatively similar. They don’t! (See Table 2)

As we will explain, the compliance pathway in the proposed rule for nPB is quite diWerent 
and clashes with rules or proposed rules for MC, PCE, and TCE. Manufacturers who use MC, 
PCE, or TCE or who have suppliers using MC, PCE, or TCE should immediately comment 
about nPB and request harmonized, reasonable, and achievable rules for those solvents.  
 
Harmonized rules are a must 
The proposed rule for nPB handles solvent management and employee exposure in a far 
diWerent manner than to analogous rules/proposed rules for MC, TCE, and PCE. In fact, 
each of the four rules take a diWerent approach. Manufacturers will ﬁnd adapting to the new 
rules to be more accessible if the rules resemble each other. 
 
The ECEL for methylene chloride (MC) is 2 ppm – that’s 40 time higher than the ECEL for 
nPB. The ﬁnal rule for MC categorically and, to us illogically, prohibits MC in vapor 
degreasers, not even in reduced pressure sealed systems. Granted, MC has a STEL (short 
term exposure limit). The presence of a STEL should not in and of itself be a barrier to using 
MC. To de facto prohibit such use seems to us to be an unwarranted example of in loco 
parentis for adult workers. 

 
The ECEL for PCE is 0.14 ppm – that’s 2.8 times higher than nPB.  The proposed rule for PCE 
does not provide a similar expectation that manufacturers could comply with the ECEL in 
vapor degreasing, even though one might expect lower emissions because the boiling point 
of PCE is somewhat higher than that of nPB, and even though, as a NESHAP solvent, PCE 
tends to be used in more modern, less-emissive degreasers.   
 
The ECEL for TCE is exceedingly low, 0.06 that of nPB.  Vapor degreasing with TCE is 
generally prohibited, with an extended time for some NASA uses. WCPP self-certiﬁcation is 
not proposed. It seems to us illogical to prohibit use of TCE, without providing a mechanism 
to demonstrate eWectiveness appropriate engineering controls. Also, extended time 
exemptions of up to 10 years for NASA and defense uses seems highly unreasonable to us. 
Accepting that TCE is so highly dangerous, the lives of people working at NASA and at 
certain defense contractors should not be put in danger. If NASA and defense contractors 
can control exposure to TCE, why can’t the rest of us? And why does there have to be a 10 
year phaseout time?  In addition, why should only “the big guys” get extensions. Small 
medical device manufacturers also provide product that is “safety-critical”. We discuss 
“safety-critical” and other categories of cleaning a bit further down in this article. 
 
Inﬂuence change; adapt to change 
We think manufacturers should take advantage of the opportunity to make comments to 
the EPA about all four solvents. Based on our interpretation of the proposed rule for 1-BP, 
the mechanism for making comments appears in Section VIII of the proposed 1-BP rule. In 
Section VIII, the ECEL for not only nPB about also for the other 3 solvents would be 
formalized in a diWerent EPA document, including solvents where the comment period has 
passed and even where a ﬁnal rule has been issued. We suggest that the rule for MC should 
be considered as cast not in concrete but in wet, mushy concrete – or, at least that’s what 
should happen. All you folks ought to speak up right away, to better protect the 
environment, employees, and U.S. manufacturing! 
 
Changes in the cleaning processes are inevitable for many manufacturers. Changes may be 
impelled by new, more stringent customer demands on product quality or product 
performance. This includes new standards limiting contamination level. Becoming the 
ﬁttest may involve becoming more cost-eWective by replacing old, ineWicient cleaning 
equipment with cost-eWective processes that achieve reliable cleaning.   
 
Considerations associated with amended TSCA activities include the costs and aggravation 
in evaluating new equipment and developing and validating new cleaning processes.  Your 
company or your customers’ company may institute policies that go beyond the EPA 
proposals and rules. Corporate policy may ban even a whisper of certain chemicals. 
Particularly with complex supply chains, “equivalent” cleaning processes that don’t really 
work as well may replace the tried-and-true aggressive solvents. This is exacerbated by the 
fact that chemical suppliers may elect to cease U.S. production; we have already seen this 
with trichloroethylene. 
 
Regrettable substitution 
In the 1-BP proposed rule, we ﬁnd a single use of the phrase “regrettable substitution” 
(Section IV(A)1biii). Most people consider the concept of “regrettable substation to be using 

an alternative chemical that then becomes subject to safety/environmental investigation. 
Flashpoint-inerted trans-DCE azeotropic blends contain chemicals that are or may be 
considered PFAS; and trans-DCE is itself under scrutiny by EPA amended TSCA.  How long 
will they be available? Who knows.  Given these considerations, these blends often 
considered a short-term substitute. They could be part of a good survival strategy 
depending on performance and the eWort needed to validate the new process. Substitute 
chemicals and processes come with other risks to employee safety; and those risks are not 
suWiciently outlined or addressed. Flammability is a real issue. It can be managed. 
However, improperly handled, there can be risks to people and property. Many substitute 
chemicals and chemical blends have not been adequately studied. 
 
Cleanliness categories – consequences of not cleaning 
Other substitute chemicals are dissimilar to nPB, MC, TCE, and PCE.  In general, the more 
dissimilar the chemical, the more process development will be needed. The regulations for 
the four solvents are so burdensome to most manufacturers that not-in-kind substitutes 
require more cleanliness and materials compatibility testing than do more closely related 
solvents. Examples of not in-kind substitutes relative the four halogenated solvents include 
hydrocarbons, modiﬁed alcohol, isopropyl alcohol, water and aqueous blends, HFEs and 
HFOs used without trans-DCE, d-limonene as well as “chemical free” processes (eg. steam, 
laser, or plasma cleaning).  
 
Particularly with not-in-kind substitutes, adaptation changes in the cleaning process that 
are impelled by EPA amended TSCA require substantial eWort and investment. The amount 
of cleaning process development and testing depends on the negative impact of 
inadequate cleaning or not cleaning on the ultimate produce. Investment in new processes 
includes substantial testing for cleanliness veriﬁcation/validation.  
 
When we teach or help clients, we often explain that process development, cleanliness 
testing, cleaning process validation is particularly crucial in areas where public safety, 
defense capability, and patient safety are issues. Some people refer to this type of cleaning 
as “Where Failure is Not an Option.” Because our background includes defense, aerospace, 
pharma, and medical device manufacturing, we teach the category where “if you don’t 
clean it right, airplanes and missiles fall out of the sky and patient’s asses fall oW.” EPA has 
wisely chosen the far more elegant term “Safety/Critical Cleaning.” In the safety/critical 
category, unintended consequences to the product include ineWective unacceptable 
residue. Unintended consequences also include unforeseen, undesirable interaction with 
materials of construction and, in the case of medical devices with human biological 
systems. 
 
Back in what we fondly recall as “The Great Freon Festival”, the EPA Signiﬁcant New 
Alternative Policy (SNAP) Program was instituted to address use of Ozone-depleting 
Substances (ODS) that were phased out due to the Montreal Protocol agreement. The SNAP 
program, which is wide ranging and is active today, covers diverse areas like aerosols, 
refrigerants, ﬁre suppressants, and cleaning. Under SNAP there are three cleaning 
categories, Metals Cleaning, Electronics Cleaning and Precision Cleaning. The three 
cleaning categories reﬂect the era when what became SNAP was being developed (starting 
in the 1980s). in the nPB economic analysis, EPA includes four categories of cleaning in the 
Economic Analysis Section 7.7 of the Economic Analysis for 1-BP (6) 

 
We use a similar approach in teaching cleaning to students, practicing engineers, and 
professionals in process cleaning. We teach these four categories of cleaning (Table 3); 
more details are available in a publicly available webinar (5) 
 
We suggest that the four categories be included in the ﬁnal rule for 1-BP and in any revisions 
of rules for MC, TCE, and PCE. One reason is to avoid the consequences of “regrettable 
substitution” and “unintended consequences.”  We suggest that “self-certiﬁcation” by 
solvent users should include the category(ies) to which they need to clean as well as how 
they are approaching the requirements of the rule. We suggest that manufacturers requiring 
safety/critical cleaning be provided with additional time to comply with the regulation. We 
suggest that it is counterproductive and in our humble opinion dangerous to public safety 
and patient safety to push those manufacturers toward solvent substitution. The risks of 
“regrettable substitution” and “unintended consequences” are simply unacceptable! 
 
Table 3: Cleaning categories 
Cleaning Category 
Major features 
General cleaning 
• Preliminary cleaning 
• Gross soil removal 
• Visual inspection suZicient 
• Little to no testing required 
Precision cleaning

• Consistency required

Adaptation 
Adaptation is the key to survival. Here are some ﬁnal thoughts. 
• 
Be vigilant – be aware of impending changes. 
 
• 
Submit comments to the EPA. 
 
• 
Change the cleaning process methodically and logically. 
o Use a team with the appropriate expertise, in-house and external.  
o Be educated about the pros and cons of alternative cleaning processes.  
o Run cleaning tests on your product. This includes waste management and 
cleanliness testing.   
o Estimate the full costs of process conversion. Set up a budget. We almost 
guarantee you will underestimate the degree of eWort (7).  

 
• 
Change your manufacturing plan to something that does not require cleaning. 
 
• 
Move your manufacturing facility overseas. This adaptation comes with a high cost.  
 
Some adaptations are unwise 
§ 
Ignore the problem; and hope someone else solves it for you. This is unlikely in 
the long term. It’s more likely that bigger companies will move to areas in the 
world with less stringent regulations. 
• Expect large companies and the military to protect you.  “Special treatment” is 
evanescent and unreliable. Large entities protect themselves. 
 
§ 
Pick a cleaning process based only on sales claims. Don’t run cleaning tests. 
 
§ 
Assume that it won’t cost that much to change 
 
§ 
Stop manufacturing. This is a great strategy if you are independently wealthy 
and/or ready to retire.  
 
For over 30 years, BFK Solutions has been helping manufacturers to adapt their cleaning 
processes in order to survive in this rapidly changing and challenging environment.

--- COMMENT ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471-0130 AND DOCKET ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 ---
September 11, 2024 
 
To Whom it May Concern; 
 
I am writing to comment on the EPA’s proposed regulation for use of 1-bromopropane as a 
laboratory chemical under the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA) (Docket ID EPA-HQ-OPPT-
2020-0471). While I appreciate the EPA’s efforts to protect public health and close existing 
regulatory gaps, I believe the proposed rule has not considered the nature of laboratory 
operations. It is, therefore, poorly tailored and creates an unnecessary regulatory and economic 
burden. 
EPA should align its requirements for laboratory use of hazardous chemicals, including 1-
bromopropane, more closely with OSHA’s Laboratory Standard. EPA should develop a common 
regulatory approach to mitigating the risks of chemical exposures in laboratories, and apply that 
approach to all chemicals for which EPA has regulated, is currently proposing to regulate, or may 
regulate in the future, under TSCA. 
Shortcomings of Existing Regulation 
To date, the use of hazardous chemicals in laboratories has been regulated primarily by OSHA’s 
Laboratory Standard (29 CFR 1910.1450). This represents a significant improvement for 
laboratories over previous OSHA regulations including the Hazard Communication Standard and 
substance-specific regulations in 29 CFR 1910 Subpart Z. However, while the Laboratory 
Standard has been beneficial, OSHA’s regulations fail to protect non-employees (e.g. students 
working in academic labs) and many public-sector employees. This is a notable gap that EPA 
can, should, and has begun to address in recent rulemakings under TSCA. It is crucial that any 
changes in the final rule maintain this advantage over the existing OSHA regulation. This will 
ensure a more comprehensive and up-to-date approach to safety in laboratories, benefiting all 
who work within them. 
Common Approach to Chemical Regulation Aligned with OSHA Standards 
EPA should develop a common approach to mitigating the risks of chemical exposures in 
laboratories and apply this approach to all chemicals regulated under TSCA. This would reduce 
the paperwork and economic burdens on laboratories, which use hundreds to thousands of 
chemicals. A single, holistic Chemical Hygiene Plan is more effective than a multitude of chemical-
specific compliance programs. 
A unified approach would not only streamline compliance but also enhance safety by providing a 
consistent framework for managing chemical risks. Laboratories are dynamic environments 
where the introduction of new chemicals and procedures is routine. A common regulatory 
approach would allow laboratories to adapt more swiftly and effectively to new regulations, 
ensuring that safety measures are always up-to-date and comprehensive. Aligning with OSHA’s 
Laboratory Standard (29 CFR 1910.1450) would ensure that the regulations are practical and 
relevant to the actual conditions in which 1-bromopropane is used in laboratories. 

To date, EPA’s proposed and final rulemakings under TSCA have not followed a consistent 
framework. For example, the final rule for methylene chloride requires laboratory operators to 
maintain records of “Personal protective equipment (PPE) and respiratory protection used by 
potentially exposed persons,” whereas the proposed rules for perchloroethylene and carbon 
tetrachloride require that these records include “the name, workplace address, work shift, job 
classification, and work area of each person … and the type of PPE selected to be worn by each 
of these persons” as well as “The basis for specific PPE selection.” EPA’s proposed rule for NMP 
introduced a requirement that operators post prescriptive signage on regulated areas that was 
not included in previous rulemakings. EPA’s proposed rules for carbon tetrachloride, 
perchloroethylene, and NMP each require operators to develop direct dermal contact controls 
(DDCC), which is not present in the proposed rule for 1-bromopropane, despite the requirement 
to provide for dermal protection. The present proposed rule would also introduce several novel 
requirements not previously included in EPA’s TSCA rulemaking, including the proposed self-
certification requirement, and prohibition against releases caused by implementation of 
engineering controls. 
While these variations in requirements may be insignificant for operators of facilities that use a 
single chemical regulated under TSCA, they dramatically increase the economic burden of 
compliance for laboratories that may use all of them. 
EPA should develop a common approach to mitigating the risks of chemical exposures in 
laboratories and apply this approach to all chemicals regulated under TSCA. 
Use of 1-Bromopropane as a Laboratory Chemical 
EPA’s determination that 1-bromopropane poses an unreasonable risk when used as a laboratory 
chemical was not based on exposure data associated with laboratory use (EPA Document #740-
R1-8013, page 123). EPA chose to use exposure data from aerosol degreasing as the basis for 
estimating exposures from laboratory use of 1-bromopropane. However, no data or evidence 
have been provided to support a claim that aerosol exposures are a valid proxy for laboratory 
exposures. There are several reasons to believe that they are not. EPA’s schematic of the Near-
Field/Far-Field Model for Aerosol Degreasing (EPA Document #740-R1-8013, page 107) shows 
a worker applying a 1-bromopropane aerosol to a part ~18” from their face, without any 
engineering control present. Laboratory use of chemicals, however, involves chemicals contained 
in process vessels, which are manipulated in chemical fume hoods that separate the user from 
those chemicals by means of a sash and inward airflow. Further evidence that laboratory use of 
1-bromopropane does not involve aerosol application can be found in the products marketed to 
laboratories. MilliporeSigma (product IDs B78106 and 8.01667), Thermo Fisher Scientific 
Chemicals (product ID A10461), TCI America (product ID B0638), and Spectrum Chemical 
(product ID B1157) all sell 1-bromopropane for laboratory use. All of those products are liquids in 
glass bottles at atmospheric pressure. None are aerosol containers. 
EPA did obtain exposure data for a use of 1-bromopropane that is a viable proxy for laboratory 
use – processing as a reactant. According to EPA’s risk evaluation, this is “the use of 1-BP as a 
raw material in the production of another chemical, in which 1-BP is reacted and consumed.” This 
is the predominant use of 1-bromopropane in laboratories. The primary factor that distinguishes 
processing as a reactant from laboratory use is scale – laboratory use involves vessels that can 
be easily manipulated by a single person and quantities that can be transferred between vessels 
using handheld containers as opposed to the multi-ton scales that may be used in industrial 

chemical facilities. Therefore, while it is accurate to suggest that laboratory exposures may be as 
high as industrial processing as a reactant depending on the use volume, engineering control, 
and PPE, it is not accurate to suggest that laboratory exposures may be as high as exposures 
from aerosol degreasing. The exposure data for processing 1-bromopropane as a reactant had 
central tendencies of 3.83 x 10-3 ppm and 1.52 x 10-3 ppm, both of which are below the proposed 
ECEL. 
If EPA continues to base its risk estimate for laboratory use of 1-bromopropane on 
exposures from aerosol degreasing, it should define laboratory use to include only aerosol 
processes, and should only require implementation of a Workplace Chemical Protection 
Plan for laboratories that use 1-bromopropane as an aerosol. 
WCPP Framework 
The current proposed rule requires a Workplace Chemical Protection Plan (WCPP) that includes 
Direct Dermal Contact Controls for 1-bromopropane. While this appears to offer flexibility, the 
requirements for the WCPP are not well-suited to laboratory operations. Laboratories use 1-
bromopropane in a wide variety of operations, often in small quantities. 
Laboratories frequently handle 1-bromopropane in volumes ranging from 0.1 to 500 mL. The 
chemical is typically both stored and used inside of various containers and vessels, and is 
transferred between containers using handheld equipment such as syringes, cannulas, or funnels. 
The vessels themselves are manipulated within controlled environments such as fume hoods or 
glove boxes. These methods and settings inherently minimize the risk of direct dermal contact. 
This stands in stark contrast to industrial applications where 1-bromopropane might be used in 
larger quantities, in more open environments, and in applications such as coatings or adhesive 
removers that increase risk of direct dermal contact. In other industrial settings such as 
manufacturing the use of 1-bromopropane might involve continuous processes that are highly 
engineered and are performed consistently for many years. 
The WCPP framework is identical for industries with well-defined, consistent operations and for 
laboratories with diverse, variable operations. This one-size-fits-all approach is inappropriate for 
laboratories, which may only perform specific operations once and handle 1-bromopropane 
exclusively in containers, making direct dermal contact unlikely. Clarifying the definition of direct 
dermal contact in the context of laboratory operations would help ensure that the regulations are 
appropriately targeted and do not impose unnecessary burdens on laboratory personnel. 
EPA should adopt a Laboratory Standard under TSCA that requires development of a 
single, holistic Workplace Chemical Protection Plan rather than require development of a 
multitude of distinct chemical-specific Workplace Chemical Protection Plans. 
Use of the Hierarchy of Controls 
The hierarchy of controls is a useful framework for structuring assessments of risk mitigations. 
However, documenting its use in each operation is not useful. As highlighted previously, 
laboratories are dynamic environments which frequently introduce new chemicals, and may only 
perform a specific operation a single time. While it may be sensible to document the rationale for 
eschewing elimination or substitution of 1-bromopropane in a manufacturing plant, to do so for 
every operation performed in a laboratory simply generates excessive paperwork. 

Any requirement to incorporate the hierarchy of controls should clarify that operators of 
laboratories will be permitted to satisfy such requirement by incorporating general 
provisions that apply across all operations into their existing documentation (e.g. a 
Chemical Hygiene Plan). 
Prohibition Against Increased Releases 
EPA is requesting comment on how the proposed requirement that owners or operators attest 
that the engineering controls selected do not increase emissions of 1-bromopropane to ambient 
air outside of the workplace may impact the availability, feasibility, or cost of engineering controls 
as a means to reduce workplace exposures to or below the proposed ECEL.  
The predominant form of engineering control employed in laboratories is the chemical fume hood. 
This proposal would significantly decrease the availability and feasibility of using fume hoods to 
control 1-bromopropane exposures, by significantly increasing the cost and complexity of 
constructing and operating fume hood systems. Existing chemical fume hoods are typically not 
equipped with any equipment to capture contaminants. Therefore, existing fume hoods would not 
be available to laboratory workers if this rule were implemented. In some cases, it may be possible 
over time to install appropriate scrubbers, but that will not be possible for all fume hoods. Chemical 
fume hoods are used to capture a wide range of acidic, basic, and organic vapors and gases, as 
well as organic and inorganic particulates. This is particularly true for manifolded fume hood 
systems. No technology exists which would be capable of efficiently and effectively removing such 
a broad range of contaminants, or of capturing 1-bromopropane in the presence of those other 
contaminants. Any equipment installed to remove contaminants from exhaust gases will take up 
physical space, increase the weight that must be supported by the building, and will require energy 
to run. Each of these will be compounded by the need to replace existing blower motors with 
larger models that consume more energy to compensate for the added flow restriction from the 
treatment equipment. Where these conditions can be satisfied, the installed equipment would also 
generate significant quantities of hazardous waste in the form of contaminated filters or scrubbing 
liquid. 
EPA is requesting comment on the types and costs of technologies firms would adopt to comply 
with the prohibition on increased releases of 1-bromopropane to outdoor air associated with 
engineering controls used in the implementation of the WCPP. The most likely technology that 
operators of laboratories would first implement would be a venturi scrubber. The cost of this is 
difficult to estimate within the timeframe allotted for comment. Laboratory fume hood systems may 
have flow rates ranging from 600 cfm to over 200,000 cfm, and may be used to exhaust gases 
that require varying materials of construction. An operator of laboratories might have upwards of 
1200 chemical fume hoods. This would equate to approximately 900,000 cfm of exhaust. 
According to EPA’s Air Pollution Control Technology Fact Sheet for Venturi Scrubbers (EPA-
452/F-03-017), and adjusted to 2024 dollars (using the Bureau of Labor Statistics’ CPI Inflation 
Calculator), installation and operation of venturi scrubbers on all chemical fume hoods could have 
an annualized cost of over $308 million, not including the cost for treatment and disposal of 
generated waste or of upgrading existing fume hood blower motors.  
The benefit this would provide remains unproven. EPA’s risk evaluation for 1-bromopropane 
found that it does not present an unreasonable risk of injury to health (general population). EPA 
did not evaluate risks to the general population from ambient air and disposal pathways for any 

conditions of use. This absence of data does not establish a basis for imposing such a costly 
requirement on operators of laboratories. 
EPA should not implement the proposal to prohibit releases of 1-bromopropane to outdoor 
air, nor should EPA require operators of laboratories to conduct monitoring for the 
purpose of identifying increases in emissions. Failing that, EPA should clarify that, for 
laboratories, the continued use of fume hoods does not constitute an increased release of 
1-bromopropane to outdoor air. 
Restricted Areas and Warning Signs 
EPA has requested comment on designation of “restricted areas” and on warning signage for the 
same. OSHA’s General Industry Standard for Beryllium (29 CFR 1910.1024(m)(2)) is offered as 
a potential model for these requirements. 
EPA should carefully consider the current and future scope of rulemaking under TSCA, and how 
those rules will apply cumulatively to laboratories when crafting any such requirements. In a 
typical laboratory, the entire lab room would typically be considered the regulated area. Numerous 
chemicals are used in various experiments, and the set of chemicals in simultaneous use changes 
daily. A typical academic organic chemistry laboratory can keep between 2000 and 5000 unique 
chemicals on hand. It is entirely foreseeable, therefore, that a single laboratory could be a 
regulated 
area 
for 
1-bromopropane, 
1,1-dichloroethane, 
1,1,2-trichloroethane, 
1,2-
dichloroethane, 1,2-dichloroethylene, 1,2-dichloropropane, 1,3-butadiene, 1,4-dioxane, multiple 
phthalates, acetaldehyde, acrylonitrile, aniline, carbon tetrachloride, ethylene dibromide, 
formaldehyde, 
methylene 
chloride, 
N-methylpyrrolidone, 
o-dichlorobenzene, 
octamethylcyclotetrasiloxane, 
p-dichlorobenzene, 
perchloroethylene, 
tris(2-chloroethyl) 
phosphate, and vinyl chloride. 
The warning sign required by §1910.1024(m)(2) reads: 
DANGER 
REGULATED AREA 
BERYLLIUM 
MAY CAUSE CANCER 
CAUSES DAMAGE TO LUNGS 
AUTHORIZED PERSONNEL ONLY 
WEAR RESPIRATORY PROTECTION AND PERSONAL PROTECTIVE CLOTHING AND 
EQUIPMENT IN THIS AREA 
In order that it be legible, a sign containing this language would have to be at least the size of a 
3” x 5” index card. The foreseeable laboratory above could be required to post 345 sq. in. of 
signage containing nearly 600 words. It is not difficult to envision that chemical-intensive 
laboratories would eventually be physically unable to comply with all required postings as they 
would occupy a space larger than the area of the door. 
EPA should adopt a Laboratory Standard under TSCA that requires development of a 
single, holistic Workplace Chemical Protection Plan rather than require development of a 

multitude of distinct chemical-specific Workplace Chemical Protection Plans. This should 
include unified requirements for laboratory signage with sufficient flexibility to 
accommodate requirements imposed by other regulatory frameworks (e.g. OSHA, NFPA). 
Dermal Protection and Personal Protective Equipment Selection 
EPA is soliciting comments on the requirements proposed for appropriate PPE selection. EPA 
also requests comment on the degree to which additional guidance related to use of PPE might 
be appropriate.  
EPA’s proposed requirements for respiratory protection are appropriate, and should be enacted 
as proposed. EPA’s proposed requirements for dermal protection should be modified for 
laboratory use of 1-bromopropane. Dermal exposures to chemicals in laboratories occur 
predominantly through incidental splashes, not continuous contact. Chemicals are not used in 
sufficient quantities for a worker to immerse their hands, and they are not used outside of storage 
containers or process vessels. Gloves can also be changed frequently – it is common practice in 
laboratories to immediately doff and dispose of gloves that come in contact with a laboratory 
chemical, and replace them with a clean, unused pair. The small quantities of material utilized, 
and the smaller size of laboratory equipment which often requires fine manipulations, also elevate 
the need for manual dexterity in glove selection. Because of this, a lower level of protection is 
appropriate in laboratories than in other industrial uses of chemicals. The 8th edition of the 
Permeation/Degradation Resistance Guide for Ansell Chemical Resistant Gloves (Ansell Guide) 
is a useful source for information to support glove selection. While it shows that polyvinyl alcohol 
or laminate film gloves are undoubtedly good choices when handling 1-bromopropane, the Ansell 
Guide shows that viton/butyl gloves have a permeation breakthrough time of 182 minutes and 
nitrile gloves have a breakthrough time of 23 minutes. Either of these would afford a user sufficient 
time to replace their gloves in the event of a splash or spill without compromising safety. 
However, neither the Ansell Guide nor any other publicly available resource of which I’m aware 
provides information regarding protection against combinations of chemicals. The likely 
combinations of chemicals that laboratory workers may be reasonably expected to be exposed to 
when working with 1-bromopropane are functionally infinite. The scope of the Laboratory 
Standard is defined in part by the use of multiple chemicals or chemical procedures. Most of these 
combinations will not have been envisioned by glove manufacturers, and, therefore, will not have 
been tested. Glove selection must also account for non-chemical hazards such as heat and 
considerations such as the ability to manipulate equipment without slipping. These factors cannot 
be foreseen in advance for all cases making it unwise for glove materials to be specified in 
regulation. 
Glove selection requirements should allow for reliance on the professional judgment of 
Chemical Hygiene Officers and Certified Industrial Hygienists when selecting chemical 
protective gloves for use of 1-bromopropane. 
Self-Certification 
EPA requests comment on establishing a self-certification requirement, and/or reporting 
requirements, for purchasing and continued use of 1-BP or products containing 1-BP for certain 
conditions of use.  

The proposed self-certification requirement is a clear example of what safety scholars have 
termed “safety clutter”. (Rae, A. J., et al. (2018). Policy and Practice in Health and Safety, 16(2), 
194–211.) The signing of a self-certification statement will not improve the functioning of existing 
engineering controls or PPE. It’s even less clear what added safety value will be created by 
sending an additional copy of a self-certification to a vendor for each individual purchase of 1-
bromopropane. It will, however, increase the amount of paperwork required in order to purchase 
and use 1-bromopropane. At academic operators of laboratories, this will contribute to the 
problem of ‘administrative bloat’ (Weinstein Jr., P., Progressive Policy Institute, August 2023). 
This will be particularly acute for smaller academic operators of laboratories which do not currently 
employ a Certified Industrial Hygienist. If this provision were adopted in future rulemakings, as 
well, operators of laboratories would routinely find themselves having to submit multiple self-
certification statements attesting to the presence of the same controls in the same laboratories 
with each chemical purchase. 
EPA also seeks comment on whether, in future rulemakings, it should require reporting to EPA of 
the type of records specified in 40 CFR 751.815. 
A typical academic organic chemistry laboratory can keep between 2000 and 5000 unique 
chemicals on hand while biomedical laboratories often have between 200 and 500. It is entirely 
foreseeable, therefore, that a single laboratory could use 1-bromopropane, 1,1-dichloroethane, 
1,1,2-trichloroethane, 
1,2-dichloroethane, 
1,2-dichloroethylene, 
1,2-dichloropropane, 
1,3-
butadiene, 1,4-dioxane, multiple phthalates, acetaldehyde, acrylonitrile, aniline, carbon 
tetrachloride, ethylene dibromide, formaldehyde, methylene chloride, N-methylpyrrolidone, o-
dichlorobenzene, octamethylcyclotetrasiloxane, p-dichlorobenzene, perchloroethylene, tris(2-
chloroethyl) phosphate, and vinyl chloride. A requirement to submit records maintained pursuant 
to future TSCA rulemakings would rapidly become a requirement, in practice, to track and report 
each hazardous chemical used by each laboratory user. It will be economically burdensome for 
operators of academic laboratories to compile and report those records into a well-designed 
system. However, given that EPA’s current approach is to issue separate rulemakings for 
individual chemicals, it seems more likely that separate reports would have to be submitted for 
each regulated chemical, dramatically increasing the burden. EPA would also have to account for 
the fact that a record identifying the name and class period of a student taking a laboratory course 
likely qualifies as an educational record under FERPA, which strictly regulates disclosure of such 
information. 
EPA should not implement self-certification requirements, nor should EPA require 
duplicative submissions of such statements to chemical vendors. EPA should not require 
routine reporting of records of the type specified in 40 CFR 751.815 in this or in future 
rulemakings. 
Recordkeeping Requirements 
EPA proposes to require documentation of worker information that far exceeds that necessary to 
identify individual workers; specifically, “the name, workplace address, work shift, job 
classification, and work area of each person.” Academic operators of laboratories in particular will 
struggle to collect this information. Persons who may handle 1-bromopropane in university 
laboratories may be volunteers, visiting scholars, undergraduate students enrolled in laboratory 
courses, undergraduate students working as paid laboratory assistants, graduate students 
enrolled in laboratory courses, graduate students working as teaching assistants, graduate 

students working as paid laboratory researchers, graduate students receiving extramural 
fellowships, postdoctoral scholars, staff researchers, adjunct faculty, or tenured or tenure-track 
faculty. Each of those would represent a different job classification. Many researchers switch 
between classifications each academic period based on considerations that do not meaningfully 
impact the likelihood of exposure to 1-bromopropane (e.g. a graduate student who had been 
working as a paid researcher receiving an extramural fellowship). A single researcher may even 
switch between classifications within the same day (e.g. a graduate student who both works as a 
teaching assistant and who performs research towards their dissertation). Further complicating 
the issue of job classification within university laboratories is that universities commonly maintain 
information regarding students and employees within separate databases. 
Given the obstacles to satisfying the recordkeeping requirements including individual 
assignments, and demographic data contained the proposed rule, it could act as a de facto 
prohibition on the use of 1-bromopropane in academic laboratories. 
EPA should replace the current proposed recordkeeping requirements with language 
identical to OSHA’s Laboratory Standard and PPE Standard, except as necessary to 
account for the applicability of the proposed rule to non-employees. 
The proposed rule for 1-bromopropane is poorly tailored to laboratory use and creates an 
excessive economic burden. I urge the EPA to align its requirements more closely with OSHA’s 
Laboratory Standard and develop a common approach to chemical regulation that applies to all 
laboratory chemicals. This will ensure effective protection without imposing unnecessary burdens 
on laboratories. 
Thank you for your consideration. 
 
Sincerely, 
 
Christopher M. Kolodziej, Ph.D.

TURI Comments on 1-Bromopropane (1-
BP) Rulemaking Under TSCA Section 6(a) 
 
Docket Number - EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
September 23, 2024 
 
 
The Massachusetts Toxics Use Reduction Institute (TURI) offers the following comments in 
response to the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA) draft risk evaluation for 1-
Bromopropane (1-BP). These comments are provided in the context of TURI’s experience 
implementing the Massachusetts Toxics Use Reduction Act, Massachusetts General Laws chapter 
21I (TURA)1.   
 
Aspects of EPA’s draft risk evaluation are not consistent with the TURA program’s experience on 
protecting human and environmental health with respect to the use of 1-BP. In the draft risk 
evaluation, the potential harm of air emissions of 1-BP could be further addressed; certain 
assumptions are made regarding the effectiveness use of personal protective equipment (PPE); and 
the potential of well- developed safer alternatives to 1-BP could be better accounted for.  
 
TURI encourages EPA to consider these factors as it finalizes this risk management rule to ensure 
human health and the environment are protected from the adverse effects associated with 1-BP 
exposure. We believe further consideration of these factors would better promote the adoption of 
safer and more sustainable alternatives, which are currently available and accessible.  
 
1 MASSACHUSETTS TOXICS USE REDUCTION ACT - 
https://malegislature.gov/Laws/GeneralLaws/PartI/TitleII/Chapter21I 

Docket – EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
TURI O9icial Comments 1-BP

Background – The Massachusetts Toxics Use Reduction 
Act (TURA)   
 
TURA has unique reporting requirements that result in the collection of data, which other states 
and the federal government do not have. TURA requires large-quantity chemical users in the state 
to report annually on their use of certain toxic chemicals, pay an annual fee, and conduct toxics 
use reduction (TUR) planning every two years. In the TUR planning process, businesses examine 
opportunities to reduce toxic chemical use by adopting safer processes or inputs.    
 
The TURA program works from a number of core principles and approaches.  These include the 
following.   
 
• Focus on use:  The TURA program focuses on identifying the reasons why chemicals are 
used and wastes are generated. The focus is on protecting human and environmental health 
by reducing or eliminating the use of toxics wherever possible.    
• Focus on hazard:  TURA focuses on reducing or eliminating toxic or otherwise hazardous 
chemicals. There is no requirement to prove that exposure will occur, or to calculate risk, 
in order to take action to eliminate or reduce a hazard.    
• Protection of workers, consumers, and the environment:  TURA’s mandate is not only to 
prevent ambient environmental exposures resulting from industrial emissions, but also to 
take worker and consumer exposures into account.   
• Avoiding risk shifting:  TURA is intended to avoid transferring the risk of harm among 
groups of people or among environmental media. 
 
Effective January 2016, 1-BP was designated as a Higher Hazard Substance under TURA; this 
designation lowered the reporting threshold to 1,000 lbs./year. The Higher Hazard Substance 
designation under TURA provides a way to draw businesses’ attention to the chemicals of greatest 
concern and to ensure that these chemicals are prioritized in toxics use reduction efforts. In 
adopting this designation, the TURA program took account of existing data and the conclusions 
of authoritative bodies on both acute and chronic adverse health effects of 1-BP, including 
neurotoxicity, hepatotoxicity, reproductive and developmental toxicity, and carcinogenicity.  
Massachusetts Data on 1-BP Use and Releases 
 
Data collected under TURA may be useful in understanding the potential impacts of 1-BP. 1-BP 
was added to the TURA list of Toxic or Hazardous Substances in 2009, requiring facilities to begin 
reporting their use and release in 2010. Regularly reportable substances on the TURA list have 
reporting thresholds of 25,000 pounds for those manufactured or processed, and 10,000 pounds 
for those “otherwise used,” similar to the Toxics Release Inventory (TRI).  
 

Docket – EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
TURI O9icial Comments 1-BP 
 
 
 
 
3 
Figure 1 and Table 1 show 1-BP use and release in Massachusetts from 2010 to 2022, as reported 
under TURA.2  In 2010, the first year of reporting, three Massachusetts facilities reported the use 
of 1-BP and substantial releases to the environment. For example, one facility used 14,500 lbs. of 
1-BP, and released over 11,000 lbs. of 1-BP to the environment. The high release-to-use ratio may 
help to illustrate how the absence of regulatory attention can affect industry’s approach to using a 
toxic chemical throughout its lifecycle; if it has not been flagged as a concern by regulatory 
agencies. For example, workers may be less cautious in their handling of the chemical, or process 
design to reduce use and exposure may not be pursued. After three years on the TURA list of Toxic 
substances, the proportion released to the environment had decreased, possibly due to greater 
awareness and changes in the approach of industry. 
 
The Higher Hazard Substance designation in 2016, with its lower reporting threshold to 1000 lbs. 
per year, led to an increase in the number of facilities reporting use of 1-BP under TURA. In 2016, 
21 facilities reported use of the HHS 1-BP, up from just 2 facilities in 2015.  
 
Since its designation as an HHS, toxics use reduction efforts have helped to reduce the overall 
amount of 1-BP used in Massachusetts. As of 2022, there are 15 facilities using 1-BP in the State. 
Table 1 summarizes the 2022 TURA reporting data on 1-BP use by facility and sector. Metal 
coating and allied services, semiconductors and related devices and adhesives and sealants are the 
sectors responsible for the most use of 1-BP in MA as of 2022. The majority of use of 1-BP is 
reported to be generated as a “byproduct.” Under TURA, this means that the 1-BP is a non-product 
output from manufacturing processes, such as use in vapor degreasing and other industrial cleaning 
processes.  
 
The information available under TURA does not represent all uses of this chemical by 
Massachusetts businesses. There may be other 1-BP users who are not captured under the TURA 
reporting requirements due to lower quantities of use, a smaller number of full-time equivalent 
workers (FTEs), and/or the exclusion of certain industry sectors.

propyl bromide.

6 
Conditions of Use and EPA’s Proposed Workplace 
Chemical Protection Program (WCPP) 
 
Based on 35 years of experience collaborating with businesses and workers to reduce the use of 1-
BP and other halogenated solvents, TURI recommends a careful review of the WCCP exemptions. 
The recommendation is based on the following: 
 
• The existence of safer alternatives that are technically and economically feasible for 
implementation by users of 1-BP, whose adoption could be incentivized through 
stronger regulation;  
• TURI’s observations of worker exposure to 1-BP despite PPE requirements; and 
• The release of 1-BP near environmental justice communities.  
Availability of Alternatives 
TURI has decades of experiences that may be of relevance in EPA’s efforts on 1-BP and other toxic 
solvents. Much of this is catalogued in CleanerSolutions, TURI’s publicly available database 
containing 25 years of information and testing results on safer alternatives to solvent cleaning3. 
The database may be useful for EPA and companies to better understand the extensive availability 
of safer alternatives to 1-BP for use with different contaminants, substrates and equipment.  A few 
specific examples of the availability of alternatives are provided below. 
Alternatives in Manufacturing 
TURI notes the inclusion of industrial vapor degreasing as a condition of use which is not banned 
and subject to a workplace chemical protection program. Toxics Use Reduction has proven 
successful in eliminating the upstream use of 1-BP in vapor degreasing, among other conditions 
of use.  
 
In one case TURI supported an electronics component facility to switch away from 1-BP in their 
vapor degreasing unit4. Through eliminating 1-BP from its vapor degreasing process, the company 
achieved significant cost benefits and a safer environment for workers and fence line communities.  
 
Another company in MA used 1-BP in a vapor degreaser at their facility to clean oils, lubricants, 
and greases from copper and steel parts. The TURI lab identified an alternative cleaning method 
for the company that not only decreases the potential human health hazard but also saves time and 
money. 1-BP was replaced with an alkaline aqueous cleaner in an ultrasonics tank for heat pipe 
cell cleaning to replace its vapor degreasing process. The implementation of this new aqueous 
 
3 TURI CleanerSolutions Database. https://www.cleanersolutions.org/ 
4 TURI Case Study on Elimination of 1-BP Use During Vapor Degreasing. 
https://www.turi.org/publications/cd-aero-case-study/ 

Docket – EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
TURI O9icial Comments 1-BP 
 
 
 
 
7 
cleaner and equipment significantly improved workplace health and safety, reduced environmental 
impact, eliminated the need to report under TURA and reduced operating costs5.  
Alternative Products 
TURI carried out an extensive review of different product categories for available products which 
do not contain any of the TSCA five priority chemicals, including 1-BP. Spot cleaners, electronics 
degreasers and auto parts degreasers, were identified without 1-BP or other chemicals of concern. 
Some of these products may be used under the COUs permitted in the rule. The availability and 
cost of these safer and effective alternatives may warrant a review of the permitted applications of 
1-BP under the current rule.  
 
Under the current rule, insulation consumer products would not be banned. However, during 
TURI’s review, a cold pipe insulation product without 1-BP, or any of the other chemicals of 
concern, was identified. The water-based product provided satisfactory performance, while 
presenting reduced hazard properties and a price point on the lower end of the spectrum. TURI 
recommends carefully reviewing the insulation exemption, as banning this use could help drive 
the development and adoption of similar products. TURI would be happy to provide additional 
information from this review of alternatives.  
Assumptions regarding PPE and Engineering Controls 
TURI has extensive experience collaborating with workers who use 1-BP and other halogenated 
solvents in the context of the conditions of use (COUs) covered under the WCPP. TURI’s 
experience supports the view that PPE is not a reliable source of protection for workers handling 
1-BP.  
 
In TURI’s experience, PPE use can be inconsistent and/or ineffective. TURA program staff have 
learned from businesses and workers about the difficulties posed by PPE; equipment can be 
difficult to use and can compromise work effectiveness, and according to the pollution prevention 
hierarchy is a last-resort option for protecting workers. TURA program staff have made these 
observations in their own work within Massachusetts, and have also observed similar issues when 
visiting a technical assistance program in another state.   
 
TURI program staff have observed 1-BP users often do not use gloves when handling the chemical. 
Workers also lack information on the correct choice of glove. According to OSHA, “1-BP easily 
travels through most glove materials. Recommended glove materials for protection against 1-BP 
are supported polyvinyl alcohol or multiple-layer laminates.” However, TURA program staff have 
reviewed safety data sheets (SDSs) for 1-BP and have found that many SDSs do not specify the 
type of gloves that workers should use. 
 
Similarly, when visiting facilities that use 1-BP or other halogenated solvents, TURA program staff 
have observed that workers generally do not use respirators. Some Massachusetts facilities use 
open top, manual vapor degreasers, which is one of the conditions of use that EPA has recognized 
 
5 TURI Report on Alternatives to Halogenated Solvents Used in Surface 
Cleaning . https://www.turi.org/publications/alternatives-to-halogenated-solvents-used-in-surface-
cleaning/ 

Docket – EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
TURI O9icial Comments 1-BP 
 
 
 
 
8 
as a source of unreasonable risk. Workers manually place baskets into these open-top vapor 
degreasers, and based on TURA program staff observations of these workers, they may receive 
substantial dermal and respiratory exposures when leaning over the degreaser and breaking the 
vapor barrier. Again, TURA program staff have noticed workers not using respirators when 
conducting this process. 
 
TURI notes the emphasis placed on closed loop processing and automated systems (e.g. vacuum 
degreasers) as a solution to comply with the proposed WCCPs. In its extensive work with 
companies, TURI has found that there is still significant risk of exposure for workers who use 
these engineering controls, especially when chemicals are added to and disposed from the system. 
On a visit with a partner agency to facilities in another state, TURA program staff observed workers 
carrying solvents in open buckets within the facility, creating risks of respiratory exposures, 
splashes and spills.     
Environmental Justice Considerations 
Under the current 1-BP rule, fugitive air emissions of 1-BP from industrial sites would continue. 
This is evidenced by TURA reporting in 2022 in which all 1-BP releases (~29,000 lbs) were 
reported as fugitive air emissions. These facilities would likely be permitted to continue using and 
releasing 1-BP under the WCCP COU listed in the draft risk management rule.  
TURI would emphasize that the draft rule does not protect certain fence line communities from 
potential exposure. In many cases, preventing these releases would help achieve environmental 
justice goals. TURI has analyzed the use of Toxics in Massachusetts, including 1-BP, in the context 
of environmental justice. The map in Figure 2 shows the facility which reported the highest 1-BP 
releases in 2022, in relation to environmental justice communities. The potential of this rule to 
advance environmental justice nationwide is the rationale for TURI to suggest a careful 
examination of the permitted COUs, especially those which may result in emissions of 1-BP to the 

Docket – EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
TURI O9icial Comments 1-BP 
 
 
 
 
9 
environment. TURI would be happy to provide information on other facilities in Massachusetts 
that release 1-BP in proximity to environmental justice communities.

Re: 
U.S. Environmental Protection Agency Proposed Rule: 1-Bromopropane (1–BP); 
Regulation Under the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA); EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
 
Dear Ms. Masten: 
 
Dow appreciates the opportunity to provide comments on EPA’s proposed risk management 
rule for 1-BP. Although Dow does not manufacture nor use 1-BP, we welcome the opportunity to 
provide data that will help inform EPA’s prioritization, risk evaluation, and risk management. 
  
Laboratory Capabilities and Cost Estimates 
 
EPA has requested information on the availability of laboratory capacity needed to meet the 
proposed standard, and the costs associated with such testing. The following information is 
provided to help meet this request from EPA. 
 
First, to determine if a lab can meet the method sensitivity needs for a lowered exposure limit, a 
company needs to determine the limit of quantitation (LOQ or reporting limit) that the lab must 
meet. Standard industry practice for industrial hygiene (IH) sampling methods is to meet 10% of 
the exposure limit, not the exposure limit. For 1-BP, the Existing Chemical Exposure Limit 
(ECEL) is 0.05 ppm (0.25 mg/m3), and 10% of the ECEL would be 0.005 ppm (0.025 mg/m3). 
Following National Institute for Occupational Safety and Health (NIOSH) 1025, this would be a 
detection limit of 0.3 ug/sample for an 8-hour sample duration (Note, NIOSH 1025 has a 
maximum 12 L sample volume used in the calculation below). Each lab used would have to be 
able to quantify 1-BP at 0.3 ug to meet 10% of the ECEL. 
 
Calculating LOQ: 
(ECEL)(10%) = [(Needed ug LOQ)(24.45)]/[Maximum Sample Volume in L)(Molecular 
Weight as g/mol)] 
(0.05 ppm ECEL)(0.1)=[(Needed ug LOQ)(24.45)]/[(12 L)(122.99 g/mol)] 
Needed ug LOQ= [(12 L)(122.99 g/mol) (0.05 ppm ECEL)(0.1)]/(24.45) 
Needed ug LOQ= 0.3 ug 
 
 
 

 
 
Page 2 of 3 
®TM Trademark of The Dow Chemical Company (“Dow”) or an affiliated company of Dow 
 
 
General Business 
 
Two labs commonly used for IH sample analysis were contacted regarding their ability to meet 
the 1-BP ECEL and the 0.3 ug reporting limit (10% of the ECEL). None of the labs could 
currently meet this low limit. Current analysis uses a Gas Chromatograph paired with a Flame 
Ionization Detector (GC/FID). One of the labs indicated it may be able to meet the lower limit by 
conducting a method extension study. The estimated cost for the method extension was $500. 
This does not include costs for actual sample analysis. However, a simple method extension 
using existing GC/FID technology is unlikely to work. To detect the low level of 0.3 ug, a more 
sensitive Mass Spectrometer (MS) detector or Electron Capture detector (ECD) would likely be 
needed. Estimates for a method extension using GC/MS or GC/ECD technology ranged from 
$2,500 to $5,000.  This does not include cost for actual sample analysis.   
 
It is important to note that each lab that will analyze IH samples needs to conduct its own study 
to determine if it can meet the lower detection limit. That means the method extension cost of 1-
BP for each lab could range from $2,500 to $5,000. Each company would have to pay this cost 
for the lab it uses. For example, if 10 different IH labs were to analyze 1-BP samples, the total 
cost would be about $25,000 to $50,000. We are sharing this information because we believe it 
is important for EPA to understand that when an exposure limit is lowered, companies need to 
be able to detect 10% of the limit, and each lab used for analysis must modify its methods to 
detect at the new lower limit. It is an individual cost that each company must pay.  
 
It is also important to note that not all labs will be able to meet the new lower exposure limit. To 
determine lab capacity and capability, a company needs to contact each lab used for sample 
analysis to understand if it has the ability to meet the lower limit. It would benefit industry’s data 
collection efforts if EPA could contact some commonly used IH labs during the ECEL setting 
process to understand if the proposed ECEL limit is technically feasible and get a cost estimate 
on this change impact. To do this, EPA should consider working with the Wisconsin 
Occupational Health Lab (WOHL) Lab that analyzes IH samples collected for OSHA’s voluntary 
consultation program. 
  
Setting of a De Minimis Level 
 
Dow urges EPA to set a de minimis level for 1-BP to allow for low level impurities that do not 
contribute to unreasonable risk. Setting a de minimis not only helps EPA focus its risk 
management process on the uses and levels that pose risk, but it also helps industry. When 
there is a de minimis level, industry is able to test whether a product or entity would be 
regulated, and it targets resources in the areas where they are most needed. It is important to 
note that without a de minimis, analytical testing and quantification are impractical, as one 
cannot measure to zero with any analytical technique. 
 
In general, more clarity on how EPA sets de minimis levels is needed so this approach can be 
leveraged to other chemicals. This would be very valuable to both EPA and industry because it 
would help focus resources as EPA moves forward in its evaluation of prioritized chemicals. 
Dow would appreciate the opportunity to work with EPA to develop a framework for setting a de 
minimis for risk management chemicals.

General Business 
 
Thank you again for the opportunity to provide these comments. If you have any questions or 
would like to discuss further, please contact me by phone at 989.496.8393 or by email at 
m.lafore@dow.com.

Mike LaFore
