--- COMMENT ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471-0149 AND DOCKET ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 ---
AFL-CIO Comments on EPA’s Proposed Rule on 1-Bromopropane (1-BP) Regulation 
Under the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA) 
EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
September 23, 2024 
 
 
The AFL-CIO welcomes this opportunity to comment on EPA’s proposed risk 
management rule for 1-Bromopropane (1-BP). The AFL-CIO is the federation of 60 national and 
international labor unions representing 12.5 million working people across a wide variety of 
industries. Our unions represent workers in a broad range of industries and processes where they 
are exposed to 1-BP, including manufacturing, aircraft maintenance, disposal, cleaning, and an 
array of other industrial and federal government settings in which they regularly use and are 
exposed to products that contain 1-BP. The AFL-CIO and its affiliated unions therefore have a 
vital interest in the elimination of the unreasonable risks posed by workplace exposures to 1-BP 
and urge EPA to quickly finalize this important risk management rule. 
 
In most respects, we fully support EPA’s proposed rule. It is well-established that 
occupational exposure to 1-BP contributes to significantly increased risks of cancer and 
neurological disorders. We therefore fully support EPA’s determination that industrial uses of 1-
BP must be quickly eliminated in those conditions of use where safer alternatives are available 
and where EPA cannot be assured that implementation of workplace control measures can 
eliminate those risks. We fully support EPA’s proposal to condition the continued use of1-BP for 
certain conditions on compliance with an existing chemical exposure limit (ECEL) of 0.05 parts 
per million (ppm) as an 8-hour time-weighted average and implementation of worker chemical 
protection plans (WCPPs) that include control measures using the hierarchy of controls, 
exposure monitoring, worker training, recordkeeping and other necessary protections. We also 
applaud EPA in devising self-certification requirements as an additional check to ensure 
owner/operators seeking to use 1-BP will only be able to obtain the toxic substance if they certify 

2 
 
to the distributors, under penalty of perjury, that they are complying with the ECEL and worker 
chemical protection plans (WCPP). However, we would not support a self-certification as a 
replacement for the WCPP measures. 
 
As discussed below, we have three areas of concern, which we believe EPA must address 
before finalizing this rule: First, the proposal must provide workers and their representatives with 
the right to be actively involved in developing the workplace chemical protection plan and in 
observing workplace exposure monitoring, and to have ready access to monitoring records and 
self-certification records. Second, the prescriptive controls for dermal protection must be revised 
to require owner/operators to consider alternative control measures, consistent with the hierarchy 
of controls, before providing workers with appropriately selected/assigned protective gloves, and 
to self-certify in order to obtain 1-BP for their conditions of use. Third, EPA should adopt the 
primary regulatory alternative for vapor cleaning and degreasing, permitting these operations to 
be conducted only in closed-loop systems.  
I. 
Involving Workers and their Representatives in Developing, Implementing, and 
Monitoring the Worker Protection Plan 
 
a. Access to Records 
The proposed rule’s recordkeeping provision requires owners/operators to “document the 
notice to and ability of any potentially exposed person that may reasonably be affected by [1-BP] 
inhalation exposure to readily access the exposure control plans, facility exposure monitoring 
records, PPE program implementation or any other information relevant to [1-BP] in the 
workplace.” § 751.815(b)(3). Similarly, proposed section 751.807(c)(2)(iii) requires 
owners/operators to make “the exposure control plan and associated records available to 
potentially exposed persons.” These provisions must be expanded to explicitly require 
owners/operators to provide access to all of these records to the workers’ designated 

3 
 
representatives, and EPA should define “designated representative” to include unions and, for 
unrepresented workers, other representatives they designate to obtain these records on their 
behalf.  
Unions, as the recognized or certified collective bargaining agent, have the right under 
the National Labor Relations Act (NLRA) to obtain information about workplace exposures. 
They often have the expertise to analyze exposure information, are positioned to assist the 
workers they represent in understanding and utilizing the information available to them about 
workplace conditions, conduct training, and routinely bargain for improved working conditions. 
They therefore have an important role to play in ensuring the rule effectively protects potentially 
exposed workers.  
Where those workers are not represented, however, it is equally important that they have 
the opportunity to designate organizations or individuals to gain access to this information. Too 
often, hourly workers may not have the expertise to evaluate these exposure and compliance 
documents, and designated experts can assist them in understanding these issues.  Broadly 
defining “designated representative” would be consistent with the approach taken by both the 
Occupational Safety and Health Administration (OSHA) and the Mine Safety and Health 
Administration (MSHA).  
For example, OSHA’s “Access to Employee Medical and Access Records,”1 entitles 
workers and their “designated representatives” to medical and workplace exposure records. The 
rule defines “designated representative” as “any individual or organization to whom an employee 
gives written authorization to exercise a right of access.” The definition continues that, “for the 
purposes of access to employee exposure records and analyses using exposure or medical 
 
1 29 C.F.R. § 1910.1020. 

4 
 
records, a recognized or certified collective bargaining agent shall be treated automatically as a 
designated representative without regard to written employee authorization.” 2 OSHA has also 
recently codified a long-standing practice of not only allowing unions to participate in workplace 
inspections, but to also permit workers to designate any outside party to serve as their 
representative during inspections.3 MSHA similarly allows miners—whether or not represented 
by a union—to designate a representative of their choosing.4  
EPA should accordingly require owner/operators to grant worker representatives—either 
unions or, for unrepresented workers, other representatives they designate—access to the 
information owner/operators must record and maintain under both §§ 751.807(c)(2)(iii) and 
751.815(b)(3). Moreover, EPA should require owner/operators to make these records available to 
potentially exposed persons and their designated representatives promptly when requested, and 
the rule should make clear that relevant information cannot be redacted. 
b. Development of the Exposure Control Plan 
The proposed rule requires owners/operators continuing to use 1-BP to develop and 
implement detailed exposure control plans and, as discussed, to “make the [plan] and associated 
records available to potentially exposed persons.” § 751,807(c)(2)(i) and (iii). Owner/operators 
must not only be required to make these plans available to “potentially exposed persons;” they 
must be required to involve those workers and their designated representatives in the 
development of the plans. These workers and their representatives are critically important 
participants in devising any control strategies. They work directly with the products and know 
 
2 Id. § 1910.1020(c)(3). 
3 29 C.F.R. § 1903.8(c). 
4 The Mine Safety Act permits “a representative authorized by . . . miners” to participate in mine inspections. 30 
U.S.C. § 813(f). MSHA regards “[a] miners’ representative [as] any person, group or organization designated by two 
or more miners to represent their interests during health and safety enforcement processes at their mine.” MSHA, 
“Miners’ Representative Guide,” available at https://arlweb.msha.gov/minersrepguide/minersrepguide.asp; see also 
30 C.F.R. § 40.1(b). 

5 
 
the practicalities and vulnerabilities of various control strategies and other related work 
processes—especially under non-ideal conditions—and often are able to identify the most 
effective and practical methods to remedy them.  
 As discussed in the last section, where the workers in a particular workplace are 
organized, their “designated representative” is their union and where they are not organized, 
workers should either be permitted to designate a representative or the owner/operator can 
consult with the workers themselves. Without running afoul of the restrictions NLRA section 
8(a)(2) places on “employer dominated labor organizations,”5 owner/operators can meet with 
workers, poll them, or otherwise solicit their input on how best to devise a control plan. 
c. Exposure Monitoring 
While the proposed rule requires owner/operators to provide “potentially exposed 
persons” with the opportunity to observe exposure monitoring (§ 751.807(b)(2)(v)), EPA has 
again neglected the important role unions and other designated representatives play in this 
regard. OSHA standards routinely require employers to provide both workers and their 
designated representatives with the opportunity to observe monitoring, as an important check to 
ensure the monitoring is being conducted correctly and in a manner that is representative of 
employee exposure.6 
II. 
Prescriptive Controls 
EPA has proposed that for certain COUs where the Agency has determined that 
inhalation exposures do not pose an unreasonable risk of harm, but that dermal exposures do, 
owner/operators will simply be required to deploy a PPE program of providing and maintaining 
 
5 29 U.S.C. § 158(a)(3). See Electromation, 309 N.L.R.B. 990 (1992), enf’d, 35 F.2d 1148 (7th Cir. 1994); E.I du 
Pont, 311 N.L.R.B. 893 (1993). 
6 See, e.g., OSHA Methylene Chloride Standard, 29 C.F.R. § 1910.1052(d)(6). 

6 
 
protective gloves.7  While it may not be necessary, in those circumstances, to require 
owner/operators to conduct the airborne exposure monitoring required for COUs covered by the 
proposed WCPP, it remains important that owner/operators employ the hierarchy of controls to 
determine whether other control strategies could obviate the need to resort to or supplement with 
PPE. As EPA recognized in proposing to require owner/operators using NMP to implement the 
hierarchy of controls to reduce dermal contact with that toxic substance, addressing workplace 
exposures through the hierarchy of controls is considered best practice by the industrial hygiene 
community.8 Under this strategy, which prioritizes “exposure control strategies from most 
preferred to least preferred techniques,” PPE, like protective gloves, may be used only after the 
owner/operator determines that it cannot adequately control exposures by eliminating the hazard, 
substituting a less hazardous substance, or implementing engineering or administrative controls, 
in that order.9 In their plan, owners/operators must be required to justify their approach.  
We urge EPA to adopt a similar approach to dermal exposures to 1-BP.  For example, if 
an engineering control is to close the process and eliminate the possibility of skin contact for 
production workers, there may be no need to require gloves for these workers. Or there may be 
other controls that reduce but do not eliminate dermal exposure. If unreasonable risk remains 
after moving through the hierarchy of controls, dermal personal protective equipment (PPE) 
should be required. In developing an exposure control plan, owner/operators must include 
provisions for providing appropriate dermal PPE for maintenance workers who have to open 
 
7 89 Fed.Reg. at 65098-99; § 751.809. 
8 American Industrial Hygiene Association. “Hierarchy of Controls: White Paper.” AIHA-
assets.sfo2.digitaloceanspaces.com/AIHA/resources/White-Papers/HOC-White-Paper.pdf. V. 1. May 17, 2024.  
9 EPA, Proposed Rule, “n-Methylpyrrolidone (NMP); Regulation Under the Toxic Substances Control Act 
(TSCA),” 89 Fed.Reg. 51124, 51142 (June 14, 2024). 

7 
 
closed systems to work on them and emergency responders, in addition to any controls farther up 
the hierarchy. 
III. 
Vapor Degreasing 
For open-top and in-line batch vapor cleaning and degreasing, the AFL-CIO supports the 
primary regulatory alternative that would require facilities to use only closed-loop vapor 
degreasers for any batch vapor cleaning and degreasing; to use ventilation systems and 
engineering controls that achieve exposure concentrations of less than 0.5 ppm as an 8-hour 
time- weighted average; to isolate the vapor degreaser in a ‘‘regulated area’’ with controlled 
access to minimize exposures to 1–BP; and to require the use of respirators with APF of 10 for 
any worker in the regulated area of the vapor degreaser and the use of gloves made of supported 
polyvinyl alcohol or a multiple-layer laminated material. EPA has already identified a study 
showing air emissions can be reduced by 98% or more when a closed-loop degreaser is used 
instead of an open-top vapor degreaser.10 This engineering control is a common and effective 
approach to isolate the worker from this chemical exposure. Because EPA is required to set 
limits based on health, and the industry has an effective control measure readily available, the 
Agency should adopt this regulatory alternative. 
 
10 89 Fed.Reg. at 65093-94.

--- COMMENT ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471-0153 AND DOCKET ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 ---
Environmental Defense Fund 
 
Comments on 1-Bromopropane (1–BP); Regulation Under the Toxic Substances Control 
Act (TSCA); Proposed Rule 
 
Docket ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471; 89 Fed. Reg. 65066. August 8, 2024 
 
 
Submitted: September 23, 2024 
 
 
 
Introduction 
 
Environmental Defense Fund (EDF) appreciates the opportunity to provide comments to the 
Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) on 1-Bromopropane (1–BP); Regulation Under the 
Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA); Proposed Rule.1 
 
1. EPA should revise its flawed approach to estimating the unreasonable risks to frontline 
communities. 
EPA has calculated risks to fenceline communities from 1-bromopropane that are clearly 
unreasonable risks but discounts these unreasonable risks by misrepresenting the significance of 
its own estimates and the cancer benchmark used.  Further, the Agency fails to fully consider the 
risks faced by potentially exposed or susceptible subpopulations as required by TSCA. EPA has 
underestimated the actual exposures of 1-bromopropane to fenceline communities and does not 
fully consider risks for developmental toxicity.   
 
A. EPA misrepresents the cancer benchmarks in an attempt to trivialize the cancer 
risks estimated for potentially exposed or susceptible subpopulations.  
EPA’s characterization of the Clean Air Act benchmark is misleading at best.  EPA description is 
not consistent with its use of cancer benchmarks under the Clean Air Act. The Agency 
selectively mentions only one end of the cancer benchmark range considered in determining 
whether a risk is an acceptable risk under section 112(f).  The Agency considers a cancer risk of 
less than one in one million generally acceptable and a risk of greater than 1 in 10,000 generally 
unacceptable.  For risks within those bookends, EPA will make take in account several factors in 
 
1 1-Bromopropane (1-BP); Regulation Under the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA); Proposed Rule, 
89 Fed. Reg. 65,066, Aug. 8, 2024. (Proposed Rule). 
 

 
2 
 
determining whether a risk is unacceptable, including among others the distribution of risk 
within the population and the presence of sensitive subpopulations at various risk levels.2   
 
The following is taken from Exhibit 21 Summary Of Assumptions And Criteria For Evaluating 
Public Health Risks of EPA’s Residual Risk Report to Congress3

1 Screening assessment may be based on upper-end estimated HAP exposure at the location of either the

considered. 
2 Refined assessment based on more detailed and site-specific, and less conservative, estimated HAP

equivalent, reviewed in light of any additional credible and relevant information, are typically used. 
 
Referring to 1 in 10,000 as a presumptive benchmark4 while discussing multiple risks greater 
than 1 in 1,000,000 appears to be an attempt to trivialize the risks to fenceline communities and 
to falsely convey that EPA is being conservative in its estimation of risks. Nothing can be further 
from the truth.  As discussed below, EPA’s estimation of exposure and risks are not conservative 
(i.e., does not overestimate risks). 
 
 
2 EPA OAQPS, Residual Risk Report To Congress EPA-453/R-99-001 (March 1999). 
https://www.epa.gov/sites/default/files/2013-08/documents/risk_rep.pdf 
 
3 Ibid 
 
4 Proposed Rule at 65101. 

 
3 
 
Further, under TSCA it is a requirement not only a policy that EPA specifically consider the 
risks to potentially exposed or susceptible subpopulations. When assessing risks to fenceline 
communities who are often exposed to multiple chemicals, many of which cause cancer and 
often the same types of cancer, EPA’s TSCA program would be advised to consider how other 
EPA programs such as Superfund address risks to communities that are exposed to multiple 
carcinogens. “EPA believes that setting a 10-6 risk level for individual chemicals and pathways 
will generally lead to cumulative risks within the risk range (10-4 to 10-6) for the combinations of 
chemicals typically found at Superfund sites.”5   
  
EPA notes that the cancer benchmarks are not a bright line. While the cancer benchmarks may 
not be a bright line, this does not justify a dismissal of risks or allowing greater risks for 
fenceline communities under TSCA. Further, the TSCA requirement that EPA specifically 
consider the risks to potentially exposed or susceptible subpopulations and the recognition by 
other Agency programs that cancer benchmarks that allow a smaller degree of risk (generally a 
benchmark of 1 in 1,000,000) are more appropriate for sensitive or more highly exposed 
subpopulations would call for considering risks greater than 1 in 1,000,000 unreasonable risks. 
 
B. EPA should determine that the clear risks to fenceline communities it has identified 
are unreasonable risks. 
Despite calculating multiple risks using a methodology that systematically underestimates the 
risks from 1-bromopropane, the Agency does not identify unreasonable risks to fenceline 
communities.  Rather the Agency stunningly states that “EPA’s fenceline analysis for the air 
pathway for 1–bromopropane indicates that EPA is not able to conclude that there are no 
potential risks to fenceline communities.”6   This statement clearly conflicts with EPA’s own 
analysis.  EPA goes further and states that it “is not making a determination of unreasonable risk 
based on the fenceline screening analysis.”7  Clearly there are unreasonable risks and EPA does 
not have the discretion to pretend they do not exist. 
 
EPA identified risks greater than 1 in 1,000,000 for cancer resulting from releases from 105 
facilities at 100 meters and for 47 facilities at greater than 100 meters. These are risks from each 
of these facilities EPA considered them in isolation. EPA did not aggregate multiple exposure 
sources or settings within a given condition of use, such as combined exposures from multiple 
facilities. If EPA had done this even within conditions of use, both the number of facilities that 
contribute to risks greater than 1 in 1,000,000 for cancer would likely be larger as would the 
magnitude of the risks.  Further, considering the exposures to fenceline communities from 
multiple facilities may have resulted in greater risks for developmental toxicity. 
 
5 EPA. Soil Screening Guidance. https://semspub.epa.gov/work/HQ/175231.pdf. 
 
6 Proposed Rule at 65101. 
 
7 Proposed Rule at 65101. 

 
4 
 
As EDF has commented elsewhere on EPA’s Fenceline Approach used for estimating the 
exposures and risks of 1-bromopropane,8 EPA falsely equates the use of some conservative 
assumptions in its exposure and risks assessment with conservative assessments. By outright 
excluding exposures, considering exposures in isolation, and excluding peak or otherwise 
elevated exposures, EPA has developed a Fenceline Approach that systematically underestimates 
exposures and thus risks.9  
 
EPA did not aggregate exposure and risk across conditions of use. Real-world scenarios in which 
fenceline community residents may be subject to combined risks from two or more conditions of 
use are not considered. For example, EPA’s approach fails to account for the aggregate chemical 
exposure and risk faced by a person who lives both near an industrial facility and next door to a 
dry cleaner emitting a given chemical.  
 
Further, the fenceline approach does not rely on sufficiently robust data. For example, the use 
only of annual TRI release data will obfuscate release peaks, such as accidental releases, and 
facility start up and shut down events. These peak releases are particularly important for 
considering risks for developmental toxicity.  
 
EPA also tries to undermine the validity of the risks it estimated by stating that there “are some 
uncertainties associated with the fenceline analysis for the air pathway for 1–BP.”10  Yet, the 
uncertainties in the fenceline approach serve to underestimate the exposures as the SACC has 
noted, not overestimate them.11  
 
C. EPA should reconsider risks for developmental toxicity. 
Developmental toxicity is the most sensitive endpoint, yet EPA states that protecting for cancer 
will be protective for all endpoints. However, EPA clearly does not appropriately consider the 
scenarios that are appropriate for developmental toxicity. 
 
 
 
8 EPA. Draft TSCA Screening Level Approach for Assessing Ambient Air and Water Exposures to 
Fenceline Communities Version 1.0 at 62 (Jan. 2022), https://www.epa.gov/system/files/documents/2022-
01/draft-fenceline-report_sacc.pdf (“Fenceline Approach”). 
 
9 Comments of Environmental Defense Fund on Draft Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA) Screening 
Level Approach for Assessing Ambient Air and Water Exposures to Fenceline Communities 
https://www.regulations.gov/comment/EPA-HQ-OPPT-2021-0415-0085. 
 
10 Proposed Rule at 65102. 
 
11 Science Advisory Committee on Chemicals Meeting Minutes and Final Report No. 2022-01, 
https://www.regulations.gov/document/EPA-HQ-OPPT-2021-0415-0095 
 

 
5 
 
EPA underestimates exposures in what it terms the most conservative exposure scenario: “The 
fenceline analysis also evaluated the most ‘‘conservative exposure scenario’’ that consists of a 
facility that operates year-round (365 days per year, 24 hours per day, 7 days per week) in in a 
South Coastal meteorologic region and a rural topography setting.”12  
 
In the Fenceline Approach, EPA simply divided the annual TRI releases by the number of 
operating days to estimate a daily release.  
“The release scenarios consider two potential facility operating conditions. The first 
represents a facility that operates year-round (365 days per year), 24/7. The second 
represents a facility that operates generally on a Monday through Friday schedule (260 
days per year) for 8 hours per day, 5 days per week. The difference between the two 
release scenarios is the resulting total daily release, frequency of release, and duration of 
release. These conditions result in a different exposure pattern that is captured by 
modeling both release scenarios. As an example, if a facility has a total annual release of 
10,000 473 lb/year, then the daily release from a facility operating 365 days/year, 7 days 
per week, and 24 hours per day would be 27.4 lb per day for every day of the year over a 
24-hour period. If the facility operates 260 days per year, 5 days per week, for 8 hours per 
day, the daily release would be 38.5 lb per day, but only Monday through Friday and over 
an 8-hour period.”13  
 
This is not the most conservative estimate for developmental toxicity which is the most sensitive 
endpoint.  The more conservative estimate would be to consider the developmental toxicity 
window and to assume there are a fewer number of operating days.  EPA’s second scenario 
which assumes 260 days per year for 8 hours per day would be a more conservative assumption 
because it would result in a higher daily release number that would apply for the short-term 
exposure window.   
 
* * * 
The many ways in which the Fenceline Approach excludes or underestimates exposure 
demonstrate that the best available science, required by TSCA Section 26(h), is not being used to 
estimate risks to fenceline communities and other susceptible subpopulations.   
 
In addition to not using the best available science, EPA trivializes and ignores the serious risks 
faced by fenceline communities and pregnant women, both potentially exposed or susceptible 
subpopulations that require specific considerations under TSCA. 
 
12 Proposed Rule at 65102. 
 
13 Draft TSCA Screening Level Approach for Assessing Ambient Air and Water Exposures to Fenceline 
Communities Version 1.0 at 62 (Jan. 2022), https://www.epa.gov/system/files/documents/2022-01/draft-
fenceline-report_sacc.pdf (“Fenceline Approach”).

2. EPA should not dismiss unreasonable risks to fenceline communities by pointing to 
non-existent regulations and phantom benefits of the WCPP. 
In its discussion of risks to fenceline communities, EPA handwaves at non-existent regulations 
as a future means to address current exposures and risks.  As one example, EPA points to 
anticipated National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP) under the 
Clean Air Act.  
“As NESHAPs continue to be reviewed, and as the majority of facilities assessed, and 
those which indicate potential risk, in the fenceline analysis were vapor degreasing 
facilities, the NESHAPs process under the CAA will assess risk to the general population 
at the fenceline and regulate as necessary.”14   
 
There are, of course, multiple problems with this. First, there is no regulation in place.  EPA 
cannot determine how a non-existent regulation will address the risks faced by communities.  
Second, even if EPA were in the process of developing a proposed NESHAP and thus has a 
sense of what a draft proposed rule would look like, this is not necessarily equivalent to the final 
regulation. A draft proposed rule would likely be subject to Executive Order 12866 review and 
likely would be modified during that review before proposal.  The proposed rule would be 
subject to public comment, would be subsequently revised, and would also likely be subject to 
another Executive Order 12866 review and modification before finalization. Third, even if the 
NESHAP were to mitigate the risks so that they were no longer unreasonable, in the interim the 
fenceline communities would continue to be subject to the risks for an undetermined length of 
time.  This open-ended delay and uncertain action is for a chemical that, among other harms, 
causes cancer and developmental toxicity. 
 
EPA also points to its proposed WCPP requirements as something that “may reduce exposures to 
the general population for facilities identified in the fenceline analysis.”  (Apparently this refers 
to fenceline communities and the general population beyond.)  Yet, EPA does not explain how or 
to what degree it estimates that the WCPP will reduce exposure to the general population.  It is 
clear that EPA does not know how much of a reduction will result because elsewhere in the 
preamble the Agency requests comment on “on best practices to remove and reduce fugitive 
emissions of 1–BP from relevant operations including, but not limited to, chemical 
manufacturing, vapor degreasing, electronics degreasing, cold cleaning, and adhesives 
manufacturing.”  
 
Elsewhere in the preamble, EPA states that it “recognizes that owners and operators may have 
difficulty distinguishing between emission increases due to implementation of the WCPP and 
emissions increases resulting from other factors such as increased manufacturing, processing, or 
 
14 Proposed Rule at 65102. 
 

 
7 
 
use of 1– BP.”15 This clearly contradicts and undermines the Agency’s assertion that the WCPP 
requirements “may reduce exposures” to those living near these facilities.  
 
Thus, there is no factual basis for EPA to dismiss the unreasonable risks to fenceline 
communities based on the CAA NESHAP or the WCPP. 
 
3. EPA is required to address the unreasonable risks it has identified not develop a 
mechanism for notifying communities that their risks are increasing. 
 
EPA must use section 6(a) to mitigate the clearly unreasonable risks it has identified. Yet rather 
than address these unreasonable risks, EPA requests comment “on methods to inform fenceline 
communities of any increases of 1–BP emissions to ambient air.”16 
 
It is not clear what EPA expects the fenceline communities to do both now when they are 
experiencing unreasonable risks, and in the future when the releases and the magnitude of the 
risks they face increase.  Does EPA expect them to move?  Congress tasked EPA - not the 
communities - with addressing unreasonable risks presented by the manufacturing, processing, 
distribution in commerce, use and disposal.  EPA must address these unreasonable risks, not 
push the Agency’s responsibility onto fenceline communities.

EDF appreciates EPA’s consideration of these comments.

Re: 1-Bromopropane (1-BP) Proposed Rulemaking Under Section 6(a) of the Toxic 
Substances Control Act (TSCA); Proposed Rule (RIN 2070-AK73) 
  
Dear OPPT,  
  
Thank you for the opportunity to comment on the “1-Bromopropane (1-BP) Proposed 
Rulemaking Under Section 6(a) of the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA)” (herein 
after referred to as the “1-BP proposed rule”). MilliporeSigma is a leading supplier to the 
global Life Science Industry, providing solutions and services for research, development, and 
production of biotechnology and pharmaceutical drug therapies.  
  
MilliporeSigma supports the protection of consumers and workers from chemical risk and 
values the implementation of science-based exposure limits, such as the Workplace Chemical 
Protection Program (WCPP). We also believe that compliance and intended risk mitigation 
goals (as outlined in the 1-BP proposed rule) are best achieved with clear, logical, and well-
defined regulations. 
 
 
1) 
1-BP Use at MilliporeSigma  
  
MilliporeSigma’s use of 1-BP is critical to the research and innovation. EPA’s proposal permits 
MilliporeSigma’s continued uses of 1-BP as follows:   
  
• 
Industrial and commercial use in laboratory chemicals:  
o 
1-BP is used for manufacture of products used in laboratory settings, including 
research and development applications such as chemical synthesis, analytical 
standards, and antibodies.  
 
 
 

 
2 of 6 
The life science business of Merck KGaA, Darmstadt, Germany operates as MilliporeSigma in the U.S. and Canada. 
 
 
2) 
Establish De Minimis Concentration  
 
It is critical that the EPA establish a de minimis allowance for the presence of 1-BP. As EPA is 
aware, it is impossible to demonstrate experimentally the total absence of a substance. An 
entity can only measure the absence down to an experimental level of detection.  
 
As stated in the previous N-Methylpyrrolidone Proposed Rulemaking Under Section 6(a) of 
TSCA1, the EPA emphasized the significance of the OSHA Hazard Communication Standard 
(29 CFR 1910.1200), which sets a de minimis level of 0.1% for carcinogenic chemicals and 
1% for non-carcinogenic ones. Moreover, EPA asserted as a matter of risk management 
policy, the Agency believes that the widespread awareness by industrial and commercial 
workplaces of the de minimis levels in the OSHA Hazard Communication Standard would 
generally support successful implementation of the levels EPA identifies. 
 
On the international level, 1-BP is identified as a substance of very high concern by the 
European Chemicals Agency and that Article 33(1) of the REACH Regulation details that 
businesses are only required to disclose when their products contain substances of very high 
concern that exceed 0.1%. While 1-BP is suspected to be carcinogenic, this indicates a need 
for a de minimis level for 1-BP that would be 0.1% under the OSHA Hazard Communication 
Standard. 
 
Furthermore, if 1-BP is present in a mixture above 0.1%, its presence would be noted on a 
Safety Data Sheet and an importer, processor, or user would know that it would have to 
evaluate its workplace against the WCPP.  
 
MilliporeSigma strongly encourages the EPA to adopt 0.1% as the de minimis level for 1-BP 
and to maintain consistency in the establishment of de minimis levels for proposed 
rulemaking under Section 6(a) of TSCA, aligning with both national and international 
regulations.  
 
 
3) 
Self-Certification  
 
Under the EPA’s 1-BP proposed rule, MilliporeSigma would be defined as a distributor and 
would have additional responsibilities under the proposed rule as outlined below:  
 
“To ensure distributors are only selling 1-BP to owners or operators of facilities able to 
implement and comply with the workplace requirements of the WCPP, EPA is proposing 
to require owners or operators who self-certify to provide a copy of the facility's 
current self-certification statement to the distributor from whom 1-BP is being 
purchased, for every purchase of 1-BP. EPA is also proposing for the distributors to 
collect, maintain, and retain a copy of the self-certification statement. EPA is also 
proposing to require distributors to keep records, such as invoices, that indicate the 
name of the purchaser and facility, date of sale, and quantity of 1-BP purchased. 
Distributors of 1-BP for the uses described in this unit may only distribute to those 
 
1 EPA-OPPT-0744-0015 

 
3 of 6 
The life science business of Merck KGaA, Darmstadt, Germany operates as MilliporeSigma in the U.S. and Canada. 
 
 
facilities that provide the correct self-certification statement for purchasing. EPA 
realizes that some facilities may not engage in the 1-BP uses listed in this unit at the 
time this proposed rule is finalized. Owners or operators that may wish to purchase 1-
BP after the publication of the final rule would still be required to submit the self-
certification statement to the distributor from whom 1-BP was initially purchased in 
order to purchase 1-BP, certifying that the facility for which 1-BP is being purchased 
will implement and comply with the WCPP. EPA is also proposing that distributors 
review the self-certification statement to ensure it is appropriately completed to 
include the owner or operator's and the facility's information, as outlined in this unit. 
As proposed, distributors would have to have a completed and valid self-certification 
statement for each sale of 1-bromopropane for the uses subject to the self-certification 
requirements. EPA is proposing that the distributors and owners or operators maintain 
and retain the self-certification statement and related invoices in the most 
administratively convenient form (electronic or paper) and retain the statement and 
supporting documentation for five years....“ 
  
The self-certification obligation will require MilliporeSigma, as a distributor, to invest 
additional time, effort, and resources in collecting, maintaining, and retaining copies of the 
self-certification statement for applicable customers. This will add complexity to each 
transaction for the affected product and condition of use, placing significant burden overall for 
administrative systems for both laboratories purchasing 1-BP and MilliporeSigma. 
 
MilliporeSigma is concerned that the unique self-certification approach proposed in the 1-BP 
regulatory action has not been used in prior rulemaking under section 6(a) of TSCA for 
chemicals with similar hazards. We urge the EPA to ensure consistency in rulemaking for risk 
management requirements and reconsider this approach, taking into consideration the 
burden placed on the industry from the 1-BP proposed rule. 
 
 
4) 
Monitoring Timeframe   
 
The 1-BP proposed rule states that, “EPA is proposing to require each owner or operator to 
establish an initial baseline monitoring sample to determine the magnitude of exposure for all 
persons who may be exposed to 1-BP within 33 months for Federal agencies and Federal 
contractors acting for or on behalf of the Federal Government and within 6 months after the 
date of publication of the final rule in the Federal Register for non-Federal owners and 
operators...”  
 
EPA should adopt consistency among the proposed rulemaking under Section 6(a) of TSCA. 
As with the Methylene Chloride; Regulation Under TSCA2, the proposed 180-day compliance 
timeframe was extended as it would not provide a sufficient amount of time to identify similar 
exposure groups and facilitate effective initial exposure monitoring, and as a result, this 
raised concern regarding the successful implementation of the WCPP for regulated entities.  
In consideration of the challenges of initiating the WCPP, even for facilities with industrial 
 
2 Federal Register: Methylene Chloride; Regulation Under the Toxic Substances Control Act 
(TSCA) 

 
4 of 6 
The life science business of Merck KGaA, Darmstadt, Germany operates as MilliporeSigma in the U.S. and Canada. 
 
 
hygiene programs in place, and given the difference in the occupational exposure limits 
between the OSHA PEL and the EPA ECEL that may spur an increase in the need for 
monitoring or other exposure control assessment infrastructure, EPA determined that a longer 
compliance deadline of 12 months, as provided in the primary alternative regulatory action 
described in the proposal, would be an appropriate timeframe to conduct initial monitoring, 
which likely would require regulated entities to contract new services or realign current 
industrial hygiene professionals toward WCPP compliance.  
 
MilliporeSigma urges EPA to adopt a longer timeframe of 12 months for initial monitoring.  
 
  
5) 
Re-monitoring within 15 days when monitoring results indicate non-detect or 
air monitoring equipment malfunction. 
  
In the 1-BP proposed rule, EPA is proposing to require owners and operators to re-monitor 
within 15 working days after receipt of the results of any exposure monitoring when results 
indicate non-detect or air monitoring equipment malfunction, unless an Environmental 
Professional as defined at 40 CFR 312.10 or a Certified Industrial Hygienist reviews the 
monitoring results and determines re-monitoring is not necessary.  
  
When air monitoring equipment malfunctions, such situations are known prior to submitting 
the samples for laboratory analysis. The industrial hygiene professional would void these 
samples and not submit them to the analytical laboratory for analysis.  
  
Many of our locations utilize external industrial hygiene professionals to perform air 
monitoring. Re-monitoring within 15 days will be difficult to arrange with an external vendor. 
In addition, the process using 1-BP may not be done again within the 15 days. We would 
encourage EPA to use the 1-BP proposed periodic monitoring requirements as guidance to 
monitor within 3 months unless re-monitoring is determined not necessary. This will provide 
time for the monitoring to be scheduled and increase the potential the process using 1-BP will 
be performed. 
  
It is unclear why EPA is proposing the review of exposure monitoring results be done by 
Environmental Professional as defined at 40 CFR 312.10 or a Certified Industrial Hygienist. In 
reviewing 40 CFR 312.10, we do not see where it describes the skill set to evaluate air 
monitoring results for worker exposures. A CIH may not consent to reviewing air monitoring 
results when the monitoring was performed by a person outside of their direct supervision 
due to the CIH Code of Ethics. MilliporeSigma is concerned there will be a lack of available 
Environmental Professional or Certified Industrial Hygienists to conduct a review when air 
monitoring results indicate non-detect.  
  
There are highly qualified industrial hygiene professionals capable of assessing air monitoring 
results who do not hold the CIH certification. A non-detect can occur when the result is less 
than the analytical method limit of detection along with other scenarios associated with the 
sample collection. MilliporeSigma requests EPA consider an alternative approach such as 
requiring documentation be included with the air monitoring results that the monitoring 

 
5 of 6 
The life science business of Merck KGaA, Darmstadt, Germany operates as MilliporeSigma in the U.S. and Canada. 
 
 
method used could achieve results below the action level. Such actions are typically already 
done when planning air monitoring and, when done before sampling, could improve the 
sampling will obtain results below the 1-BP action level.

VII. EPA Request for Specific Comments 
 
• 
Item #15 - EPA is also requesting comment on the proposed timeframe to 
conduct additional exposure monitoring after the cleanup of the spill or repair 
of the leak, rupture or other breakdown, as outlined in Unit IV.A.2. 
 
In the event of start-up, shutdown, spills, leaks, ruptures or other breakdowns or 
unexpected releases that may lead to employee exposure, EPA is proposing that each 
owner or operator must conduct exposure monitoring of potentially exposed persons 
(using personal breathing zone sampling) within 30 days after the conclusion of the 
start-up or shutdown and/or the cleanup of the spill or repair of the leak, rupture or 
other breakdown. 
 
The term “breakdowns” should be defined as a breakdown or failure of an engineering 
control or processing equipment.   
 
If the repair of a breakdown or start-up of the engineering controls meets or exceeds 
the prior certification or equipment performance, resampling should not be required.  
If the repair or start-up does not meet or exceed the certification or equipment 
performance, sampling should occur within 60 days of the repair of the breakdown or 
the next scheduled use of 1-BP within the process.   
  
• 
Item #40 - EPA is requesting comment on the need for and associated costs 
of ambient air monitoring, including fenceline monitoring, at fenceline 
locations; or facility emissions source monitoring, as well as information on 
the frequency and nature of air monitoring EPA should consider including as 
requirements in the final rule (such as a detection limit for 1-BP). EPA is also 
requesting comment on methods to inform fenceline communities of any 
increases of 1-BP emissions to ambient air. 
 
The Agency understands that certain engineering controls can reduce exposures to 
people inside the workplace but may lead to increased ventilation of 1-BP outside of 
the workplace, thereby increasing risks to people in fenceline communities of adverse 
health effects from exposures to 1-BP in ambient air. Therefore, EPA is proposing to 
prohibit increased releases of 1-BP to outdoor air associated with the implementation 
of the WCPP. 
 
In terms of fenceline communities and ambient air emissions of 1-BP, hazardous air 
pollutant (HAP) levels are covered by the Clean Air Act (CAA) and should not be 
included in this proposed regulation.

Dear Ms. Masten: 
The Chemical Users Coalition (“CUC”) is providing the enclosed comments on 
EPA’s Proposed Regulation of 1-Bromopropane under TSCA, published in the Federal 
Register on August 8, 2024.  
 
CUC is an association of companies from diverse industries that are interested in 
chemical management policy from the perspective of those who use, rather than 
manufacture, chemical substances.1 CUC encourages the development of chemical-
regulatory policies that protect human health and the environment while simultaneously 
fostering the pursuit of technological innovation.  Aligning these goals is particularly 
important in the context of chemical management policy in a global economy.    
 
The CUC appreciates your consideration of these comments. If you have any 
questions relating to this submission, please feel free to contact me. 
 
 
 
1 The members of CUC are Airbus S.A.S., The Boeing Company, Carrier Corporation, HP Incorporated, 
IBM Company, Intel Corporation, Lockheed Martin Corporation, National Electrical Manufacturers 
Association, RTX Corporation, Sony Electronics Inc., and TDK U.S.A. Corporation. 

 
1 
 
Before the United States 
Environmental Protection Agency 
1-Bromopropane (1-BP); Regulation Under the Toxic Substances Control Act 
89 Fed. Reg. 65066 (August 8, 2024); Docket EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
 
 
Comments of the Chemical Users Coalition 
 
 
The Chemical Users Coalition (“CUC”) appreciates the opportunity to provide these comments. 
regarding the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s (“EPA’s” and “the Agency’s”) Proposed 
Rule for the Regulation of 1-Bromopropane (1-BP) under Section 6(a) of the Toxic 
Substances Control Act (“TSCA”) (the “Proposed Rule”). CUC is an association of companies. 
from diverse industries that typically acquire and use, rather than manufacture or import, 
chemical substances. Our members depend on the availability of certain existing substances for 
which there are not technically feasible substitutes as well as a reliable pipeline for innovative 
new chemistries to be able to thrive in a competitive, global economy. Consequently, us 
members encourage EPA to develop regulatory approaches that encourage innovation and permit 
sustainability. Thus, CUC supports measures that protect health and the environment in a manner 
that enables the regulated community to pursue technological innovation simultaneously with 
economic development in the United States. This is critical in the area of chemical regulatory 
policy, which necessarily addresses emerging information about health and environmental risk. 
 
Background 
 
CUC has been an active participant in the TSCA regulatory process since the formation of the 
organization prior to the TSCA amendments in 2016 and in the Agency’s various efforts to 
implement the amendments. This has included submitting comments on EPA’s revisions to the 1-BP risk 
determination, reflecting certain policy changes surrounding TSCA risk evaluations which EPA 
announced in 2021. EPA’s final revised TSCA risk determination for 1-BP in December 2022 reflected 
these policy changes, including some with which CUC disagreed. EPA determined that 1-BP presents an 
unreasonable risk of injury to health due to the significant adverse health effects associated with exposure 
to 1-BP, including neurotoxicity, developmental toxicity from acute and chronic inhalation exposures, and 
cancer from chronic inhalation exposure.  
 
Overview of CUC Comments 
In the Proposed Rule, by allowing for use of 1-BP with controls, EPA recognizes that solvents are 
essential in many industries. As EPA has prohibited the use of other solvents, continued use of 1-BP is 
critical. CUC supports this approach. EPA is clearly focusing significantly on workplace exposures. 
However, EPA does not appear to be fully consulting with industrial hygiene experts or recognizing 
certain best practices and standards followed in the discipline of industrial hygiene. In addition, the 
exercise of EPA authority in regulating workplace practices presents significant challenges, both for the 
EPA and for the regulated community. Before EPA finalizes a risk management rule for 1-BP, it should 
provide more information to stakeholders and the public about the planned framework for the Agency’s 
compliance and enforcement efforts, and the Agency should consider whether revisions to the Proposed 

 
2 
 
Rule are needed to facilitate more effective implementation and compliance in light of the planned 
framework. Furthermore, CUC is concerned that some of the proposed measures may cause 
manufacturers to move away from 1-BP production, which will create significant problems for those 
industries that need 1-BP products. Lastly, EPA needs to be more cognizant of real-world 1-BP use 
scenarios and ensure that the phase-in of compliance mandates does not have disruptive or premature 
impacts on uses that the Proposed Rule allows to continue, including critical and essential uses and other 
environmentally beneficial uses. 
 
Industrial Use of 1-BP 
As noted by the EPA in the preamble to the Proposed Rule, 1-BP is used as a solvent in cleaning and 
degreasing operations, including vapor degreasing, cold cleaning and aerosol degreasing. EPA is allowing 
the use of 1-BP, with strict workplace controls, for these conditions of use. CUC is supportive of this 
approach. EPA has already taken action to restrict the use of a number of solvents used in these 
applications. Ensuring that industry has access to solvents needed for safe and continuous use of 
equipment and processes is extremely important. Consequently, providing for the continued permitted use 
of 1-BP is critical, and CUC appreciates that EPA has recognized such.  
 
The Workplace Chemical Protection Program 
EPA, throughout the TSCA Section 6 risk management rules proposed to date, has demonstrated a clear 
preference for requiring a WCPP to address occupational exposure concerns. CUC believes that EPA must 
build greater flexibility into the requirements under a WCPP. For example, the Proposed Rule requires 
that baseline monitoring be conducted within six months of the date of the final rule, and that under 
certain circumstances, periodic monitoring must be conducted. However, there are some conditions of use 
for which the potential for workplace exposure may occur only annually (or even less frequently), for 
example in certain batch manufacturing operations, or in a completely closed system. In certain enclosed 
systems scenarios, potential human exposures might occur only during periodic (perhaps only annual) 
maintenance. The initial monitoring requirements, and the WCPP requirements generally, do not 
differentiate between different types of workplaces and operations – they are all presumed to be the same 
and pose the same risk. EPA should make changes to the rule before issuing it in final form to permit 
flexibility in the WCPP requirements to accommodate the practical realities of a variety of manufacturing 
operations and practices.  
For example, EPA is proposing to require recurring five-year periodic exposure monitoring. In the 
alternative, CUC suggests that EPA create a new option for circumstances where 1-BP is present in the 
product/process, but the initial monitoring demonstrates that 1-BP was not detected in a statistically 
significant level. In such circumstances, further periodic monitoring would not be required as long as 
there has been no change to the process/controls. At minimum, there should be a mechanism to allow for 
less frequent monitoring. If a process has not changed and the WCPP is in place and working, continued 
monitoring will not change the (absence of) exposure. 
Another operational aspect of the WCPP that CUC believes needs to be addressed concerns the 
identification of the responsible entity for WCPP implementation and compliance. The term 
“owner/operator” for the entity responsible for implementation of the WCPP at a facility is much more 
broadly defined than terms that are currently used for standards overseen by the Occupational Safety and 

 
3 
 
Health Administration (“OSHA”). This language change means that “owners/operators” – not employers 
– are now responsible for providing respiratory protection and other personal protective equipment to 
more than their direct employees. For a facility that may have many contractors present, the new 
compliance mandate could create enormous complexities. This approach, unless significant changes are 
implemented, will be confusing and impractical, and will create tensions between EPA rules and OSHA 
rules and lead to confusion and potential compliance issues. CUC therefore recommends that EPA adopt 
the approach of requiring “employers” to be the entities responsible for providing respiratory protection 
and other PPE to their employees. The owner/operator could be the entity responsible for ensuring 
compliance with WCPP. For example, the owner/operator could be responsible for ensuring that training 
of those working in its facilities is completed; however, the employer would be the entity responsible for 
providing the training. This language change would be consistent with current OSHA practice and ensure 
protections are in place while not being overly burdensome. 
CUC Members generally support EPA’s objective as stated in the Proposed Regulation that facilities 
should implement the WCPP “in accordance with the hierarchy of controls” and to use “pollution 
prevention to control exposures whenever practicable.” Nevertheless, CUC Members do not agree that the 
terms of the 1-BP regulation should mandate that companies implementing the WCPP requirements 
should need to create records or otherwise document that they have “evaluate[d] elimination, substitution, 
engineering controls or administrative exposure controls strategies and the reason why they found these 
strategies infeasible to control exposures below the [existing chemical exposure limit (ECEL)].” While 
industrial hygiene professionals work with their colleagues to implement the hierarchy of controls when 
determining the most appropriate methods to mitigate workplace exposures to chemical substances, CUC 
considers it to be unnecessary for EPA (and presumably its enforcement staff) to require a business to 
create records reflecting how such determinations were made. This is especially concerning to CUC 
Members because in many cases where 1-BP has been in use for decades or more, such analyses would 
have been made years ago and newly required records may need to be created (or recreated). This 
requirement is unworkable and should be abandoned before the rule is finalized. 
Additionally, EPA is requesting comment on whether owners/operators should be required to attest to 
whether and why the exposure controls they have selected would not result in increased air releases of 1-
BP to the atmosphere from the workplace and keep records of that statement as part of the WCPP 
exposure control plan. CUC believes that such attestation is unnecessary and should not be required. This 
is another burdensome documentation requirement that complicates compliance. Rather than attesting, 
this information should be documented through the results of the sampling done when the process 
changes.  
EPA also has requested comments concerning an owner’s/operator’s ability to conduct initial monitoring 
within six months after date of publication of the final rule, and anticipated timelines for any procedural 
adjustments needed to comply with the requirements outlined in this unit, including establishment of a 
respiratory protection program and development of an exposure control plan. CUC believes that EPA 
must lengthen these compliance timeframes in order to allow for ease of achieving compliance with the 
rule. A large corporation with many different types of processes will need time to work through how the 
new requirements impact company operations and processes and determine the application of the new 
requirements to the various products and processes that use 1-BP. 
A general concern of CUC members is the lack of consistency of WCPP requirements from one risk 
management rule to the next. EPA needs to create a consistent framework for each rule that allows for a 
chemical’s continued use with a WCPP. The required programs will be easier to comply with when each 
chemical’s risk management rule follows a consistent program outline with a “generalized" WCPP having 

 
4 
 
the capability of being applicable to many substances and minimizing differences for occupational health 
requirements between regulated substances. CUC acknowledges that there may be circumstances of use 
of a specific chemical that warrant additional measures under a WCPP. However, the general framework 
for the WCPP should be the same from one chemical to the next, and that is not the case. EPA should 
address this issue for 1-BP and all other similar TSCA section 6 rules that it will be issuing.  
CUC members have general concerns about the approach taken to occupational exposures. Threshold 
Limit Values (“TLVs”) developed by the American Conference of Governmental Industrial Hygienists 
(“ACGIH”) are accepted as the standard for workplace exposures throughout industry. The options that 
EPA has discussed for WCPP compliance (respirator use and specific dermal protection) are not 
necessarily the industry accepted practices. EPA’s deviation from using best industrial hygiene practices 
and standards will lead to confusion and difficulties in risk communication. Employees who are 
accustomed to working with the understanding that ACGIH TLVs are the best practice may question this 
as EPA issues values that deviate drastically for specific substances. Additionally, having two agencies 
with concurrent jurisdiction over the exact same setting, albeit with differing standards, will only lead to 
confusion. To date, EPA has not disclosed how EPA and/or OSHA will both support and/or enforce 
workplace safety measures, and whether EPA even has the requisite expertise to do so.  
Compliance Timeframe Impacts on Supply Chains for Continuing Uses  
EPA should consider longer compliance timeframes for the specific prohibitions on manufacturing, 
processing, and distributing of 1-BP to avoid disruptions in supply chains for continuing uses, including 
critical and essential and other beneficial uses. The proposed timeframes are relatively short and likely not 
to be practicable at the supply chain level. As a practical matter, it can be anticipated that the Proposed 
Rule, if finalized with its compliance timeframes, will cause certain producers of 1-BP to leave the 
market, potentially cutting off supply for continuing critical uses. This is likely to have unforeseen 
economic impacts which EPA has not accounted for in its regulatory analyses and may increase the 
possibility of “regrettable substitution” in response.  
EPA should include longer phaseout periods for the prohibitions or consider other strategies to ensure that 
the supply remains available for critical, essential, and other continuing uses of 1-BP.  
De Minimis Levels, By-products, Impurities, and Articles 
EPA did not include a de minims level on concentration for 1-BP in the Proposed Rule, although it 
requested comments on whether one should be included. CUC supports inclusion of a de minimis level of 
concentrations less than 0.1% by weight. EPA should clarify that the presence of 1-BP as an impurity or 
by-product in a substance would not subject that substance or mixture to the proposed ban. In the same 
way EPA has excluded impurities and by-products from other regulations under TSCA (such as for 
purposes of Chemical Data Reporting), EPA should explicitly exclude trace amounts of 1-BP as an 
impurity or by-product from the prohibitions. CUC therefore supports an exclusion for 1-BP that is not 
intentionally added.  
EPA requested comments as to whether the presence of 1-BP in articles should be expressly from the 
restrictions. CUC believes that EPA should explicitly exclude 1-BP from the restrictions when present in 
manufactured articles. There are substantial constraints on the ability of entities who import articles that 
may contain 1-BP to determine whether such articles do in fact contain 1-BP. In light of the fact that no 
risk from exposure to 1-BP in articles has been identified, the final rule should explicitly state that articles 
containing 1-BP are exempt from restrictions. 

 
5 
 
Additionally, to avoid confusion, the final rule should define “article,” and define the term in the same 
way it has defined article in the contexts of other regulatory requirements under TSCA (e.g. the final rule 
for methylene chloride). 
CUC also recommends that EPA explicitly exclude from the restrictions 1-BP when used for research and 
development purposes. The Proposed Rule does not address this condition of use. EPA, by establishing 
compliance timeframes, is providing time for users of 1-BP to explore alternative substances to determine 
their technical feasibility in specific conditions of use. Such research and development efforts will likely 
require comparative tests and studies that will, by necessity, require use of 1-BP. To promote such efforts 
and to avoid any confusion in the regulated community, EPA should explicitly state both in the final rule 
and in the preamble thereto that use of 1-BP in research and development efforts is fully exempt from the 
restrictions. 
Self-Certification 
EPA has proposed that most owners and/or operators who implement a WCPP to self-certify as to their 
implementation and compliance with the WCPP, including a point-of-sale self-certification to purchase 
and then use 1-BP. This requirement is overly burdensome and adds little, if anything at all, to ensuring 
compliance other than adding an additional layer of bureaucracy. If a facility is required to implement a 
WCPP, that obligation exists irrespective of an obligation to self-certify. If a facility, either inadvertently 
or intentionally, fails to comply with WCPP requirements, the self-certification will not address that. 
Mistakes are mistakes, and willful violations are willful violations. The self-certification requirements 
simply overburden those entities who are already complying with the substantive WCPP requirements by 
adding a paperwork burden, which serves to disincentivize the manufacture of 1-BP. The requirement also 
creates logististical implementation challenges, due the number of different actors involved in 
manufacturing, sales and distribution of product. CUC strongly recommends that this provision be 
removed.  
 
Federal Contractors 
CUC believes that EPA needs to better understand how entities operate in the aerospace and defense 
sectors, particularly under government contracts. The Proposed Rule contains provisions that allow for 
extended compliance timeframes for government entities and contractors. However, in many instances, 
the work that a contractor does for non-federal work is performed using the same equipment and at the 
same location where federal contract work is being performed. Such facilities can’t simply meet specific 
WCPP compliance mandates for specific non-federal contract tasks. The facility as a whole would need to 
meet federal contract requirements and standards, irrespective of the specific work being performed at 
any given point in time. Accordingly, EPA should clarify that the extended compliance timeframes apply 
to government contractors broadly, not simply when they are engaged in government contract specific 
tasks.    
Conclusion  
CUC supports EPA’s efforts to appropriately manage risks presented by high priority substances. CUC 
appreciates the effort that EPA is expending to more appropriately tailor risk management requirements to 
specific conditions of use. However, CUC believes that EPA has more work to do to ensure that the 
Proposed Rule is consistent with well accepted industrial hygiene principles and requirements under the 
jurisdiction of OSHA. Accordingly, CUC asks that EPA take a closer look at real-world and generally 

 
6 
 
accepted industrial hygiene practices, existing requirements under OHSA, and adjust requirements in the 
Proposed Rule accordingly.  
CUC Members would be glad to make themselves available to discuss any questions EPA personnel may 
have concerning CUC’s comments and/or to discuss any issues related to the Agency efforts to evaluate 
and to mitigate risks associated with the use of high priority substances.
