Submitted via Federal eRulemaking Portal 
 
 
RE: 
 1-Bromopropane (1-BP) proposed regulation  
 
 
 Docket: EPA-HQ OPPT-2020-0471 
 
 
 89 Fed. Reg. 65066 (August 8, 2024) 
 
 
 
Enviro Tech International, Inc. (Enviro Tech) respectfully submits comments on 
EPA’s proposed rulemaking to address alleged unreasonable risk of injury to human 
health presented by 1-bromoprane (1-BP; Chemical Abstracts Service Registry Number® 
[CAS RN®] 106-94-5). Enviro Tech is a top supplier of stabilized n-propyl bromide and 
fluorinated solvents for industrial parts cleaning applications. Founded in 1994, Enviro 
Tech has grown into the industry-leading provider of these solvents.  Enviro Tech’s 
comments, here, address only proposed workplace exposure limits. 
 
The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA)’ s proposed rule would ban all 
consumer uses of 1-BP, except in insulation, and some industrial and commercial uses.  
Although the proposed rule does not – outright -- ban all commercial/industrial use of 1-
BP (e.g., vapor and aerosol degreasing, electronics, and electronic and metal products), 
the proposed 0.05 ppm existing chemical exposure limit (ECEL) is unattainable in existing 
and newly manufactured open top style vapor degreasers, constituting a blanket 
prohibition masquerading as mere regulation. In short, non-federal workplaces could not 
implement a Workplace Chemical Protection Program (WCPP) ensuring compliance with 
the 0.05 ppm eight-hour, time-weighted average (8-hr TWA) ECEL. The proposed rule 
operates as a ban on use of 1-BP. 
 
EPA lacks authority under Section 6(b) to regulate indoor workplace exposures 
 
 
EPA lacks authority to regulate solely indoor exposures to 1-BP.  Section 6(b) of 
the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA), as amended by the Lautenberg Act, directs 
EPA to regulate “occupational exposures”. However, nowhere does TSCA Section 6(b) 
confer authority to regulate “any” or “all” occupational exposures, including solely indoor 
exposures with no ‘environmental’ component. Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo, 
144 S.Ct. 2244 (2024), overturned the Chevron presumption that agencies acted within 
statutory authority. Loper directs courts to exercise independent judgment in determining 
whether agency action falls within statutory authority”. Loper, 144 S. Ct. at 2273. The 
agency has not identified clear bases for purporting to regulate occupational exposures 
that occur solely and exclusively indoors, with no threat or impact on the ‘environment’. 

Enviro Tech Comments to EPA Proposed Regulation at 89 Fed. Reg 65066 
Docket EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
September 23, 2024 
 
2 
 
 
 
Non-environmental workplace exposures are set by the U.S. Occupational Safety 
and Health Administration (OSHA).  Although EPA may perceive limitations on OSHA’s 
evaluation of workplace exposures, that perception does not confer upon EPA the 
authority to regulate solely, indoor workplace activities with no component of 
environmental impacts or threat. EPA exceeded its statutory mandate in attempting to 
establish a “one-size-fits-all” exposure limit by treating every “Potentially Exposed or 
Susceptible Subpopulation” as the same, when worker exposure is different and the 
toxicology implications are not the same.  EPA’s purported action exceeds the Lautenberg 
Act’s direction and is inconsistent with TSCA’s plain language. 
 
EPA can regulate occupational exposures by regulating outdoor exposures 
 
 
EPA possesses authority to protect “potentially exposed or susceptible 
subpopulations” (PESS)…at greater risk than the general population of adverse health 
effects from a chemical substance.”  PESS includes “infants, children, pregnant women, 
workers, or the elderly”. (15 USC 2602(12).) EPA can protect PESS “workers”, by 
establishing regulations to address actual and potential outdoor (“environmental”) 
exposures. For example only, consistent with the Lautenberg Act, EPA could regulate 1-
BP emissions which potentially implicate worker exposures in outdoor areas, such as a 
work yard, or which involve potential exposures to surface water, groundwater or 
impacted soils. Such regulations clearly fall within EPA’s authority to protect and preserve 
the “Environment”. But it exceeds EPA’s authority to regulate exposures which occur 
solely and exclusively indoors, with no threat or impact on the environment.   
 
Imposing a 0.05 ppm ECEL is a prohibition masquerading as a regulation 
 
 
EPA repeatedly stated it is not proposing to ban all conditions of use of 1-BP, 
specifically allowing 1-BP for specified industrial uses, including vapor degreasing with 
appropriate worker protections including a WCPP. (89 Fed. Reg. 65066, 65069, August 
8, 2024.) However, proposed section 40 CFR 751.807, which establishes the WCPP 
requirements, proposes a technically unattainable (8-hr TWA ECEL) of 0.05 ppm that 
cannot be met by existing “open top” vapor degreasers, which are the most common form 
of the machine. Included as Attachment A please find comments by Patrick Oliver, who 
has 33 years’ experience in the vapor degreasing industry, whose experience is relevant 
and instructive regarding impacts of the pending proposal. Mr. Oliver concludes it will be 
“impossible to meet the 1-BP operative exposure limit of 0.05 ppm.”  Because no entity 
can meet the 0.05 ppm standard with existing equipment, the proposal is a prohibition---
which EPA specifically denies intending. Below please find my separate comments 
explaining how this proposed “regulation” is actually a “prohibition” in real-life application. 
 
Since 1994, 1-bromopropane has been used successfully as a vapor degreasing 
solvent with physical properties making it ideal for vapor degreasing and other high 
precision cleaning needs. 1-BP does not deplete the ozone layer (SNAP approved, 2007); 
has a very low Global Warming Potential; is non-flammable pursuant to OSHA (OSHA 
2021) and DOT regulations and possesses a boiling point higher than many other vapor 
degreasing solvents which EPA may have identified as possible substitutes. 1BP solvents 

Enviro Tech Comments to EPA Proposed Regulation at 89 Fed. Reg 65066 
Docket EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
September 23, 2024 
 
3 
 
superbly clean contaminants from substrates and can be used on a wide variety of 
substrates without negative impacts. 
 
Enviro Tech is a specialist in precision cleaning, which includes vapor degreasing 
as well as smaller-use cleaning systems. Over our 27-year history, Enviro Tech has 
become the predominant formulator of US-based 1BP solvents marketing our 
trademarked EnSolv and producing products under private labels sold by other brand 
names. Our records show between 6 to 8 million pounds of neat 1BP used each year in 
the precision cleaning sector, with that number reduced year over year for the past three 
years. 
 
These comments do not apply to EPA’s proposed ban on consumer, dry cleaning 
and adhesives sector uses of 1BP products. Enviro Tech has not/does not sell in these 
categories and makes all efforts to prevent such uses. Our market knowledge and 
experience show that very little, if any, 1BP used in these sectors. The focus of these 
comments relates to vapor degreasing and other precision cleaning methods in industrial 
use. Our silence regarding the proposed ban on consumer dry cleaning and adhesives 
sector uses should not be interpreted as agreement or endorsement of the proposed rule 
in these areas. 
 
As relates to the vapor degreasing sector, the proposed rule imposes numerous 
conditions on 1BP use which constitute a de-facto ban. 
 
A manufacturer using vapor degreasing to clean products has five major concerns. 
1BP solvents meet the vast majority of users’ parameter demands for each of these 
concerns, including: 
 
1) 
Material cost; 
2) 
Effectiveness; 
3) 
Compatibility with the material to be cleaned; 
4) 
Stability of Supply; and, 
5) 
Health & Safety. 
 
The main industrial alternative to 1BP solvents is 1,2,-trans-dichloroethylene 
(TDCE; CAS RN: 156-60-5)/fluorinated compound mixtures, which may be effective in 
some uses. TDCE/fluorinated compound mixtures’ compatibility with substrates is similar 
to 1BP solvents, so effectiveness and compatibility are not at issue. 
 
Regulations have unintended consequences. For example, in 2021 EPA 
announced phase-out of hydrofluorochlorocarbon (HCFC) and HCFC-containing 
products.  Within a year and one half of the implementation, Solvay, the world’s only 
manufacturer of HCFC 365 ceased production, well before the statutory phase out was 
scheduled to end. Given the current proposal’s likelihood to quickly destroy 1BP usage, 
it is reasonable to expect 1BP raw material manufacturers and/or solvent formulators to 
discontinue production due to compliance costs and diminishing sales. The risk to 1BP 
users is the great and needless loss to those users who first choose investment in control 

Enviro Tech Comments to EPA Proposed Regulation at 89 Fed. Reg 65066 
Docket EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
September 23, 2024 
 
4 
 
technologies where the product may no longer available. Thus, the proposed rule 
negatively impacts supply stability regarding 1BP based solvents. 
 
Similarly, the proposed rule negatively impacts manufacturers’ cost concerns. A 
manufacturer wanting to continue using 1BP solvents is prohibited “except for uses 
continued under the WCPP, self-certification and/or prescriptive controls”  (Proposed 
Rule page 65081) -- all instituting a draconian workplace exposure level of a 0.05 ppm 8-
hr TWA ECEL. The WCPP would necessitate unnecessarily burdensome “monitoring, 
training, recordkeeping, and other requirements to help ensure that the threshold is not 
exceeded.” Proposed Rule p. 65082. 
 
Extra costs are imposed at each step. The WCPP itself will require legal and 
consultant assistance, as each user must establish an initial baseline monitoring sample 
to determine exposures. Additional monitoring is necessary within 30 days after each 
change in the workplace or work practices. If it could be met, meeting the 0.05 ppm 8-hr 
TWA ECEL will require, in most cases, new manufacturing equipment, new ventilation 
systems and other physical changes in the workplace in addition to additional training 
costs record keeping expenses. 
 
Compliance with the excessively stringent new standard will increase raw material 
costs and formulation expense, which will be passed on to the user and ultimately the 
consumer. Additionally, user costs increase with each step of compliance, from preparing 
a WCPP to the never-ending cost of continuous exposure monitoring. All of these 
additional – supposedly one time -- and continuous costs needlessly subvert the cost 
advantage of 1BP solvents over competing technologies and will also be passed on to 
consumers. 
 
 
Costs skyrocket again when compliance requires new equipment or physical plant 
changes like new/revised ventilation systems, which are merely a regulatorily imposed 
“gamble” on producers.  In 2023, Enviro Tech installed a new, mid-line sized “open top” 
NESHAP compliant vapor degreaser at a cost of over $90,000. Physical plant 
modifications added $35,000 to the bill. This new equipment was designed by the most 
knowledgeable professionals in vapor degreasing design and manufacture – but it cannot 
meet the proposed 8-hr TWA ECEL of 0.05 ppm, even with use of personal protective 
equipment (PPE). 
 
Enviro Tech calculates the addition of WCPP, self-certification, prescriptive 
controls, continuous monitoring, training, record keeping, etc. to the cost of the degreaser 
project would add one-time costs of over $70,000 and continuous costs of over $10,000 
per year without factoring in a likely rise in the cost of 1BP solvent. Adding lip exhaust 
machinery and extra ventilation to reduce emissions to approach 0.05 ppm were 
calculated at over $120,000 for material and installation - without guarantees the ECEL 
would or could be met by the manufacturer. In all, compliance with proposed regulation 
results in a $325,000 “gamble” with no guarantee of successfully meeting the ECEL, 
along with a continuing annual cost of at least $10,000 per year, if the degreaser were to 
use 1BP solvents. 
 

Enviro Tech Comments to EPA Proposed Regulation at 89 Fed. Reg 65066 
Docket EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
September 23, 2024 
 
5 
 
So, Enviro Tech chose to use its TDCE/fluorinated product instead of our proven 
1BP technology in the new vapor degreaser. The obvious question is, if experts in vapor 
degreasing machinery doubt the ECEL can be met and the leading experts in 1BP solvent 
chemistry conclude that the cost of compliance with the proposed rule is too great to use 
a proven 1BP product, why would any other user decide otherwise? A simple and highly 
rational answer is: they won’t. 
 
It is an easy business decision to avoid speculative and unnecessary huge capital 
outlays (by switching solvent) to extricate one’s business from a never ending regulatory 
morass by switching solvents. That is what happened throughout the history of solvent 
regulation under the SNAP program. Users of Freon, 1,1,2-trichloroethane (TCA; CAS 
RN: 79-00-5) and other banned solvents switched to hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs), HFEs, 
trichloroethylene (TCE; CAS RN: 79-01-6) and 1BP instead of opting for new or different 
cleaning systems. The same is already happening, with 1BP use declining in the current 
and last two years after three years of steady but non-increasing usage in prior years. 
One major vapor degreasing manufacturer reports that only 5% of new machines being 
currently ordered are intended to use 1BP solvents. 
 
Businesses minimize costs and employee time of non-producing activities; 
consequently, EPA’s proposed regulation will drive solvent change. However, alternate 
solvent choices are limited if the goal is to use existing equipment. The alternative choices 
available to today’s users are TDCE mixtures, which boil at a lower temperature and lack 
the same cleaning efficacy as 1BP solvents, sometimes causing production issues.  Other 
than TCE and PERC, there are no other non-flammable solvents readily available for use 
in existing vapor degreasers which match or exceed the cleaning efficacy of 1BP solvents. 
 
Vapor degreaser operators have and will change to mixtures of TDCE and various 
fluorinated compounds because doing so is the only real choice available for existing 
equipment (thus avoiding a huge capital equipment outlay). These solvents will be 
acquired at a higher cost (between $2,000 to $3,000 per drum) than 1BP solvents. Other 
solvent replacements for existing vapor degreasers are limited to pure fluorinated 
compounds which lack comparable cleaning efficacy and are priced at $4,000 to $10,000 
more than 1 BP solvents. 
 
The cost increase will continue over time, which results in overall inflation for 
consumers, but solvent substitution presents additional concerns beyond increased 
costs. 
 
HFEs are used in TDCE mixtures largely to negate the flash point of TDCE, thereby 
allowing the mixture to be “non-flammable” pursuant to OSHA and DOT regulations. 3M 
recently announced it will discontinue production of fluorinated compounds used in TDCE 
mixtures.  As a result there will be no U.S. manufacturers of products (mainly 
hydrofluoroether HFEs) used in TDCE mixtures. The only remaining source of fluorinated 
compounds for use in TDCE mixtures is China, a foreign entity of concern. (89 Fed. Reg. 
37079; May 6, 2024). TDCE products without HFEs are not useable in existing U.S. vapor 
degreasers. 
 

Enviro Tech Comments to EPA Proposed Regulation at 89 Fed. Reg 65066 
Docket EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
September 23, 2024 
 
6 
 
HFE products present multiple issues. First, HFE products used in TDCE mixtures 
have one fully fluorinated carbon, which makes them per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances 
(PFAS) under the definition used by the Organization for Economic Co-operation and 
Development (OECD).  In the U.S., all HFE’s normally used in TDCE mixtures are PFAS 
per the definition set forth in 40 CFR 704.3. Further, it is widely accepted in the market 
and in consulting circles that EPA will likely define PFAS in the same way as OECD.  
 
Secondly, nearly all HFEs used in TDCE mixtures have high or very high global 
warming potentials, which add additional concerns considering the recent phase out of 
HCFC compounds (which were also used in TDCE mixtures) due to global warming 
concerns. 
 
Also, both sides of the political spectrum favor additional tariffs on Chinese 
products, which raises costs.   
 
Additionally, there are genuine supply chain dangers from tensions between 
China, Taiwan, The Philippines and the U.S., in which case TDCE mixtures could be 
eliminated with no domestic replacement for the HFE compounds used in these mixtures. 
  
Currently, China is the largest U.S. supplier of TDCE. The sole U.S. producer of 
TDCE has been unable to keep-up with current demand.  Although there are reports that 
the U.S. supplier will be scaling production, there is no known time frame for this, which 
raises additional supply chain concerns. As discussed above, TDCE is unusable as a 
standalone substance in existing vapor degreasing equipment due to its flammability. So, 
even with a possible increasing amount of domestic supply of TDCE, the lack of HFE 
products manufactured in the US to create non-flammable mixtures raises serious 
concerns with supply stability of usable TDCE mixtures. 
 
Although HFE compounds are currently used in TDCE mixtures, there are new 
compounds, HFOs, which can also be added to TDCE to address flammability issues. 
Currently, neat HFO is usable for this purpose, costing from $4,000 to $9,000 per drum 
more than comparable HFE compounds. TDCE mixtures will likely remain from $2,000 to 
$4,000 per drum more expensive than comparable 1BP solvents, but this could rise to 
$6,000 per drum, if using HFO compounds. 
 
There is much trepidation in industry regarding EPA’s TSCA review of TDCE. 
Many are rightly concerned that EPA will treat TDCE the same way as perchloroethylene 
(PERC; CAS RN: 127-18-4), trichloroethylene (TCE; CAS RN: 79-01-6) and 1BP, leaving 
manufacturers with few options and unnecessarily increased costs. To reduce costs and 
regulatory constraints, we see large, multi-national companies off-shoring manufacturing 
to Mexico and Asia.  Regulating TDCE mixtures similarly to 1BP would add to increasing 
reasons to off-shore manufacturing. 
 
Cost is the driving force when users considering how to comply with the proposed 
rule, whether by attempting compliance with ECEL or by solvent substitution. As 
discussed above, substitution is the rational business response; but is problematic based 
compliance time-lines in the proposed rule.

Based on our 27 years of experience, the cost of completing similar tests for TDCE 
mixtures is in the range of $250,000 and could require two years – for each product. 
Further, the costs and time required are multiplied by each different mixture. A review of 
Enviro Tech’s records shows more than one hundred separate tests have been 
completed for Enviro Tech’s family of 1BP solvents. Enviro Tech estimates costs will 
approach $1,000,000 to duplicate the test results for an alternative full line of TDCE 
solvents, with repetitions for each new mixtures with compounds other than HFEs. 
 
Testing is necessary for each specific product variant, and for different 
manufacturers.  Consequently, even if competing manufacturers have similar products, 
both must test to ensure product quality. Thus, multiple manufacturers will incur 
substantial redundant and overlapping verification costs, which Enviro Tech does not 
believe was considered by EPA.  These costs will be passed on to users and then to 
consumers, all adding to inflation.  Also, testing labs may not have the capacity to 
complete the desired tests in a time frame necessary under the proposed rule. 
 

Enviro Tech Comments to EPA Proposed Regulation at 89 Fed. Reg 65066 
Docket EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
September 23, 2024 
 
10 
 
Although qualifying/verifying tests for 1BP were completed over multiple years, 
EPA’s proposed timeline for compliance would require alternatives testing be commenced 
and completed quickly, requiring needless planning, tracking, testing, record keeping, etc. 
during the transition period, all of which become irrelevant if and when positive results are 
produced for the intended substitute. 
 
Another major concern is the availability of new equipment, including airless 
degreasers and equipment to modify existing machinery. The Enviro Tech degreaser 
discussed above took over four months to build and deliver plus three weeks for 
installation, ventilation and space modifications. Although Enviro Tech does not anticipate 
a large increase in new machinery by 1BP users, if that were to happen, it would take 
many years for the number of machines and other equipment to be produced to meet that 
demand.  According to a manufacturer of airless degreasers, current production time is 
up to more than a year.  Therefore, it would be impossible for users to meet the timelines 
set forth in the proposed rule.  
 
 
 
    Alternative Substances for 1BP Have Comparable Hazard  
 
 
    Concerns or Unknown or Limited Hazard Information 
 
 
 
Available alternatives to 1BP, albeit with inferior performance, have comparable 
hazard concerns to 1BP or unknown/limited hazard information. EPA’s proposed rule 
would require entities to change from a chemical with well-known/well-characterized 
hazards to chemicals with comparable hazard concerns to 1BP or to chemicals with 
unknown or poorly characterized hazards. EPA has not considered the unintended 
consequences of its proposed rule, which will serve as a de facto ban, and will drive 
regrettable substitution. 
 
 
TSCA Section 6(c)(2)(C) states in part that:1 
 
 
[I]n deciding whether to prohibit or restrict in a manner that 
substantially prevents a specific condition of use of a chemical substance 
or mixture, and in setting an appropriate transition period for such action, 
the Administrator shall consider, to the extent practicable, whether 
technically and economically feasible alternatives that benefit health or 
the environment, compared to the use so proposed to be prohibited or 
restricted…(emphasis added). 
 
 
EPA’s assessment of alternatives does not meet this legal standard. As discussed 
previously, available alternatives are barely technically or economically feasible and 
inadequately tested for all replacement uses.  Based on EPA’s own analysis, the available 
alternatives do not benefit health or the environment. In fact, in terms of environment, 
alternatives may actually affect far more people negatively than 1BP solvents used in 
these industrial sectors. This has not been evaluated in the pending rulemaking. 
 
 
1 
TSCA § 6(c)(2)(C),15 U.S.C. § 2605(c)(2)(C). 

Enviro Tech Comments to EPA Proposed Regulation at 89 Fed. Reg 65066 
Docket EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
September 23, 2024

Enviro Tech evaluated EPA’s alternatives assessment for 1BP. We summarize this 
information below in Table 1, with EPA’s assigned scores of “High” or “Very High” or for 
hazards that EPA concluded information was not available or had inconclusive 
information.2 
 
 
Enviro Tech notes that the human health hazards EPA identified for TDCE in its 
2023 alternatives assessment do not reflect the scope of human health hazards EPA 
identified as potential concerns in its 2020 Final Scope of the Risk Evaluation for trans-
1,2-Dichloroethylene. In that document, EPA stated that it identified the following hazard 
concerns that may be considered for the risk evaluation:3 
 
Absorption, distribution, metabolism, and elimination] ADME, cancer, 
cardiovascular, 
developmental, 
endocrine, 
gastrointestinal, 
hepatic, 
musculoskeletal, neurological, nutritional and metabolic, ocular and 
sensory, renal, reproductive, respiratory and skin and connective tissue. 
 
 
EPA proposes to establish an alternative assessment approach to prohibit existing 
chemical substances without considering costs and other non-risk factors required under 
TSCA Section 6(b)(4)(A). This is especially problematic when the alternatives identified 
by EPA have similar or greater toxicity concerns or no/inconclusive hazard information to 
ensure that employers may properly implement protective measures in the workplace. 
Again, EPA is restricting 1BP such that it will prevent continued use of this substance by 
facilitating regrettable substitutions.

CONCLUSION 
 
In the proposed rule, EPA states its work is an effort to comply with Executive 
Order (EO) 13990 Protecting Public Health and the environment and Restoring Science 
to Tackle the Climate Crisis. As demonstrated above, the proposed rule will push the 
market to TDCE/fluorinated mixtures of comparable hazard or with limited/no hazard 
information, or substances that are actively under global regulatory restrictions. This 
includes PFAS compounds, including high global warming potentials for all. PFAS 
compounds must also be transported across the world to the U.S. (on diesel fuel burning 
ships) in order to replace a product that is not considered a global warmer and is <91% 
manufactured within the U.S.  Enviro Tech further notes that EO 13990 instructed federal 
agencies to “be guided by the best science”12 and to make decisions “following 
the science”. It is unclear why EPA has chosen to use an archaic and 
scientifically debunked method for deriving its ECEL (i.e., application of a 
linear low-dose extrapolation for cancer).  
 
This approach has been summarily rejected by leading carcinogenicity 
experts, including Dr. Bruce Ames, who stated the following about the  
incorrect assumptions and interpretations that led to this 
approach: 
  
 
A particularly relevant [method] is that the effects of the huge doses given 
to rodents were extrapolated down to low doses using a linear correlation, 
which is incorrect. Then the newly-calculated risk level was applied to the 
whole population, which resulted in an inordinate amount of concern about 
hypothetical risks with little evidence. 
 
Enviro Tech encourages EPA to revise its proposed rule as follows: 
 

Enviro Tech Comments to EPA Proposed Regulation at 89 Fed. Reg 65066 
Docket EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
September 23, 2024 
 
14 
 
● 
Require 1BP solvents to be used in degreasers that meet all current 
NESHAP specifications for open top vapor degreasers; 
 
● 
Require 1BP solvents users to use all current NESHAP work practices for 
open top vapor degreasers; 
 
● 
Require vapor degreaser exposure levels to be 6 ppm or less in an area 
adjacent to the vapor degreaser. As reported, this is the average exposure 
of vapor degreasers using 1BP solvents, which includes both old non-
NESHAP compliant degreasers, NESHAP complaint degreasers and an 
airless degreaser. By requiring NESHAP compliant degreasers, 6 ppm 
exposure level or less can be met in the area adjacent to the vapor 
degreaser without a gamble on a huge capital outlay. Further, emissions to 
outside air will not be increased and may possibly be reduced since a 
majority of vapor degreasers are currently operating at this level;  
 
● 
Require vapor degreaser operators to wear respirators with a factor of 50; 
 
● 
Require the use of proper gloves: Viton or Silvershield-like gloves where 
contact is reasonably foreseeable and nitrile gloves where only splash 
protection is required and 
 
● 
Modify the effective time line for implementation to at least two years to 
allow for adequate machinery to become available and for substitutes 
testing to be completed.  
 
Employing an 8-hr TWA ECEL of 6 ppm as the limit and the requirement for 
respirators with a 50 rating would bring anticipated workplace exposures for vapor 
degreaser operators to .12 ppm. This level can be met with the least amount of 
marketplace disruption, while ensuring worker protection and protecting the security of a 
U.S.-based supply chain. Doing so would allow ample time for users to evaluate their best 
course and allow time for manufacture of adequate machinery. Doing so would also 
prevent foreign entities from controlling the availability of alternative substances to 1BP  
and will ensure EPA’s compliance with legal requirement to consider costs and other non-
risk factors when regulating substances under TSCA Section 6.

Enviro Tech Comments on 1-BP proposed regulation (Docket EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471) 
Attachment A 
 
From: Patrick Oliver <poliver@baronblakeslee.net>  
Sent: Tuesday, September 10, 2024 3:04 PM 
To: Rich Morford <rmorford@ensolv.com>; Scott Aulinskis saulinskis@envirotechint.com 
 
Subject: Baron Blakeslee EPA nPB Ruling Comments 
 
Hello Gentlemen, per our meeting on September 4, 2024, here are my comments about the pending EPA 
regulations on nPB, which would establish an enforceable operator exposure limit of 0.05 PPM.  The 
following information consists of my opinions and observations having worked in this industry since 1991, 
and focuses on what I believe to be my best contribution, “process and equipment".  This information is 
presented in good faith and it is incumbent upon the user to determine fitness for use. 
 
• 
OPEN TOP VAPOR DEGREASERS:  In conventional open top vapor degreasers, it is our opinion it 
will be impossible to meet the proposed nPB operator exposure limit of 0.05 PPM.    
• 
ALTERNATIVE HALOGENATED SOLVENTS: In most cases there are solvent replacements for nPB, 
specifically fluorinated solvents blended with trans-1,2-dichloroethylene.  However the cost of these 
solvents may be 2X to 3X higher per pound as compared to nPB.  In the removal of tenacious soils, such 
as chlorinated paraffin drawing compounds used in the production of tubing (used in aerospace, defense 
and medical applications), these fluorinated solvents blended with trans-1,2-dichloroethyle…do not clean 
as well as nPB.  Incomplete cleaning in these applications is detrimental to the tubing because this type 
of component is generally annealed, and any organic residue left on surfaces will contaminate the tubing.  
Organic residue on tubing left after cleaning and will also contaminate the vacuum annealing or heat 
treating furnace which can be very costly and time consuming to remediate. 
 
• 
BI-SOLVENT / CO-SOLVENT CLEANING PROCESSES:  The Bi-Solvent process involves an initial dip 
into a tank of organic solvating agent followed by rinsing and drying in that vapor degreaser, generally 
using a pure fluorocarbon rinsing agent in the second (clean) sump.  The Co-Solvent process places the 
organic solvating agent in the boil sump of a two sump vapor degreaser, followed by rinsing and drying, 
generally using a pure fluorocarbon rinsing agent in the second (clean) sump.  These processes are 
generally used for electronics cleaning applications, and are not as well suited to heavy duty cleaning 
requirements in which nPB is typically used successfully.  The pure fluorocarbon rinsing agents are 
typically the most expensive solvents on the market 

Enviro Tech Comments to EPA Proposed Regulation at 89 Fed. Reg 65066 
Docket EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
September 23, 2024 
 
17 
 
• 
AQUEOUS:  One possible alternative may be Aqueous Cleaning as a substitute for nPB vapor 
degreasing.  However, there are many parts which can simply not tolerate being cleaned with water or 
some types of parts, if exposed to water, will never dry fully or properly.  In the electronics cleaning 
market, modern microelectronic packaging with lower component standoff heights, and finer pitch, are 
already beyond the limits of the ability of an aqueous process.  Aqueous cleaning agents may be 
formulated with surfactants, but the required water rinse is not effective because the high surface tension 
and steric properties of water prevent the water molecules from getting into those tight spaces and rinsing 
off the aqueous cleaning agent.  Compared to solvent vapor degreasing, aqueous cleaning processes have 
a higher process cost due to the much higher energy consumption involved in heating and drying water.  
Water has a much higher specific heat than halogenated hydrocarbon solvents like nPB and the energy 
requirement is thus far higher with an aqueous process compared to vapor degreasing.  In fact, a 
comparable aqueous process may use up to ten times the amount of electrical energy as compared to a 
vapor degreasing process of equivalent capacity and scale.  Cycle times are also higher in an aqueous 
process, given the number of process steps required (wash / rinse / rinse / dry for example).  Solvent also 
evaporates completely leaving no residue, and is more effective at the removal of organic, non-polar type 
soils, where as an aqueous process has the potential to leave water spots and corrosion on part surfaces.  
The potential for contamination redeposition or cross contamination of process stages is far higher in an 
aqueous process.  With the many individual process stages required in an aqueous process (wash / rinse 
/ rinse / dry for example) the floorspace requirement for aqueous cleaning equipment is much higher, 
which also increases process costs.  Solvents used in vapor degreasing are recyclable and have no finite 
lifespan.  Aqueous cleaning agents, by comparison, react with the substrate being cleaned, the soil being 
removed, and the oxygen in the air, all of which result in a finite lifespan for aqueous cleaning agents.  
Aqueous cleaning processes also use a lot of water, and consumables, which increase process cost and 
environmental impact. 
• 
MODIFIED ALCOHOL / HYDROCARBON: Modified Alcohol / Hydrocarbon chemistries are 
flammable or combustible and operate at a very high process temperature and parts generally emerge 
from the process at a temperature well beyond what operators can touch.  The high process temperature 
of emerging parts from a Modified Alcohol / Hydrocarbon process can create potential for part corrosion 
or staining.  As parts cool, airborne moisture & particulate can accumulate on part surfaces, potentially 
causing surface appearance defects & residue deposition.  Modified Alcohol / Hydrocarbon degreasing 
processes often require the use of a corrosion inhibitor.  Because of its physical properties, Modified 
Alcohol / Hydrocarbon results in very poor distillation rates.  Distillation in typical Modified Alcohol / 
Hydrocarbon degreaser designs is not continuous because of the significant amount of energy required 
to distill the solvents.  Inevitably, when oil & grease is removed from parts, some volatile fraction of those 
hydrocarbon-type soils accumulates in the distilled Modified Alcohol / Hydrocarbon.  As a result, those 
components of contamination end up in the distilled Modified Alcohol / Hydrocarbon, where they can 
deposit on part surfaces.  For this reason, these types of cleaning processes are never used in oxygen 
service cleaning for aerospace, defense or medical applications.  Residue is ultimately a symptom of poor 
distillation and chemistry issues that compromise the purity of that distillation process.  Modified Alcohol 
/ Hydrocarbon, cannot be used to remove solder flux or paste from printed circuit boards.  The Modified 
Alcohol / Hydrocarbon chemistry can form organic acids, especially in the presence of chlorinated or sulfur 
based metalworking fluids, which can cause extensive damage to equipment & parts.  Finally, Modified 
Alcohol / Hydrocarbon requires the use of vacuum vapor degreasing systems, substantially exceeding 

Enviro Tech Comments to EPA Proposed Regulation at 89 Fed. Reg 65066 
Docket EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
September 23, 2024 
 
18 
 
costs of conventional open top vapor degreasing systems that can use halogenated hydrocarbon solvents 
like nPB. 
 
FLAMMABLE SOLVENTS:  The type of equipment used with flammable solvents are generally 
Airless vacuum vapor degreasers, which are far above the capital cost of conventional open top vapor 
degreasing systems that can use halogenated hydrocarbon solvents like nPB.  Another additional cost in 
implementing flammable solvent  type of equipment is in the classification of the area in which the 
flammable solvent degreaser is installed.  Within a ten foot “bubble” around the perimeter of the 
equipment, no direct sources of ignition may be present and the room requires special components and 
construction methods for compliance with standards.  Fire suppression systems may also be required.  
The risk and cost associated with using flammable solvents is quite high.  Here are some comparisons 
between isopropyl alcohol (IPA) and nPB in regard to using either solvent in a typical industrial cleaning 
process:  IPA may NOT be used in conventional vapor degreasers not designed to accommodate 
flammable solvents.  An IPA system may require multiple IPA rinses to produce a completely residue free 
part.  This system may either be an Airless vacuum vapor degreaser, or in a conventional multi-stage dip 
console type cleaning system, could require an expensive drying system, which needs to be explosion 
proof, indirectly heated, exhausted, and may need nitrogen inerting or fire suppression.  IPA is flammable 
with a flashpoint of 53.1F.  All of the halogenated hydrocarbon vapor degreasing solvents generally used 
in vapor degreasers are non-flammable.  IPA is hygroscopic, drawing moisture from the air, which can 
result in water spots.  IPA has an evaporation rate of 1.5 (based on the generally accepted evaporation 
rate scale where n-butyl acetate = 1).  nPB has an evaporation rate of 4.7, so it’s considerably more 
evaporative than IPA, which translates to a faster drying process, more thorough solvent reclamation 
inside a vapor degreaser, and lower residues.  IPA waste is likely to be viewed as “hazardous” by waste 
haulers because it is “flammable”.  nPB waste is non-hazardous.  Generally it is not considered 
economically feasible to distill IPA for recycling.  On the other hand, nPB can be distilled and used 
indefinitely as long as acid acceptance is in control as specified by the solvent manufacturer.  The KB value 
of nPB is approximately 130.  It’s hard to find a consistent source for the KB value of IPA, but the highest 
I have heard is 50, but more commonly it’s in the 20s.  The higher the number, the more likely the solvent 
is to dissolve oils, greases, waxes, etc.  IPA has a relatively low soil loading capacity, compared to nPB, and 
will quickly become saturated, leading to soil redeposition, requiring more frequent disposal.  When 
properly structured and operated, there should be no residue of nPB left on part surfaces when the parts 
emerge from the degreaser.  nPB will generally produce a cleaner part, more consistently with lower 
residue than any IPA process. 
I hope this information is useful for our purposes.  If you have any questions, or would like me to 
expound on any of these points, kindly let me know.  I will assist in any way I can. 
  
Best Regards, 
Patrick Oliver

Re: 1-Bromopropane (1–BP); Regulation Under the Toxic Substances Control Act 
(TSCA) 
 
Dear Ms. Basten, 
 
On behalf of the Household & Commercial Products Association1 (HCPA) and its 
members, we want to convey our comments on 1-Bromopropane (1-BP); Regulation 
under the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA) (EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471). HCPA and 
its members have been involved throughout the risk evaluation of 1-BP and have a 
vested interest in ensuring that the ﬁnal regulation addresses the identiﬁed risk 
considerations but also is practical in implementation while ensuring that the risk 
management is appropriately managed and science-based.  
HCPA is unaware of existing consumer uses for 1-BP and reminds EPA that the 
SACC panel noted that many of the initially identiﬁed consumer products no longer 
appear to exist.2 We hypothesize that the limited number of consumer uses has been 
phased out since the original information was gathered. We encourage the EPA to 
update the ﬁnal rule to reﬂect the minimal consumer use better. HCPA also agrees that 
it is appropriate to prohibit consumer uses and supports the proposed prohibitions of 
consumer uses. 
HCPA shares the Agency’s concerns and uncertainty regarding the feasibility and 
cost estimates of implementing the Workplace Chemical Protection Program (WCPP).3 
 
1 HCPA is the premier trade association representing the interests of companies engaged in the 
manufacture, formulation, distribution and sale of more than $180 billion annually in the U.S. of familiar 
consumer products that help household and institutional customers create cleaner and healthier 
environments. HCPA member companies employ hundreds of thousands of people globally. HCPA 
represents products including disinfectants that kill germs in homes, hospitals and restaurants; air 
fresheners, room deodorizers, and candles that eliminate odors; pest management products for pets, 
home, lawn, and garden; cleaning products and polishes for use throughout the home and institutions; 
products used to protect and improve the performance and appearance of automobiles; aerosol products 
and a host of other products used every day. 
2 See page 34 of the SACC Report, https://www.regulations.gov/document/EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471-
0050 
3 See Section 7.12.2 of the 1-BP Economic Analysis, https://www.regulations.gov/document/EPA-HQ-
OPPT-2020-0471-0114 

1625 I Street NW, Suite 700, Washington, DC 20006 | 202-872-8110 | www.theHCPA.org 
 
 
 
 
2 
There is little doubt that the installation of equipment and controls necessary to 
implement a WCPP will be daunting for many companies, especially for small and 
medium-sized companies, and would represent a signiﬁcant business need. This is 
compounded by the ﬁnalized prohibitions to methylene chloride and proposed 
restrictions to perchloroethylene and trichloroethylene, and the absence of viable 
degreasing alternatives.4 HCPA strongly recommends that the EPA actively elicit 
targeted stakeholder feedback better to describe the cost estimates and potential WCPP-
driven prohibitions. 
HCPA is concerned that certain commercial and industrial uses are prohibited 
without an opportunity to demonstrate that the risks could be managed via a WCPP or 
other existing regulatory authorities. While the proposal notes the overlapping 
regulations, there needs to be a determination or analysis of the viability of using these 
existing and well-established authorities to address or complement the risks identiﬁed 
under TSCA authorities. HCPA recommends that the Agency perform this analysis 
before ﬁnalizing the rule. Further, HCPA recommends that the Agency provide an 
opportunity for companies to demonstrate compliance with existing authorities or 
WCPP for these uses if they can demonstrate addressing the risk. 
HCPA recommends increasing the regulatory implementation timelines by at least 
six months, as the current 3-month timeframe between stages (manufacture, processing, 
distribution, commercial use) is insufﬁcient. The additional time will better allow 
impacted industries to make the necessary supply chain changes, such as packaging 
and labeling, while allowing for the implementation of the proposal’s recordkeeping 
and downstream notiﬁcation requirements.  
HCPA strongly supports the Agency’s inclusion of a de minimis level of 1-BP and 
recommends a value consistent with OSHA’s Hazard Communication requirements of 
0.1% by weight.5 This will allow manufacturers and processors to perform appropriate 
due diligence associated with residuals of allowable uses, byproducts, or co-
manufacturing non-TSCA uses while addressing the risk considerations of 1-BP. 
Aligning these requirements would help the industry, especially downstream users, 
readily determine and ensure compliance with their obligations under TSCA while 
being consistent with the approach of de minimis thresholds with existing international 
regulations, like the European Chemicals Agency’s Article 33(1) of the REACH 
Regulation. 
HCPA recommends that the ﬁnal rule continue excluding articles from the rule as 
articles have not been shown to present an unreasonable risk. This exclusion could be 
revoked case-by-case when the EPA ﬁnds that articles signiﬁcantly contribute to 
unreasonable risk. We recommend that EPA follow an approach similar to that done 
with Signiﬁcant New Use Rules, using the standard deﬁnition of “article” for the broad 
 
4 See Section 7.12.3 of the 1-BP Economic Analysis, https://www.regulations.gov/document/EPA-HQ-
OPPT-2020-0471-0114 
5 See the recently ﬁnalized OSHA Hazard Communication Standard, page 44380 
https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2024-05-20/pdf/2024-08568.pdf  

1625 I Street NW, Suite 700, Washington, DC 20006 | 202-872-8110 | www.theHCPA.org 
 
 
 
 
3 
exclusion and speciﬁcally deﬁning articles in scope, where needed.6 
HCPA is concerned that the Agency has not adequately described the beneﬁts and 
costs associated with self-certiﬁcation, as described in §751.811. This includes the point-
of-sale requirements placed upon facilities and distributors and the associated 
compliance and recordkeeping burden of completing and managing the self-
certiﬁcations. While we appreciate that the scope of conditions of use is very limited, it 
is unclear what beneﬁt self-certiﬁcation would provide. The costs described in the 
Economic Analysis7 appear to underestimate the recordkeeping burden. There also 
appears to be an enforcement provision in §751.811(b)(2)(v) placed upon a distributor 
that may be difﬁcult to implement in practice. HCPA recommends revisiting this 
provision to describe the beneﬁts better and ensure the costs are appropriately 
captured. 
HCPA has concerns that the Energized Electronic Equipment Cleaner portion of 
the Alternatives Assessment that indicates Aqueous (i.e., with deionized water) or 
mechanical (e.g., carbon dioxide pellet or media blasting) methods may also be non-chemical 
alternatives to using products containing 1-BP for energized electrical equipment cleaning.8 The 
usage of an aqueous (deionized or otherwise) method would lead to an acute 
electrocution risk. It should also be noted that the referenced report, Safer Alternatives for 
the Textile Cleaning Industry: Alternative Spotting Agents and Evaluation of Improvements in 
Carbon Dioxide and Wet Cleaning Technologies,9 does not look at energized systems and 
may not be an appropriate comparison for this condition of use. HCPA strongly 
recommends the removal of this portion of the Alternatives Assessment. 
HCPA appreciates the allowance of Good Laboratory Practice Standards at 40 CFR 
part 792 or the use of a laboratory accredited by the AIHA or another industry-
recognized program, as required by proposed 751.807(b)(2)(i)(C). 
HCPA recommends that the agency incorporate well-deﬁned conditions of use into 
the ﬁnal rule. In our experience, particularly with deﬁnitions under EPA’s National 
Volatile Organic Compound Emission Standards for Consumer Products10 and the 
California Air Resources Board General Consumer Products Regulations11, having well-
deﬁned product categories has signiﬁcantly helped manufacturers and regulators 
appropriately describe and develop efﬁcacious products in the marketplace. Many
