FEDERAL eRULEMAKING PORTAL 
Re: 
Proposed Regulation of 1-Bromopropane (1-BP) under the Toxic Substances Control Act 
(TSCA), 89 Fed. Reg. 65066 (August 8, 2024) Docket (EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471) 
Dear Ms. Masten: 
Morgan, Lewis & Bockius LLP appreciates the opportunity to provide comments on the U.S. 
Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA or Agency) proposed regulation under the Toxic 
Substances Control Act (TSCA) for 1-Bromopropane (1-BP), also known as n-propyl bromide (nPB), 
on behalf of an industrial client.  
As a global leader in specialty metal tubing, our client has a longstanding commitment to ensuring 
the health and safety of its employees and the communities in which they live and work, and to 
protecting the environment. Our client has extensive experience in the safe and responsible use of 
1-BP in an industrial lubricant and as a vapor degreasing solvent in one of the largest open top 
vapor degreasing units in the world. In 2008, pursuant to guidance of EPA and the state 
environmental agency, our client’s facility completed a conversion from the use of trichloroethylene 
(TCE) in their industrial lubricant and as a vapor degreasing solvent to nPB. In 2016, when EPA 
announced the first 10 chemicals for regulation under the newly revised TSCA, our client began 
actively investigating available alternatives for nPB with the intent of eliminating nPB from its 
manufacturing process. Thus far, however, our client has not been able to find an alternative to 
nPB that can achieve the same functionality and cleaning capability necessary to meet the exacting 
customer-driven specifications for critical components in national defense programs, life-saving 
medical implant devices, nuclear power generation, space exploration, and commercial aerospace. 
Nearly all of the alternatives on the market today either pose health and safety and/or 
environmental concerns and are also being restricted themselves, or require significant process 
changes of varying degrees that will take time to identify, test, implement, and obtain customer 
approval for.  These comments provide important details about our client’s operations and its 
efforts to identify safe and effective substitutes, as well as the significant impacts the rule as 
currently proposed will have on our client’s operations, workforce, and customers, which our client 
urges EPA to consider as it finalizes this proposed rule.  
I. 
Executive Summary 
Our client manufactures high-precision small bore long-length metal tubing for highly critical 
applications for a range of government programs and private industries, as noted above. Over the 
past eight decades, our client’s tubes have enabled pioneering innovations that shape the future of 

 
FEDERAL eRULEMAKING PORTAL 
September 23, 2024 
Page 2 
technology. Whether it is exploring the depths of the ocean, operating within the human body, 
supporting our nation’s defense, or venturing into outer space, these small diameter metal tubes 
are engineered to ensure the performance of crucial applications. 
 
Given the nature of their end-uses, it is crucial that all contaminants be removed from the interior 
and exterior of the metal tubing at multiple stages in the production process, including the final 
pre-delivery stage. If contaminant removal is only partial, it could result in the catastrophic failure 
of critical components in the nuclear, aerospace, defense, and medical implants industries. 
Therefore, our client’s customers require that each piece of metal tubing pass microscopic 
inspection of its interior and external surfaces before the tubing leaves the facility. 
 
Based on its monitoring data, our client has determined that to meet EPA’s proposed Existing 
Chemical Exposure Limit (ECEL) of 0.05 ppm would require all workers, and occupational non-
users (ONUs) at the facility (approximately 200 individuals), to wear a continuous flow airline 
respirator in a work area that exceeds 100,000 square feet (sq. ft.). As explained in more detail 
below, given the nature of our client’s operations and the footprint of the impacted facility, these 
controls are impractical and would present significant safety risks to employees.  
  
While it believes that the proposed ECEL for 1-BP is impracticably low (particularly for 
manufacturing operations such as the ones it performs), our client is committed to taking the 
necessary steps to comply with EPA’s requirements, which, under the specific circumstances of our 
client’s facility, can only be done by eliminating the use of 1-BP in its operations. For the last eight-
plus years, our client has been researching suitable alternatives, many of which present alternative 
risks such as fire or explosion risk, while others present environmental or other concerns because 
they contain chlorinated solvents such as trans-1,2-dichloroethylene (trans DCE) and a 
fluoropolymer (PFAS) constituent or are otherwise in the process of being more heavily regulated 
by EPA (such as methylene chloride, TCE and PCE). Even where potentially suitable alternatives 
can be identified, they may not function as required to meet the exacting standards of our client’s 
customers.  For example, testing with aqueous alternatives has presented quality issues in our 
customer’s process that fail to meet customer quality standards―specifically, the exacting 
specifications dictated by the United States Department of Defense (DOD), the United States 
Department of Energy (DOE), and medical products manufacturers that ensure the integrity of 
mission-critical specialty parts. Based on our client’s experience in aggressively working to identify 
substitutes, a minimum of 60 months is needed to eliminate the use of 1-BP from its 
manufacturing processes and ensure that no person is exposed above the proposed ECEL.   
 
II. 
Discussion 
Our client solves worldwide technical and metallurgical challenges by manufacturing high 
performance tubes for super-critical applications. From aerospace to nuclear power stations, and 
renewable energy to medicine, our client’s small-diameter tubes operate in diverse industries and 
improve the safety and quality of life for millions worldwide. Without our client’s tubes, 
technological advancement in many industries would not be possible. To help EPA understand the 
criticality of nPB in our client’s process, we start with a brief overview of tube applications and the 
use of nPB in the manufacturing process.  
 

 
FEDERAL eRULEMAKING PORTAL 
September 23, 2024 
Page 3 
a. Tube Applications.  
 
Our client manufactures high-pressure tubes that perform in a range of critical defense, medical, 
aerospace and marine applications. For example, our client is a qualified supplier for both 
commercial and military aircrafts. The tubes that our client supply play a crucial role in the safe 
functioning and performance of aircraft engines. They operate within a variety of critical engine 
systems including fuel delivery, fire suppression tubes, drain lines, and bleed air systems. These 
tubes must meet stringent requirements for durability, corrosion resistance, and heat resistance to 
ensure optimal engine performance and safety. Our client also leads the manufacture of specialty 
alloy tubing for rocket engines and satellites that deliver the highest levels of performance for deep 
space exploration by delivering excellent durability that requires zero maintenance over decades of 
use.  
 
Our client is currently the sole supplier for three products supporting US defense applications, and 
the sole domestic supplier for additional US defense programs. Our client also produces nuclear 
tubes for components that are critical to the safe running of nuclear reactors. The tubes provide 
secure housing for control rods and nuclear fuel rods and enable precise control over reactor 
power output and reactivity. They are engineered to withstand the high temperatures, pressures, 
and radiation levels present in the reactor environment, and maintain the integrity of the reactor 
system. The uses of these tubes, like those for rocket engines and satellites, require that they be 
safe, effective and durable over decades of use with zero maintenance. The manufacturing process 
results in quality-assured tubing for nuclear fuel rods compliant with ASME Pressure Vessel Code 
NCA 3800 and ASME NQA-1 – audited and approved by all U.S. Commercial Nuclear customers. 
Our client’s nuclear fuel tubes help generate electricity that millions of people in the United States 
use daily.  
 
Our client also delivers medical tubes that meet the highest standards not only in strength-to-
weight ratios, but also in terms of microbiological corrosion resistance and fatigue life. These 
medical devices not only help improve people’s health, but also transform and save lives. For 
example, our client is a leading manufacturer of biocompatible tubes for Cardiac Rhythm 
Management (CRM) devices. CRM medical devices used for this treatment include pacemaker lead 
wires, defibrillators, and catheters. Tubes are crucial for the functioning of these implantable CRM 
medical devices to ensure a safe patient cardiac rhythm. Our client also manufactures implant-
grade tubing for both coronary and peripheral stents, working with customers to custom-make 
stent tubes that meet stringent quality, reliability, and performance requirements. Our client 
develops thin-wall tubing solutions that enable smaller and safer heart valves that last a lifetime.  
 
b. Critical Role of nPB in the Manufacturing Process.  
The tubing products described above are produced from more than 50 alloys, including stainless 
steels, nickel alloys, cobalt-chrome alloys, and reactive and refractory metals. Tubes are 
manufactured with outside diameters from 0.25 mm (0.010 in) up to 38.1 mm (1.5 in), in straight 
lengths over 44 feet long and coils up to 150 feet long. The tubes can be made with wall 
thicknesses from less than 0.05 mm (0.002 in) up to 3.8 mm (0.150 in). Profiles include round, 
rectangular, square, oval, or elliptical. The diameter, length, and profile needs are dictated by the 
customer’s applications, and must be produced in accordance with customer-furnished designs and 
specifications.  
 

 
FEDERAL eRULEMAKING PORTAL 
September 23, 2024 
Page 4 
Many of these alloys are problematic to cold draw due to lubrication issues and high work 
hardening rates, a situation not made easier by the very thin, uniform wall specifications. Precise 
control of wall thickness is an extremely critical aspect of our client’s manufacturing process. Tubes 
are fabricated via a series of cold-working processes.1 On average, each product requires 6 cold-
working cycles, with some requiring as many as 20 cold-working cycles. In most of these cycles, 
nPB is used as a carrier to deliver lubricant to the outer diameter (OD) and/or inner diameter (ID) 
of the tubes to protect those surfaces during the manufacturing process. This lubricant must be 
removed after each cold draw and prior to any subsequent heat-treating operation. Each cold-
working cycle also requires precision cleaning to remove any lubrication or other impurities (e.g., 
fines and debris) in a consistent and reliable fashion before the tube can go through further 
processing. After degreasing, tubes are heat-treated and prepared for the next cold-working cycle. 
The tubes are ultimately degreased using nPB multiple times during fabrication to ensure strict 
customer cleanliness and integrity specifications are met. 
 
Because of the extensive length of some of the tubes, movement through the cycles involves a 
highly choreographed operation. Many tubes are moved to the open-top degreaser through a work 
area exceeding 100,000 sq. ft. using large overhead cranes. Once inside the open-top vapor 
degreaser, tubes are moved by an automated crane system, and are dipped into the initial liquid, 
or “dirty side” of degreaser solvent sump, lifted and tilted so liquid inside the tubes drains back 
into dirty side sump. While tipping, tubes remain suspended in vapor phase for additional 
degreasing. Then tubes are transferred into clean side sump, where they are dipped into liquid 
solvent, raised into vapor phase again, and tilted, before being removed from the degreaser. 
Importantly, the basic manufacturing process and equipment is the same for all federal contract 
and commercial applications. The functions that nPB perform are critical to our client’s ability to 
meet the customer-specific specification (including DOD and DOE) as well as applicable 
engineering and regulatory standards.  
c. Exposure Points Emission Controls and PPE.  
 
Consistent with the objective of the proposed 1-BP regulation to reduce nPB exposures for workers 
and ONUs, ensuring worker safety is of the utmost importance to our client.  Our client has 
implemented a detailed Occupational Safety and Health Administration (OSHA) worker safety 
program that currently operates to reduce nPB exposures. As part of that program, our client has 
been actively working to reduce worker exposure to nPB using both engineering controls and 
personal protective equipment (PPE).  Engineering controls include the use of fume exhaust hoods, 
increased ventilation, and coolant to reduce volatilization. Required PPE includes the use of dermal 
protection (PVC or nitrile gloves) and respiratory protection (half-face air purifying respirators) 
when performing specific activities that use nPB. The use of PPE is clearly communicated to 
workers through safety training and is enforced at each workstation through signage and worker 
oversight. Respirator training is provided annually and includes employee medical fitness 
evaluations, fit testing, respirator types and selection, procedures for proper respirator use and 
care, written respiratory protection program compliant with OSHA standards, air contaminants 
overview, and exposure measurement and detection methods. In addition to the engineering 
controls and PPE, all new employees complete health and safety training that includes hazard 
 
1 “Cold-work” is actually performed at ambient room temperatures; the term is meant to 
distinguish it from “hot work” where materials are being heated. 

 
FEDERAL eRULEMAKING PORTAL 
September 23, 2024 
Page 5 
communications (how to identify presence or release, routes of exposure, protection from chemical 
hazards, and hazardous waste disposal), PPE use, life threatening programs (Energy 
Control/Lockout Tryout, cranes, forklift, confined space entry, molten metal, working at heights), 
Lockout/Tagout, emergency response procedures, ergonomics, and safe behaviors.  
 
d. This Facility Cannot Meet the Proposed Inhalation Exposure Limit of 0.05 ppm.  
 
EPA is requesting information on potential engineering, administrative, or prescriptive controls and 
how feasible it would be for entities to implement these strategies in their operations. By way of 
background, in 2008 our client converted from using TCE as its primary solvent for degreasing and 
in its lubrication process to using nPB. At the time, nPB was promoted by EPA as a safer and more 
environmentally friendly alternative to TCE for degreasing, although there was no (and there is still 
no) federal permissible exposure limit (PEL) set by OSHA or National Institute for Occupational 
Safety and Health (NIOSH) Recommended Exposure Limit (REL) for nPB.  In the absence of these 
values, our client has used the California OSHA PEL of 5 parts per million (ppm) as a workplace 
exposure standard. To ensure that workers and ONUs are not exposed to nPB above 5 ppm, the 
company has implemented several safety measures, as described in subsection c above, and 
conducts periodic personnel monitoring. Despite the current employee safety measures, our client 
does not achieve the proposed ECEL target of 0.05 ppm. Developing a Workplace Chemical 
Protection Program (WCPP) to meet the ECEL within the proposed 36-month compliance deadline 
for federal contractors would impose significant safety and operational challenges. 
 
Based on current nPB monitoring, to meet the ECEL within the proposed compliance timelines, our 
client would most likely need to implement the use of a NIOSH-approved supplied-air respirator 
(also referred to as airline respirator) for all workers and ONUs who are present on or near the 
manufacturing floor.2 The use of such equipment would create significant safety concerns and 
material handling challenges for maneuvering the long, thin tubes manufactured by our client, 
including tubes over 44 feet in length. Most tubes are moved throughout the manufacturing area 
using large overhead cranes, which would present serious risk of entanglement with any 
continuous flow airline respirators. Additionally, although the company does not believe that use of 
powered air purifying respirator (PAPR) systems would be effective for extended use, as would be 
required at this facility, the use of a PAPR for every worker and ONU on site can also create 
additional physical hazards from entanglement of the hoses in the site equipment and materials, as 
well as obstruction of the user’s vision by the hood. In other words, the physical layout of the 
facility and the location of degreasing and equipment cleaning activities―both of which are 
necessitated by the physical requirements of the products being manufactured―inhibits the 
practical and safe use of this additional PPE. As noted, the very nature of the product (long thin 
metal tubes) can also present an entanglement risk for the hoses associated with both types of 
respirators.  No other safety measures have been identified that could be used to reduce nPB 
concentrations to below the proposed ECEL in the interim other than entirely replacing both the 
current degreasing process and the use of nPB.  
 
2 Costs to implement a plant-wide airline respirator system that covers workers and ONUs across 
the over-100,000-square-foot plant floor are estimated to exceed $1.0 million, and the system is 
estimated to take about 8 months to install. 

 
FEDERAL eRULEMAKING PORTAL 
September 23, 2024 
Page 6 
e. Identifying Safe and Suitable Substitutes for 1-BP for Use in the Company’s 
Manufacturing Process.   
EPA requests comment on whether there are available substitutes for the use of 1-BP as a solvent 
in open-top and in-line batch vapor degreasing and the amount of time needed for vapor 
degreasers to transition to an alternative process or solvent. Our client has been actively working 
for over eight years to identify viable substitute chemicals and process changes to replace 1-BP, 
which it currently uses in both lubricant delivery and degreasing applications. To date, they have 
not found a safe and suitable alternative, although they are continuing to invest substantial 
resources to this effort and are actively working to find solutions. 
As early as 2014, our client began to evaluate aqueous cleaning as a potential degreasing option to 
replace nPB. In 2016, when nPB was designated as a high-priority chemical under TSCA and EPA 
initiated the risk evaluation process, our client began evaluating other alternatives to reduce or 
eliminate 1-BP from its manufacturing process.  These alternatives included additional chemical 
substitutions (various trans DCE formulations, Solveco hydrocarbon, modified alcohols) and 
additional equipment changes (closed-loop vacuum degreasing, aqueous cleaning). In total, at 
least seven chemical/equipment options were considered and evaluated in detail between 2016 
and 2022. Since 2022, when the risk evaluation of 1-BP was finalized by EPA, our client has 
engaged in an even more aggressive evaluation of alternatives focusing on replacing 1-BP in 
current degreasing equipment (chemical modifications) and changing current equipment to a 
closed loop system (equipment modifications). In total, this most recent evaluation has included at 
least six chemical modifications, five equipment modifications, and four lubricant modifications.  
In addition to the alternatives for degreasing applications, our client is evaluating lubricating 
alternatives for that essential part of the manufacturing process. Currently, nPB is used in the 
vapor degreaser to remove lubricants from the metal tubes (as described in Section II.b above), 
and changes to the lubricant could also impact the efficacy and suitability of a degreasing 
substitute. To date, none of the degreasing alternatives to 1-BP have been able to produce the 
same quality product that meets the exacting specifications of our client’s customers for any of the 
lubricants tested. In addition, several of the alternatives considered for vapor degreasing 
presented other safety concerns and new toxicity risks (e.g., PFAS and trans DCE) and 
fire/explosion risks (e.g., acetone), which has excluded those chemicals as viable substitutes. 
Others potential substitutes are also in the process of being evaluated (and possibly restricted) by 
EPA (including methylene chloride, PCE, and TCE). Our client does not want to make a “regrettable 
substitution” by switching to a chemical that may be found to be highly dangerous or toxic and 
therefore is focused on carrying out extensive vetting and testing before making any changes to 
the existing process.  
It also must be noted that any testing currently being completed by our client is done using small 
six inches pieces of metal tubing or flat, roughly 4”x4” square metal pieces composed of stock 
samples of similar metal alloys referred to as “coupons”. Equipment and chemical testing of very 
small metal pieces may not be an accurate substitute for the testing of tubes over 44 feet in length 
that are currently being produced. As a result, any testing of alternatives must go through multiple 
rounds of quality assurance review, including testing with different metal alloys to ensure that the 
final equipment/chemical selected can meet production specifications and successfully be scaled 
up. Compounding the challenges are the fact that 1-BP is currently used as both a lubricant and in 
a degreasing solvent in different parts of the manufacturing process, as noted above. Accordingly, 
compatible substitutes that are both safe and effective for both processes will need to be 

 
FEDERAL eRULEMAKING PORTAL 
September 23, 2024 
Page 7 
identified, tested, and approved.  And even once substitutes are successfully identified, our client 
must consider the specifications required for all final products, including those produced for DOD 
and DOE, as well as its commercial customers. Process changes require customer approval 
(including from DOD and DOE) that could span up to 18 months after trials are completed. 
Further illustrating the complexity of identifying a safe and reliable substitute for 1-BP, the 
company has determined this will require a significant equipment change, specifically, the purchase 
a new large-scale vacuum degreaser for use with the substitute chemicals (likely either modified 
alcohols or hydrocarbons, or a modified alcohol-hydrocarbon blend), at a cost in excess of $11 
million. Because both the chemicals and degreasing equipment will be entirely new with regard to 
the company’s processes, and because it is imperative the process performs as expected, the 
company will first need to purchase a smaller, less-expensive degreasing unit to simulate scalable 
aspects of the vacuum degreasing operation. This up-front investment will facilitate the company’s 
implementation of its testing plan to compare the efficacy of various solvent formula, confirm 
compatibility of the solvents with the different lubricants and alloys employed, determine if 
adjustments to lubrication strategy are required, understand long-term solvent stability, lifecycle 
and effects on equipment, prove cleaning and solvent drying effectiveness with various part 
geometries, and (ideally) develop scalable processes. The company has estimated that the smaller 
degreaser will require eight months to acquire and install, and that this complete process will take 
at least 24 months to complete. 
 
Please see Exhibit A to these comments for a chart summarizing many of the recent efforts and 
challenges faced by the company in finding a safe and reliable substitute for 1-BP.  
 
f. 
The Company is Trying to Avoid Making a Regrettable Substitution. 
In light of its prior experience switching from TCE to 1-BP, the company is concerned about 
selecting a substitute that may itself need to be replaced in a few years, particularly given the 
extensive R&D, time and cost that will be required for the 1-BP replacement. Unfortunately, that 
eliminates many possible alternatives. 
As EPA acknowledges in its Economic Analysis of the Proposed Regulation of 1-Bromopropane 
Under TSCA Section 6(a) (“Economic Analysis”), EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471-0114_content, “Three 
solvents sometimes used for vapor degreasing are also included in the first 10 TSCA work plan 
chemicals: methylene chloride, TCE, and PCE. The risk evaluations for these three chemicals all 
found an unreasonable risk under TSCA for their use in vapor degreasing. Given the regulatory 
uncertainty while EPA considers risk management options for these chemicals, this analysis 
assumes that these chemicals would not be adopted as alternative vapor degreasing solvents for 
1-BP.” Economic Analysis, §5.3. These solvents are indeed among those proposed to our client as 
substitutes and rejected for precisely these reasons. 
Perhaps the most commonly proposed ingredient in substitutes offered to our client is trans DCE); 
that, too, presents challenges, including its own health risks, as well as possible insurance, 
environmental and supply chain issues (since it is often combined with fluorinated chemicals). As 
EPA notes in its Economic Analysis, “In addition, one alternative to the use of 1-BP in vapor 
degreasing are nonflammable mixes of trans-1,2-dichloroethylene (trans DCE) and fluorinated 
chemicals; the availability of these chemical mixes could be impacted by 3M’s decision to cease 
manufacturing per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS), including Nederlandse Opstelpunten 
voor Ethercommunicatie (NOVEC) products (mixes of trans DCE and PFAS) at the end of 2025. The 

 
FEDERAL eRULEMAKING PORTAL 
September 23, 2024 
Page 8 
fluorinated inerting agents are also under scrutiny by the U.S. EPA and other regulators because of 
concerns about PFAS.” Economic Analysis, §5.3. 
In short, each of the different chemicals and substances being considered come with potential risks 
of their own, which must be carefully evaluated and appropriate health and safety solutions 
identified before they can be considered for implementation.  This requires more time than the 
proposed rule provides for, as further discussed below. 
III. 
The 36-Month Compliance Deadline Is Infeasible For this Facility.  
EPA requests comment on any advantages or drawbacks for the timelines outlined in the proposed 
and alternative regulatory actions. Based on the alternatives already considered (and the many 
already rejected), our client currently estimates that it will need at least 60 months (five years) 
from the time the rule is finalized to meet the 1-BP ECEL requirements.  
TSCA Section 6(d)(1)(E) requires that any final risk management rule must provide a reasonable 
transition period. TSCA Section 6(c)(2) requires that EPA consider the economic consequences of 
the rule, including the likely effect of the proposed rule on the national economy and technological 
innovation. The final rule must allow adequate time for our client to transition to away from nPB so 
that it can mitigate impacts to the national supply chain―as well as avoid any detrimental national 
security impacts―for its highly specialized components. By way of example the facility’s previous 
transition from TCE to nPB was a straightforward process that required limited process changes, 
and even that comparatively simple transition took approximately 2.5 years, or 30 months.  Any 
transition necessitated by EPA’s final rule, which will require more complicated and extensive 
process changes, will be much more complex and time-consuming, as illustrated by the efforts our 
client has taken to date to find a suitable replacement for nPB (described in Section II.e and 
Exhibit A). 
EPA’s proposed timeline also fails to leave sufficient time for new approvals of regulatory controls.  
For example, our client’s facility is a Title V facility. Assuming our client will have to eliminate nPB 
and upgrade its degreaser, under the Clean Air Act a New Source Review (NSR) would be required 
by both the EPA and state environmental agencies. The NSR process could take 12-18 months 
before our client could even purchase a new degreaser. Actual construction and replacement 
cannot begin until after a suitable 1-BP substitute is determined and customer specifications are 
approved. Lead times for an approved degreaser (which would need to be custom built) is 
estimated to be 12-18 months. Site preparation and building/infrastructure modifications to 
accommodate a new degreaser and solvent should be expected to take 12 months or more to 
complete. If, as expected, our client is required to deploy upgraded engineering controls, we 
estimate that it could take 18 months or more to validate process changes that are necessary to 
ensure the quality and integrity of products especially for United States military defense 
applications. EPA’s proposed compliance dates are thus grossly underestimated.  
a. If the Rule is Finalized as Proposed, The Company Will Require an Exemption from the 
Proposed Compliance Deadlines Pursuant to TSCA Section 6(g). 
 
EPA has requested public comment regarding the need for exemptions from the proposed 
requirements pursuant to the provisions of TSCA Section 6(g). In light of the exacting customer-
driven specifications necessary for the components that our client manufactures at its facility, 
which are critical in applications such as national defense programs and nuclear power generation, 
an exemption under Section 6(g) is not only warranted but necessary in order to ensure production 

 
FEDERAL eRULEMAKING PORTAL 
September 23, 2024 
Page 9 
continuity should EPA choose to finalize either the 36-month timeline for federal contractors or 
prohibit the use of 1-BP as a solvent in open-top and in-line batch vapor degreasing. 
 
TSCA Section 6(d) provides that any TSCA Section 6(a) rule must specify mandatory compliance 
dates, which shall be as soon as practicable with a reasonable transition period “but not later than 
5 years after the date of promulgation of the rule, except in a case of a use exempted under 
subsection (g).” 15 U.S.C. § 2605(d)(1)(B). Under Section 6(g) of TSCA, EPA is authorized to grant 
an exemption from a risk management rule if the Administrator makes one of three separate 
findings: 
 
(A) the specific condition of use is a critical or essential use for which no 
technically and economically feasible safer alternative is available, taking into 
consideration hazard and exposure; 
(B) compliance with the requirement, as applied with respect to the specific 
condition of use, would significantly disrupt the national economy, national 
security, or critical infrastructure; or 
(C) the specific condition of use of the chemical substance or mixture, as 
compared to reasonably available alternatives, provides a substantial benefit to 
health, the environment, or public safety. 
 
15 U.S.C. § 2605(g)(1). Section 6(g) exemptions require the Administrator to “analyze the need for 
the exemption”; to establish a specific time period for the exemption; and to promulgate 
reasonable conditions “to the extent that the Administrator determines the conditions are 
necessary to protect health and the environment while achieving the purposes of the exemption.” 
15 U.S.C. §§ 2605(g)(2)-(4). Critically, Section 6(g) exemptions allow EPA to recognize that in 
certain circumstances, the importance of the need to use a specific substance outweighs 
associated risks, even when those risks might not be appropriate in other applications.  
 
i. 
The Use of 1-BP is Critical and Essential For the Manufacturing Supercritical  
Components For Which No Technically and Economically Feasible Safer 
Alternative. 
 
As detailed above, the precision specialty tubes manufactured by our client are subject to exacting 
size and cleanliness requirements, and nPB is a key component of our client’s ability to meet both 
of those requirements. Currently, nPB is used to aid in the cold work process and as a degreasing 
solvent to remove all impurities on the tubes prior to heat treating and drawing. The facility 
operates one of the largest open-top and batch vapor degreasing units in the world, which is 
necessary to degrease tubes lengths over 44 feet long. At present, and despite multiple years of 
efforts to identify suitable substitutes, nPB is currently the only safe and effective method for 
working and degreasing tubes used during the manufacturing process to the precise specifications 
demanded by the company’s customers and products market, and is incorporated into all approved 
protocols for manufacturing defense-related components. 
 
ii. 
If Finalized, the Proposed Compliance Deadline of 36 Months Would 
Significantly Disrupt the National Economy, National Security, and Critical 
Infrastructure.  
 
The proposed rule (and proposed regulatory alternatives) would require our client to adhere to a 
three-year compliance deadline for eliminating nPB from its manufacturing process. As detailed 

 
FEDERAL eRULEMAKING PORTAL 
September 23, 2024 
Page 10 
above, our client’s monitoring results indicate that it would have to reduce worker and ONU 
exposed by two orders of magnitude to meet EPA’s proposed ECEL of 0.05 ppm. To achieve the 
ECEL relying on the facilities’ existing technology and controls, all workers and ONUs at the facility 
(approximately 200) would need to wear a continuous flow airline respirator in a work area that 
exceeds 100,000 square feet. While those measures would reduce the inhalation exposures, they 
would subject workers to significant physical risks. The company’s manufacturing operations 
require employees to be able to move freely about the facility and requires the use of cranes to 
move the large, thin tubes. Installation of a continuous flow airline respirator (which the company 
has identified as the form of PPE most likely to be able to achieve the ECEL) in that environment 
would increase the risk of tripping, choking, and other physical hazards during facility operations 
and hamper the free flow of movement by virtue of being tethered to the air flow source. 
Therefore, meeting the proposed ECEL would not only expose workers and ONUs to an increased 
level of physical risk due to the facility-specific processes, but also reduce the facility’s production 
capacity and hinder our client’s ability to satisfy commercial and government defense contracts for 
the most demanding supercritical applications.  
 
In the meantime, critical government orders will not be able to be fulfilled, jeopardizing our 
nation’s defense and nuclear energy programs (since those orders are typically placed years in 
advance). By way of example, the company has product orders under federal contracts for the US 
Department of Energy for which production will extend into 2026, assuming use of all current 
production capacity. Interruption of delivery on these orders is simply not an option.   
 
iii. 
Our Client’s Tubes Provide a Substantial Benefit to Health and Public safety. 
 
The company’s manufacturing process has led to groundbreaking new technologies and amazing 
new products that meet the toughest specifications for the most challenging applications. As noted 
previously, this facility is the sole supplier for three critical products supporting U.S. government 
defense programs run by the U.S. Department of Defense and U.S. Department of Energy and is 
the sole domestic supplier for components necessary to continued operation of an additional 
defense program. These products are necessary for successful continuation of these programs, 
with orders placed for these materials years in advance to ensure continuity in supply. 
 
Our client appreciates EPA’s acknowledgement of the procurement requirements that federal 
contractors must follow, see 89 Fed. Reg. at 65089, but would like to emphasize that the three-
year proposed timeline does not adequately provide for both the time required by the procurement 
process and the time required to implement process changes once approved. Importantly, the 
process of receiving approval from the U.S. defense programs that utilize the critical components 
manufactured at our client’s facility cannot begin until after the company has successfully identified 
an acceptable substitute, and the process of implementing any changes cannot occur until a 
proposed change has been approved. Once an alternative has been identified, customer approval 
involves a number of steps and could take more than 18 months. This process first requires the 
company to submit a process qualification plan to the customer for review and approval. After the 
qualification plan is approved, it typically takes the company 6 months to execute the qualification 
plan. The results are documented in a report that is submitted to the customer for further review 
and approval, which typically takes an additional 3 to 6 months before final approval is granted. 
Once approved, our client would then have to actually implement the transition. A recent example 
for a relatively simple process qualification for a critical defense-related product required more 
than five years. Based on the current status of the research efforts described above in Section II.e 

 
FEDERAL eRULEMAKING PORTAL 
September 23, 2024 
Page 11 
and the procurement estimates and timelines obtained to date, our client estimates that the very 
earliest this transition could be completed is five years from publication of a final rule.   
 
In light of the progress made in research efforts to date, our client anticipates that a viable 
alternative is within reach but will require more time than is allowed under the proposed rule. 
Therefore, EPA should revise the proposed rule to incorporate a time-limited exemption under 
TSCA Section 6(g) of a five-year compliance deadline to enable the conversion of open-top vapor 
degreasers with a new technology that will satisfy the objective of the proposed rule while 
mitigating impacts to the national supply chain, as required by TSCA Section 6(d). 
IV. 
Prescriptive Engineering or Administrative Controls Are Not Appropriate or Feasible.  
EPA is soliciting comment on prescribing specific engineering or administrative controls that would 
reduce inhalation exposures enough to address the unreasonable risk across all industrial uses of 
1-BP in open-top and in-line batch vapor degreasing. The company strongly discourages the 
proposed primary regulatory alternative of adopting prescriptive controls. While the use of 
performance-based measures would provide some level of flexibility in the manufacturing process 
to account for continued innovation, individual facility footprints, and individual process needs that 
may vary from facility to facility, a prescriptive control intended to apply broadly across uses could 
render certain facilities, such as our client’s facility, unable to continue their operations due to 
restrictions that cannot be reconciled with process and/or client requirements.   
In contrast, the use of performance-based measures allowing regulated entities to select measures 
applicable and appropriate for their individual facilities would allow for continued innovation, 
individual facility footprints, and individual processes needs that likely vary from industry to 
industry. 
 
V. 
Other 
a. Federal Contractor Compliance Schedule Should Apply to All Commercial Applications 
at the Same Facility.  
All of the company’s products are manufactured on the same assembly lines and go through the 
same degreaser unit utilizing 1-BP regardless of the end customer. Accordingly, it is simply not 
feasible to have separate timelines for federal and non-federal customers. As a practical matter, 
switching to new chemicals will result in a process change and procuring new equipment. It is not 
viable to make these changes before the customer―and more specifically, the federal government/ 
military customers―have approved all of the changes. Otherwise, the company could invest 
millions of dollars in new, custom-built equipment for a process that ultimately is rejected and 
cannot be used, resulting in further delays and unnecessary expense. While the proposed rule does 
not currently address such joint operations, language should be included in the final rule to make it 
clear that where the same operations and equipment are used for projects subject to government 
contracts as are used for other uses, the longer federal timeline should govern.  
 
b. Potential Costs Associated With Compliance. 
EPA seeks to obtain more information on potential costs that could be incurred. EPA estimates 
$14.8-$15.5 million annualized over 20 years (depending on discount rate) for all users. While we 
recognize that these are central estimates, we believe EPA has nevertheless greatly 
underestimated the costs of implementing this proposed rule. For this one industrial company 
alone, it will cost at least that much just to upgrade the equipment and processes – and that 

 
FEDERAL eRULEMAKING PORTAL 
September 23, 2024 
Page 12 
doesn’t include the years of employee time and associated expense to investigate alternative 
products and equipment, or increased operations and maintenance costs for the new equipment. 
Our client expects a plant-wide airline respirator system alone would exceed $1.0M to install 
(assuming installation and use of such a system were even compatible with the nature of this 
plant’s operations). In addition, routine operation and maintenance (O&M) is estimated to be 
$50,000 per year, and routine exposure monitoring to be $25,000 per year. The company 
estimates costs of over $11 million for a new closed-loop vapor degreaser (including engineering 
and construction management, site modifications, shipping, labor, fire protection, decommissioning 
and removal of the current unit, and of course the machine itself). The company also estimates an 
additional $3 million investment for fugitive emissions controls, which assumes a scenario where 
the company operates the current degreaser in parallel with a new closed-loop vapor degreaser. 
Controls would potentially include (but are not limited to) hoods, a carbon system, exhaust in and 
around the degreaser, and a temperature-controlled air system. 
c. Ambient Air Monitoring. 
Beyond discussion regarding the ECEL for workers and ONUs within the facility, EPA is requesting 
comment on the need for and associated costs of ambient air monitoring at fenceline locations, 
including fenceline monitoring, or facility emissions source monitoring, as well as information on 
the frequency and nature of air monitoring EPA should consider including as requirements in the 
final rule (such as a detection limit for 1–BP). EPA is also requesting comment on methods to 
inform fenceline communities of any increases of 1–BP emissions to ambient air.  
Our client agrees that understanding potential fenceline exposures is important but would like to 
emphasize that collection of such data under this proposal is unnecessary given current regulatory 
requirements. Our client’s facility operates under a Title V Operating Permit (TVOP) issued by the 
state environmental agency which incorporates a plant-wide applicability limit (PAL) for volatile 
organic compounds (VOC), including nPB. The TVOP conditions, including the PAL, are specifically 
designed to confirm that any facility emissions – including emissions of nPB – are within the limits 
set pursuant to state and federal regulations (such as federal National Emission Standards for 
Hazardous Air Pollutants, or pursuant to source-specific Reasonably Available Control Technology 
requirements). To demonstrate compliance with its PAL and other TVOP conditions, the facility is 
required to monitor facility emissions and must meet work practice requirements such as using 
specific fuels or conducting equipment tune-ups at specific intervals. In addition, as a condition of 
the facility permit, a modeling evaluation was completed to further demonstrate off-site impacts 
are within acceptable levels, including observable ambient air concentrations of nPB that are below 
the proposed ECEL. All Title V permits are renewed every five years, requiring that facilities re-
evaluate emissions and identify any process changes between permit applications.  
 
The existing permits and existing air quality requirements that our client maintains are considered 
sufficient to protect fenceline communities. Our client actively works with Federal and state 
agencies that develop permit limits that are protective of public health.  In reality, most facilities, 
operate at levels well below limits established in permits. However, even at the permit limits 
established for this facility, the ambient levels at the fenceline have been shown to be below levels 
that could be of concern to the community, based on guidelines established by the regulatory 
agencies and recent state ambient air monitoring. Given available data and the established permit 
conditions which the facility must comply with, it is inadvisable to conduct additional fenceline 
monitoring that could be reported to the public and easily misunderstood.   
 

 
FEDERAL eRULEMAKING PORTAL 
September 23, 2024 
Page 13 
d. Determinations for Re-monitoring 
It is not necessary to have Environmental Professional or Certified Industrial Hygienist make the 
determination if re-monitoring is required. Retesting should be determined by designated 
personnel responsible for the implementation of the 1-BP WCPP.

--- COMMENT ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471-0128 AND DOCKET ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 ---
September 9, 2024 
 
Michael S. Regan 
Administrator 
United States Environmental Protection Agency 
 
Comments to the EPA on the Proposed Rule for 1-Bromopropane 
Agency/Docket Numbers: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471; FRL-8156-01-OCSPP 
RIN: 2070-AK73 
 
Dear Administrator Regan, 
I am a practicing Industrial Hygienist who evaluates workplace exposures to chemical and physical 
hazards. I appreciate the opportunity to provide feedback on the United States Environmental Protection 
Agency’s (EPA) proposed rule on 1-Bromopropane (1-BP). 
 
1. EPA is requesting public comment on all elements of the proposed regulatory action and the 
alternative regulatory actions. 
In regard to the requirement to “…re-monitor within 15 working days after receipt of results of any 
exposure monitoring when results indicate non-detect or air monitoring equipment malfunction, unless an 
Environmental Professional as defined at 40 CFR 312.10 or a Certified Industrial Hygienist reviews the 
monitoring results and determines re-monitoring is not necessary,” the EPA must clarify in the final 
regulation the definition of a “non-detect” result. 
 
Often, Industrial Hygienists receive sampling results that indicate a result is “less than the limit of 
detection” (LOD) of the sampling method (for example, a laboratory result of <0.1 ppm). Typically, this 
type of result is indicative of a well-controlled exposure (i.e., a result which is below the ECEL Action 
Level) which does not need to be re-monitored within 15 working days. 
 
Therefore, if the EPA is going to define “less than the limit of detection” (LOD) as a “non-detect” result, 
this does not make technical sense. 
 
If, however, a non-detect result is due to sampling issues like not enough air volume collection, then re-
monitoring should be required.  
 
A clarification in definition is needed to ensure excessive personal monitoring is not pursued for 
processes that are well-controlled. 
 
Additionally, the proposed requirement to re-monitor within 15 working days may be exceedingly difficult 
to coordinate. It is often the case that businesses have to locate a contractor, negotiate pricing, establish 
a contract, and schedule on-site visits to perform exposure monitoring (due to limited staffing of company 
IH professionals). Further, some operations using 1-BP may occur very infrequently (e.g., only conducted 
quarterly or biannually or annually). For these reasons, in the event that re-sampling is needed, the 
timeline should be longer than 15 days; a 30-60 day resurvey deadline (from the date of the initial event) 
would be less burdensome.  
 
9. EPA is soliciting comment regarding an ECEL action level that is lower than the ECEL and any 
associated provisions related to the ECEL action level. 
Compliance to monitor to the proposed ECEL action level may be difficult.  
 
Currently, the only feasible method to monitor to such a low ECEL action limit is via a small charcoal tube 
(passive monitoring badges do not have a high enough air uptake rate). Using a small charcoal tube, 1-
BP can be detected down to 20% of the proposed ECEL over a full 8-hour work shift.  
 
Thus, due to the low ECEL action level, options for exposure monitoring are severely restricted. 
 

10. EPA requests comment on the feasibility of complying with and monitoring for an ECEL of 
0.05 ppm and an ECEL action level of 0.03 ppm, including occupational exposure monitoring and 
associated analytical methods. In particular, EPA requests comment on changes that may be 
needed in order to meet such a standard, for example changes related to elimination or 
substitution of 1–BP, engineering controls, process changes, or monitoring frequency. EPA is 
also interested in the information on the availability of laboratory capacity needed to meet the 
proposed standard, and the costs associated with such testing.  
Utilizing NIOSH 1500/1501(GC), it is currently feasible to perform employee personal exposure 
monitoring to comply with the ECEL of 0.05 ppm. Via a small charcoal tube (such as SKC 226-01 media) 
with a detection limit of 2.7 micrograms and a flow rate of 0.2 liters per minute, down to 20% of the ECEL 
can be detected on the sample media over the course of a work shift.  
 
Notably, under the NIOSH 1500/1501(GC), it would not be possible to detect 10% of the ECEL over an 8-
hour work shift, as the time required would exceed 480 minutes. Industrial Hygiene professionals often 
use this 10% ECEL value as an important risk-ranking criteria to professionally judge if an exposure is 
“well controlled.” If IHs are unable to establish if a sampling result is specifically <10% the ECEL using the 
NIOSH 1500/1501 method, it will be difficult for IHs (and other safety professionals) to provide certainty in 
their professional evaluations.   
 
11. EPA is soliciting comments regarding the timing of the initial exposure monitoring so that it 
would be representative of all tasks involving 1– BP where exposures may approach the ECEL. 
The timelines proposed for initial exposure monitoring seem to be reasonable for industry and federal 
agencies/contractors working on behalf of a federal agency.  
 
I am concerned, however, about widespread notification of workplace exposure limits and requirements 
via TSCA regulations (as opposed to OSHA). Owners and operators are unlikely to meet the required 
timelines for initial exposure monitoring and subsequent requirements established by TSCA regulations if 
they are not aware of them.  
 
12. EPA requests comment on the timeframes for periodic monitoring outlined in Table 1 of Unit 
IV.A.2. 
The timelines proposed for periodic monitoring appear reasonable and appropriate.  
 
However, the proposed requirement to collect two exposure monitoring measurements (below the ECEL 
action level of <0.03 ppm 8-hr TWA) taken at least seven days apart within a 6-month period appears 
impractical. A requirement of one additional sample (as opposed to two) would suffice as verification of 
exposure below the ECEL action level. 
 
13. EPA requests comment on workplace monitoring for implementation of an ECEL. EPA is 
soliciting information related to the frequency of monitoring, initial monitoring, and periodic 
monitoring that would be needed to demonstrate workplace exposure levels. Specifically, when 
this may impact the frequency of periodic monitoring where initial monitoring shows that 
employee exposures are above the level that would initiate requirements for compliance with the 
ECEL. 
The proposed requirement to collect two exposure monitoring measurements (below the ECEL action 
level of <0.03 ppm 8-hr TWA) taken at least seven days apart within a 6-month period appears 
impractical. A requirement of one additional sample (as opposed to two) would suffice as verification of 
exposure below the ECEL action level. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

14. EPA is requesting comment on the proposed timeframe of within 30 days to conduct 
additional exposure monitoring after there has been a change in the production, process, control 
equipment, personnel or work practices that may reasonably be expected to result in new or 
additional exposures at or above the ECEL action level, or when the owner or operator has any 
reason to believe that new or additional exposures at or above the ECEL action level have 
occurred.  
EPA should lengthen the proposed timeframe to conduct additional exposure monitoring following a 
change in production, process, control equipment, personnel or work practices to 45-60 days (as opposed 
to 30 days).  
 
This extended timeframe would allow employers without dedicated Industrial Hygiene staff to locate a 
contractor, negotiate pricing, establish a contract, and schedule an on-site visit (which can be a lengthy 
process) as necessary. 
 
17. EPA is soliciting comment on requiring warning signs to demarcate regulated areas, such as 
the requirements found in OSHA’s General Industry Standard for Beryllium. 
The proposed requirement to require warning signs to demarcate 1-BP regulated areas is appropriate.  
 
The EPA should further specify in the final regulation language to be included in the signage. For 
example, the sign specification that OSHA requires for Beryllium (1910.1024(m)(2)(ii)(B)) includes: 
DANGER 
REGULATED AREA 
BERYLLIUM 
MAY CAUSE CANCER 
CAUSES DAMAGE TO LUNGS 
AUTHORIZED PERSONNEL ONLY 
WEAR RESPIRATORY PROTECTION AND PERSONAL PROTECTIVE CLOTHING AND EQUIPMENT 
IN THIS AREA 
 
18. EPA is requesting comment on whether there should be a requirement to replace cartridges or 
canisters after a certain number of hours, such as the requirements found in OSHA’s General 
Industry Standard for 1,3-Butadiene, or a requirement for a minimum service life of non-powered 
air-purifying respirators such as the requirements found in OSHA’s General Industry Standard for 
Benzene. 
The EPA could feasibly establish a requirement for respirator cartridge change-out schedule similar to the 
OSHA General Industry Standard for 1,3-Butadiene (using the ECEL as the comparison tool and a 
multiplication factor).  
 
Further research would be needed to determine if the respirator cartridge change-out requirements are 
satisfactory (i.e., balancing worker protection with minimization of cartridge waste).  
Development of a 1-BP service life indicator for cartridges would be preferred over keeping track of the 
amount of time a cartridge has been used. 
 
20. EPA requests comment relative to the ability of owners or operators to conduct initial 
monitoring within the timeframes identified in this proposed rule, and anticipated timelines for any 
procedural adjustments needed to comply with the requirements in this proposed rule, including 
establishment of a respiratory protection program and development of an exposure control plan. 
The proposed timelines to conduct initial monitoring appear reasonable for owners or operators with 
dedicated Industrial Hygiene staff and/or established respiratory programs.  
 
The proposed timelines may or may not be adequate for other owners or operators. 
 
21. EPA also requests comment on whether additional time is needed to implement all aspects of 
the WCPP or if there are available substitutes for these applications. 
The proposed timeline appears reasonable for owners or operators with dedicated Industrial Hygiene staff 
and/or established respiratory programs. 

 
The proposed timeline may or may not be adequate for other owners or operators. 
 
22. EPA is soliciting comments on the requirements proposed for appropriate PPE selection. In 
addition, EPA understands that some workplaces rinse and reuse PPE after minimal use and is 
therefore soliciting comments on the impact on effectiveness of rinsing and reusing certain types 
of PPE, either gloves or protective clothing and gear. EPA also requests comment on the degree 
to which additional guidance related to use of PPE might be appropriate. 
It is unclear if the use of polyvinyl alcohol (PVA) or multiple-layer laminate glove materials is required for 
the listed occupational uses [manufacturing (import), processing as a reactant, processing as 
incorporation into articles, processing by repackaging, recycling, and disposal], or if all potentially 
exposed persons must wear them. This requirement needs further clarification. 
 
Additionally, while PVA and multiple-layer laminate gloves can withstand exposure to 1-BP for 480 
minutes according to testing, this amount of chemical resistance is not necessary for all applications/uses 
of 1-BP.  
 
As an Industrial Hygienist who frequently provides workers with PPE requirements and recommendations, 
it is my view that PPE selection should be left up to trained safety professionals knowledgeable of the 
work activities and potential for dermal exposure. For example, over prescription of a glove material that 
reduces dexterity may make certain work activities like (e.g., pipetting small amounts of highly corrosive 
acid) more hazardous (by increasing risk of splashes/spills). Further, as PPE research evolves, initial 
requirements made by the EPA may become outdated (e.g., another material provides adequate or 
excellent protection at a fraction of the cost). 
 
26. EPA requests comment on the primary alternative regulatory action (a combination of 
prohibitions, requirements for a WCPP, prescriptive controls, self-certification, and glove use) and 
whether any elements of this primary alternative regulatory action described in this proposed rule 
should be considered as EPA develops the final regulatory action. In particular, EPA is soliciting 
comment on prescribing specific engineering or administrative controls that would reduce 
inhalation exposures enough to address the unreasonable risk across all workplaces engaged in 
a condition of use. EPA also requests comment on any advantages or drawbacks for the timelines 
outlined in Unit IV.B. compared to the timelines identified for the proposed regulatory action in 
Unit IV.A. 
The EPA prescribing specific engineering and/or administrative controls may contribute positively as to 
how owners and operators comply with TSCA regulation. If, however, the controls prescribed are 
exceptionally burdensome, this may lead to negative consequences like companies unable to afford 
compliance measures.  
 
It may be better to suggest or highly encourage the use of certain controls, but to allow for a certain 
amount of professional judgement as long as personal exposures remain below the ECEL or Action 
Level. 
 
Longer compliance timelines are always appreciated when new regulations are implemented. 
 
35. EPA is requesting comment on specific controls that would mitigate the unreasonable risk 
from 1–BP and that could be included as part of a prescriptive workplace controls requirement, 
which could be considered as EPA develops the final regulatory action. Specifically, EPA is 
soliciting comment on combinations of specific engineering controls, administrative controls, and 
PPE that would reduce inhalation exposures to at or below the ECEL of 0.05 ppm as an 8-hour 
TWA for all workplaces where such controls would be required. EPA also is soliciting comment on 
the extent to which such requirements could reduce inhalation exposures to at or below the ECEL 
of 0.05 ppm as an 8-hour TWA. EPA is requesting comment on the compliance timeframe needed 
to implement engineering controls, administrative controls, and PPE that reduce inhalation 
exposures to at below the ECEL of 0.05 ppm as an 8- hour TWA for all regulated entities. 
Additionally, EPA is requesting any existing monitoring data that could inform whether a WCPP or 

prescriptive controls with a PPE program is a more appropriate regulatory action for industrial 
and commercial use of 1–BP in batch vapor degreasing. 
It is difficult to recommend specific controls for general operations due to each workplace having different 
processes, work practices, operational set-ups, etc.  
 
Suggestions for general controls include: 
• 
Elimination: conduct work processes remotely 
• 
Engineering: enclose/contain work processes 
• 
Administrative: conduct process unattended (typically start at the end of a work shift and continue 
overnight or over the weekend) 
 
In terms of implementation of new/novel Industrial Hygiene controls (which may completely change work 
processes), adequate planning (likely a year or more) and significant funds are likely needed.  
 
42. EPA requests comment on whether owners and operators should be required to attest to 
whether and why the exposure controls they have selected would not result in increased releases 
of 1–BP to ambient air from the workplace, and keep records of that statement as part of the 
WCPP exposure control plan. 
An attestation statement regarding whether and why exposure controls selected would not result in 
increased releases of 1-BP to ambient air from the workplace does not seem especially effective as it is 
likely to be viewed as an unnecessary add-on to administrative burden by stakeholders.  
 
It would be more effective to require environmental air (emission) data that proves 1-BP is not being 
released to ambient air from outside of local exhaust stacks. 
 
 
Thank you for your time and consideration. 
 
Sincerely, 
Asheton Gilbertson 
Industrial Hygienist

--- COMMENT ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471-0139 AND DOCKET ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 ---
Vapor degreasing: suggestions for clear, effective sustainable rules for 1-BP, MC, 
TCE, and PCE
Barbara Kanegsberg and Ed Kanegsberg, BFK Solutions
We have reviewed rules and proposed rules for1-BP, MC, TCE, and PCE and are 
making the following suggestions to EPA as a possible win-win approach. The proposed 
rule for 1-BP (nPB), includes interesting, innovative ideas that will make compliance and 
interpretation more accessible to the manufacturing community. We include 
suggestions to U.S. EPA Amended TSCA to harmonize rules for the four solvents, codify 
the final rules in a single location, recognize the potential for catastrophic impact on 
patient and public safety of “regrettable substitution” and suggest EPA add Reduced 
Pressure Sealed Systems as a class of cleaning equipment with built-in engineering 
controls. While our comments and suggestions focus on issues related to vapor 
degreasing, some might be adaptable to other regulatory aspects. 
Suggestion: Harmonize the final rules for MC, TCE, PCE, and1-BP (nPB)
As of September 22, 2024, while each rule or proposed rule represents a high level of 
thoughtful effort, very different and puzzling conclusions have been reached. Perhaps 
one or two EPA representatives for each solvent could form an overarching group to 
distill and build on the approach for each of the four solvents.  Just as the ECEL for MC, 
TCE, PCE, and1-BP (nPB) would be consolidated into a single document (Proposed 1-
BP rule, Unit I C (vi) ), consider issuing a single document for risk management for the 
four solvents. At the very least, change the wording for all four solvents so that in the 
final rules, the approach to risk management is similar.  
Our wish is that each harmonized rule include the following 
• Allow vapor degreasing – the ban for MC is arbitrary
• Emphasize and clarify the route of self-certification
• Recognize a category for Reduced Pressure, Sealed Systems (1) and include the 
proviso for other demonstrated technologies
• Because the criticality of cleaning depends on the end-use of the product being 
cleaned, included in each final rule, the four cleaning categories (General, Precision, 
Safety/Critical, and R&D) as defined in Section 7.7 of the Economic Analysis for 1-BP 
(proposed 1-BP rule: Unit IX Reference 3).
• Remove what appear to us to be arbitrary, unjustifiable exemptions and what might be 
perceived as special treatment for aerospace, NASA, and government facilities. 
Suggestion: Provide mechanisms for those involved in safety/critical cleaning to 
continue to use validated, established technology. For that category, allow use of 
a worker exposure level such as Cal/OSHA or ACGIH as opposed to the ECEL. 
As we explained in our August 2024 newsletter (2), Safety/Critical Cleaning is a 
category where, if the product is not cleaned appropriately, airplanes drop out of the sky 
and peoples tushies fall off. The risks associated with inadequate cleaning are 
unacceptable; product failure can result in multiple deaths or injuries. While the category 

Safety/Critical Cleaning appears in the economic analysis for 1-BP; we have not found 
mention in any of the final or proposed rules. The category must be recognized.
Safety/Critical Applications require extensive, time-consuming, costly validation and 
verification. Manufacturers balk at changing any cleaning process. For many medical 
devices, costs can run into the millions of dollars. 1-BP, TCE, MC, and PCE have similar 
molecular structures and therefor similar functions; moving from one to another is 
sometimes referred to as an “in-kind” substitution. A highly stringent regulatory 
restriction or a shortage brought on by those restrictions can force businesses to 
change to another solvent. While changes in Safety/Critical processes require process 
development and revalidation, there is less risk and, frankly less effort, for in-kind 
substitution than for moving to not in-kind substitution (using a very different molecule or 
blend). The regulations need to have similar language and to have to have similar 
requirements. It is unrealistic, risky and unnecessary to force manufacturers to interpret 
the differences between rule after complex rule. Harmonization is essential.
In addition to having EPA provide the option of self-certification for 1-BP and for the 
other three halogenated solvents for safety/critical applications, we strongly suggest that 
for this Safety/Critical category, EPA allow a worker exposure level that has been 
relatively recently established by an agency or group like Cal/OSHA (DOSH) or ACGIH. 
For safety/critical applications, the risk of adopting a cleaning agent or process that has 
not been thoroughly tested needs to include risks to the public of product failure as well 
as the risks of worker, ONU and neighborhood exposure. 
These suggestions are analogous to the way the category of “precision cleaning” allows 
for more acceptable options under the EPA Significant New Alternatives Policy (SNAP) 
Program. The Safety/Critical category should be designed so as not to pressure or even 
encourage business to change processes without complete revalidation and testing. 
Unless it can be shown that the combined risk, including that from product failure, is not 
increased by requiring an alternative process, an established albeit higher exposure 
limit should be allowed to continue. This option is important for manufacturers upstream 
in the supply chain as well as final assembly.
Suggestion: Recognize and foster the use of reduced pressure, sealed systems
What we have termed ‘reduced pressure, sealed systems’ are a cleaning technology 
that includes engineering controls to minimize air emissions and worker exposure (1). 
We suggest that such systems be recognized and included in each rule as a specific 
category. Terms like closed loop or airless do not describe the technology and can be 
misconstrued. Wording could be included to encourage development of this and other 
effective cleaning systems that reduce emissions to acceptable levels.
Details
The 1-BP rule recognizes either open-top degreasers (OTVD) or “closed loop batch 
vapor degreasers.” The term “closed loop” does not accurately or unambiguously 
describe a category of cleaning machine that minimizes both air emissions and worker 
exposure. The term can be mis-interpreted to include systems that redistill the solvent 

for re-use but do not necessarily reduce emissions to near zero. In contrast with open 
top degreasers, in a reduced pressure sealed system, there is no interface between the 
cleaning solvent and air when parts are introduced to or removed from the cleaning 
chamber. Solvent is not introduced into the cleaning chamber until the chamber has 
been closed, sealed and the internal pressure has been reduced. The chamber is not 
re-opened until solvent has been removed from the chamber, returned to storage 
container(s) and the cleaned product is dry. Final trace solvent residue can be captured 
in carbon.
Section 7.7 of the Economic Analysis for 1-BP includes the costs of replacing an OTVD 
using 1-BP with an airless degreaser containing PCE. The analysis does not include the 
option to use an airless degreaser containing 1-BP. 
We suggest the term “airless degreaser” be replaced by “Reduced Pressure, Sealed 
System.” The term ‘airless’ relates to SCAQMD rules developed during the mid-1990s. 
Airless is neither descriptive nor accurate; the cleaning systems do not achieve a true 
“airless” state. They provide containment by being sealed and operating at a reduced 
pressure, not necessarily a hard vacuum. 
Notes:
A - These comments expand on “Survival of the Fittest, Critical Cleaning, and EPA 
Amended TSCA Rules - Coping with Change,” originally published in “Clean Source,
The Official Journal of the Cleaning Lady and the Rocket Scientist” August 2024. This 
feature article was submitted to this docket on 9/21/2024. The following additional 
comments and suggestions also draw from “Understand and Address the Regulatory 
Landscape--(Survival tactics for effective product cleaning & manufacturing),” presented
by Ed Kanegsberg at the Parts Cleaning Conference (PPC) in Chicago, Sept 10, 2024.
B - Our comments and suggestions are based on the conviction that, for sustainable 
regulations, it is essential to optimize worker safety, achieve environmental protection, 
maintain product quality, and support U.S. manufacturing. Our clients include 
manufacturers of product and components, the military, government (including the U.S 
EPA), as well as providers and advocates of cleaning chemicals and cleaning 
processes. Our three decades of working with our clients inform our viewpoint. That 
said, our opinions are our own; and we have not used client funding for this 
article/submission.

--- COMMENT ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471-0143 AND DOCKET ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 ---
