RE: 1-Bromopropane (1-BP); Regulation Under the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA);
Proposed Rule, EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471
Dear Ms. Masten:
The Alliance for Automotive Innovation (Auto Innovators)1 appreciates the opportunity to provide
comments on EPA’s proposed risk management rule: “1-Bromopropane (1-BP); Regulation Under
the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA).”2
EPA is proposing in this rule to:

Prohibit the manufacture (including import), processing, and distribution in commerce of
1-BP for all consumer uses (excluding insulation for building and construction materials).

Prohibit the manufacture (including import), processing and distribution in commerce of 1-
BP for four industrial and commercial uses and applications, including uses in adhesives
and sealants, in dry cleaning solvents, spot cleaners and stain removers, in coin and
scissor cleaner (liquid, spray, or aerosol cleaners), and in other uses in arts, crafts, hobby
materials (adhesive accelerant); automotive care products (engine degreaser, brake
cleaner and refrigerant flush and anti-adhesive agents (mold cleaning and release
product); functional fluids (closed/open systems)—refrigerant/cutting oils.

Require strict workplace controls, including a 1-BP Workplace Chemical Protection
Program (WCPP), which would include requirements to meet an inhalation exposure
concentration limit, for seven occupational conditions of use of 1-BP.

Require the use of prescriptive controls for six occupational conditions of use of 1-BP.

Require purchasers to provide sellers with a self-certification, which would document the
purchaser’s commitment to comply with the 1-BP WCPP, for six occupational conditions
of use of 1-BP.

Establish recordkeeping and downstream notification requirements.
1 Auto Innovators represents the full auto industry, including the manufacturers producing most vehicles sold in
the U.S., equipment suppliers, battery producers, semiconductor makers, technology companies, and
autonomous vehicle developers. Our mission is to work with policymakers to realize a cleaner, safer, and
smarter transportation future and to maintain U.S. competitiveness in cutting-edge automotive technology.
Representing approximately 5 percent of the country’s GDP, responsible for supporting nearly 10 million jobs,
and driving $1 trillion in annual economic activity, the automotive industry is the nation’s largest manufacturing
sector. www.autosinnovate.org.
2 89 Fed. Reg. 65,066 (Aug. 8, 2024).

1050 K ST, NW | 6th Floor | Washington, DC 20001 | autosinnovate.org
A review of the data currently available to our members appears to indicate that there is little if any
use of 1-BP at this time. We would, however, like to address a specific question EPA posed in this
proposed rule as EPA’s understanding of this issue is critical not only for this rulemaking but for all
TSCA risk evaluations and management rules. Specifically:
EPA also requests comment on whether, rather than just excluding the consumer and
commercial uses of 1–BP in insulation from the prohibitions and other requirements in
this risk management rulemaking, EPA should more broadly exclude the use of articles
under TSCA section 6(c)(2)(E), which would also exclude the use of 1–BP in articles
that were not specifically evaluated in the 2020 Risk Evaluation for 1–BP, and if so,
whether and how to define ‘‘article’’ for the purposes of this rulemaking.3
In addition to articles, Auto Innovators would also like to address automotive replacement parts, a
related and crucial issue.
Articles
Section 6(c) of TSCA requires that EPA shall apply prohibitions or other restrictions to an article or
category of articles containing the chemical substance or mixture only to the extent necessary to
address the identified risks from exposure to the chemical substance or mixture from the article:
In selecting among prohibitions and other restrictions, the Administrator shall apply
such prohibitions or other restrictions to an article or category of articles containing the
chemical substance or mixture only to the extent necessary to address the identified
risks from exposure to the chemical substance or mixture from the article or the
category of articles so that the substance or mixture does not present an unreasonable
risk of injury to health or the environment identified in the risk evaluation conducted in
accordance with subsection (b)(4)(A).4
This provision limits EPA’s authority to regulate articles in commerce. EPA’s authority is clearly
limited to applying regulatory restrictions on articles only if there is an unreasonable risk finding
pursuant to a risk evaluation, and even then “only to the extent necessary” to address those
identified risks. Therefore, without a risk evaluation, the agency’s authority to regulate articles in this
rulemaking and in all others under TSCA section 6(a) is substantially limited. Auto Innovators agrees
that EPA should broadly exclude the use of articles under TSCA section 6(c)(2)(E) in this and other
rulemakings. Auto Innovators urges EPA to apply the TSCA section 6(c) provisions at the risk
evaluation stage and only include articles in subsequent risk management provisions if warranted.
Auto Innovators is concerned that in past proposed and final rules where articles have been included
as a condition of use, EPA has not identified what exposures are occurring as a direct result of the
“exposure to the chemical substance or mixture from the article.” The associated use documents
appear to make no distinction between the exposures, risks, and impacts associated with direct
chemical exposure versus any potential limited or negligible exposure to a chemical bound into an
article.
3 Id. at 65,078.
4 15 U.S.C. § 2605(c)(2)(E) (emphasis added).

1050 K ST, NW | 6th Floor | Washington, DC 20001 | autosinnovate.org
Replacement Parts
TSCA Section 6(c)(2)(D) directs EPA to exempt replacement parts for complex durable goods and
complex consumer goods that are designed prior to the date of publication in the Federal Register of
a rule under TSCA Section 6(a).
The Administrator shall exempt replacement parts for complex durable goods and
complex consumer goods that are designed prior to the date of publication in the
Federal Register of the rule under subsection (a), unless the Administrator finds that
such replacements parts contribute significantly to the risk, identified in a risk evaluation
conducted under subsection (b)(4)(A), to the general population or to an identified
potentially exposed or susceptible subpopulation.5
TSCA requires EPA to “exempt replacement parts for complex durable goods and complex
consumer goods” except in instances where the “replacement parts contribute significantly to the
risk” identified in the risk evaluation. Therefore, EPA’s Section 6(a) authority is limited to applying
regulatory restrictions on replacement parts only if there is a risk evaluation under Section 6(b)(4)(A)
that finds unreasonable risk. If the risk evaluation finds that there is an unreasonable risk, the
agency must provide evidence that the replacement parts contribute significantly to that risk.
Imposing regulatory requirements on replacement parts manufactured to repair vehicles will be cost-
prohibitive. To redesign or reengineer a part, including validation of the durability, reliability, and
safety for a vehicle no longer in production, is not a workable option. If pre-existing replacement
parts for older models are subject to Section 6 regulation, a series of significant results could follow:
 
Replacement parts would not be available for needed repairs, and consumers would not be
able to repair their vehicles, which would devalue their investment in a vehicle.
 
Automotive safety may be jeopardized as “workarounds” are developed to extend the life of
individual vehicles.
 
Repair shops and companies that manufacture and use these parts would be significantly
disadvantaged by losses in revenue and stock value.
As with articles, Auto Innovators recommends that EPA broadly exclude uses of replacement parts.
Conclusion
In conclusion, while we have no specific comments on 1-BP at this time, Auto Innovators
appreciates the opportunity to reiterate our past concerns abouts EPA’s lack of implementation of
TSCA Sections 6(c)(2)(E) on articles and 6(c)(2)(D) on replacement parts. We look forward to
understanding how EPA will implement these provisions and recommend that EPA engage with
members of the regulated community to fully understand the implications of these key provisions of
the Lautenberg Chemical Safety Act. We would welcome the opportunity to meet with EPA staff to
more fully explain our concerns.
5 Id. § 2605(c)(2)(D).

1050 K ST, NW | 6th Floor | Washington, DC 20001 | autosinnovate.org
Sincerely,
Catherine Palin
Alliance for Automotive Innovation

Dear Assistant Administrator Freedhoff: 
 
The undersigned organizations submit these comments on EPA’s proposed risk 
management rule (the “Proposed Rule”) for 1-bromopropane (“1-BP”).1 
1-BP is a highly toxic, carcinogenic solvent that is “widely used” in occupational and 
consumer applications.2  Every year, roughly a million pounds of 1-BP is released into the 
environment, exposing communities near polluting facilities to this dangerous chemical.3  And

2 
 
every year, millions of pounds of 1-BP are produced in the U.S., 4 exposing workers and 
consumers who use 1-BP and products containing 1-BP.  EPA correctly found that 1-BP—“as a 
whole chemical”5—presents unreasonable risk to human health. 
But EPA’s risk evaluation failed to evaluate the full suite of risks posed by 1-BP, and its 
Proposed Rule does not eliminate all of 1-BP’s unreasonable risks, as TSCA requires.6  In its risk 
evaluation, EPA did not determine whether fenceline communities—either as a segment of the 
general population or as a “potentially exposed or susceptible subpopulation” that EPA is 
statutorily mandated to consider7—face unreasonable risks to 1-BP.8  EPA’s proposed risk 
management rule, which prohibits very few 1-BP uses and allows some 97% of the chemical’s 
current production volume to continue without any meaningful protections for fenceline 
communities, compounds that error.  Despite finding cancer risks to fenceline communities in 
excess of unreasonable risk benchmarks, EPA’s Proposed Rule largely ignores those risks and 
does not adopt provisions that would eliminate them.  Instead, EPA inappropriately relies on 
hypothetical future regulation under the Clean Air Act to address risks that dozens of 
communities are experiencing today,9 while ignoring material differences between that statute 
and TSCA and violating TSCA’s independent mandate to eliminate 1-BP’s unreasonable risks. 
Every day, residents of fenceline communities suffer unacceptable risks of cancer 
because of their exposures to 1-BP.  A strong risk management rule that eliminates those risks is 
not only EPA’s best opportunity to protect fenceline communities, but it is also necessary to 
comply with TSCA.  We urge EPA to revise the Proposed Rule as set forth below and to finalize 
a rule that fully eliminates 1-BP’s unreasonable risks, including the unreasonable risks to 
fenceline communities that EPA illegally overlooked in its risk evaluation. 
I. 
TSCA REQUIRES EPA TO ELIMINATE A CHEMICAL’S UNREASONABLE 
RISK 
“[T]he overarching purpose of . . . TSCA is to protect the public from chemicals that pose 
an unreasonable risk to health and the environment.”10  To address the full array of risks from 
chemicals, TSCA authorizes EPA to “look comprehensively” at the hazards of a chemical “in 
 
4 Proposed Rule at 65,070. 
5 EPA, 5. Unreasonable Risk Determination at 19 (Dec. 2022) (“Revised Risk Determination”), 
https://www.epa.gov/system/files/documents/2022-12/1-BP_Final%20Revised%20RD_12-12-
22.pdf. 
6 See 15 U.S.C. § 2605(a) (requiring EPA to regulate a chemical “to the extent necessary so that 
[it] . . . no longer presents [unreasonable] risk”). 
7 Id. § 2605(b)(4)(A). 
8 See EPA, Risk Evaluation for 1-Bromopropane (n-Propyl Bromide) (Aug. 2020) (“2020 Risk 
Evaluation”), https://www.epa.gov/sites/default/files/2020-08/documents/risk_evaluation_for_1-
bromopropane_n-propyl_bromide.pdf. 
9 Proposed Rule at 65,101–02. 
10 Food & Water Watch, Inc. v. EPA, 302 F. Supp. 3d 1058, 1066 (N.D. Cal. 2018); see also S. 
Rep. No. 94-698 at 1 (1976) (“The purpose of [the TSCA bill] is to prevent unreasonable risks of 
injury to health or the environment associated with the manufacture, processing, distribution in 
commerce, use, or disposal of chemical substances.”). 

3 
 
total,”11 considering “the full extent of human or environmental exposure.”12  In 2016, Congress 
overhauled TSCA, adopting new provisions to “enhance[e] EPA’s authority to regulate 
chemicals.”13  As part of the 2016 amendments, Congress adopted a two-step process for 
assessing and regulating existing chemicals:  First, EPA must evaluate a chemical and determine 
whether it poses unreasonable risks to human health or the environment, and second, if EPA 
finds unreasonable risk, then EPA must regulate the chemical to the extent necessary to eliminate 
that risk.14 
 
TSCA requires EPA to conduct risk evaluations on existing chemicals “to determine 
whether a chemical substance presents an unreasonable risk of injury to health or the 
environment.”15  This determination must be made “without consideration of costs or other 
nonrisk factors.”16  And TSCA expressly requires EPA to consider and determine whether the 
chemical poses “an unreasonable risk to a potentially exposed or susceptible subpopulation” 
(“PESS”),17 defined as “a group of individuals within the general population” who, “due to either 
greater susceptibility or greater exposure,” face a “greater risk than the general population of 
adverse health effects from exposure to a chemical substance.”18  As EPA has recognized, 
“potentially exposed or susceptible subpopulation[s]” may include “individuals or communities 
experiencing higher levels of exposure to a chemical substance due to geography (e.g., fenceline 
communities in close proximity to facilities emitting air pollutants . . . )” or due to “aggregate 
exposures via multiple conditions of use (e.g., a worker who lives in close proximity to facilities 
emitting air pollutants).”19  It can also include “communities that due to their proximity to a 
higher proportion of industrial emitters may be experiencing greater burden” and are thus more 
susceptible to harm from chemical exposures.20 
 
Under TSCA, if EPA determines that a chemical presents unreasonable risk, EPA must 
issue a risk management rule that fully eliminates that risk.21  In the 2016 amendments, Congress 
eliminated TSCA’s mandate to address unreasonable risks using the “least burdensome” means, 
which lawmakers said had “paralyzed EPA and prevented [it] from regulating some extremely 
toxic chemicals.”22  In place of the prior directive to minimize regulatory burdens and costs, 
 
11 S. Rep. No. 94-698 at 2 (1976). 
12 H.R. Rep. No. 94-1341 at 6 (1976). 
13 162 Cong. Rec. 7981 (2016) (statement of Sen. Markey). 
14 15 U.S.C. § 2605(a), (b)(4). 
15 Id. § 2605(b)(4)(A). 
16 Id. 
17 Id. 
18 15 U.S.C. § 2602(12). 
19 Procedures for Chemical Risk Evaluation Under the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA), 89 
Fed. Reg. 37,028, 37,039–40 (May 3, 2024). 
20 Id. at 37,040. 
21 See 15 U.S.C. § 2605(a). 
22 162 Cong. Rec. 7498 (2016) (statement of Sen. Markey); see also 162 Cong. Rec. 7984 (2016) 
(explaining that, by “delet[ing] the paralyzing ‘least burdensome’ requirement in the existing law 
and instruct[ing] that EPA’s rule must ensure that the chemical substance or mixture ‘no longer 
 

4 
 
Congress required EPA to regulate a chemical that presents unreasonable risk “to the extent 
necessary so that the chemical substance . . . no longer presents such risk,” using one or more of 
seven broad risk management tools listed in the statute.23  In selecting among risk management 
tools, EPA must consider four criteria: (i) the effects of the chemical on health and the magnitude 
of exposure to human beings; (ii) the effects of the chemical on the environment and the 
magnitude of exposure to the environment; (iii) the benefits of the chemical for various uses; and 
(iv) the “reasonably ascertainable economic consequences of the proposed rule,” including the 
cost effectiveness and costs and benefits of the proposed and final regulatory action and at least 
one alternative regulatory action the Administrator considered.24  TSCA requires EPA to factor 
those considerations into its risk management decisions “to the extent practicable,”25 but it does 
not dictate how EPA is to weigh those factors or direct EPA to adopt a regulatory option where 
benefits outweigh the costs.26  That is, when more than one risk management option would 
satisfy TSCA’s mandate to eliminate a chemical’s unreasonable risk, EPA no longer needs to 
select the lowest cost or least burdensome one.27  Any risk management rule EPA adopts must be 
supported by substantial evidence in the administrative record taken as a whole.28 
II. 
EPA FOUND THAT 1-BP PRESENTS UNREASONABLE RISK, BUT IT 
FAILED TO EVALUATE THE FULL EXTENT OF THAT RISK 
“EPA has determined that 1-BP[,] as a whole chemical substance[,] presents an 
unreasonable risk of injury to health under the conditions of use.”29 Exposure to 1-BP is 
associated with a host of adverse health effects, including developmental, reproductive, liver, 
kidney, and neurotoxicity, as well as cancer.30  As EPA has acknowledged, some of these health 
harms—in particular, developmental toxicity and cancer—are irreversible.31  Despite the known 
toxicity of 1-BP, every year tens of millions of pounds of this chemical are produced32 and nearly 
a million pounds are released to the environment,33 where 1-BP is slow to degrade in the 
 
presents’ the unreasonable risk identified in the risk evaluation,” the amended TSCA “clearly 
rejects the regulatory approach and framework that led to the failed asbestos ban and phase-out 
rule of 1989”). 
23 15 U.S.C. § 2605(a). 
24 Id. § 2605(c)(2)(A).

protective option”).

atmosphere and “can be transported over long distances.”34  As a result, workers, fenceline 
communities, consumers, and the general public are exposed to this harmful chemical.  And 
though EPA has found that 1-BP poses unreasonable risks, it has failed to evaluate the full extent 
of the risks to those facing these harmful exposures. 
In 2020, EPA finalized a risk evaluation under the amended TSCA that evaluated 25 
conditions of use of 1-BP and found that the majority of conditions of use posed unreasonable 
risks to human health.35 This risk evaluation applied EPA’s former approach that determined 
whether individual uses of a chemical—rather than the chemical as a whole—posed 
unreasonable risk.36  In multiple other ways, the 2020 risk evaluation illegally disregarded and 
understated 1-BP’s risks, including, most notably, by: 
• Failing to evaluate risks to the general population from ambient air and disposal 
pathways for any conditions of use37; 
• Assuming the use of personal protective equipment (“PPE”) for worker exposure 
scenarios38; 
• Failing to identify fenceline communities as potentially exposed or susceptible 
subpopulations, and failing to evaluate the risks faced by those groups,39 in 
violation of TSCA’s mandate to evaluate risks to “potentially exposed or 
susceptible subpopulation[s]” who experience “greater exposure” to a chemical 
than the general public40; and 
• Failing to aggregate exposures to 1-BP across exposure routes.41 
EPA also understated 1-BP’s non-cancer risks.  In its risk evaluation and subsequent 
fenceline assessment, EPA selected a chronic point of departure (“POD”) of 6 parts-per-million 
(“ppm”) or higher for the general public, based on EPA’s analysis of developmental risks.42

Dividing that POD by EPA’s total uncertainty factor of 100 would result in a reference 
concentration of 0.06 ppm. 43  In 2023, however, the California Office of Environmental Health 
Hazard Assessment (“OEHHA”) calculated a far lower chronic reference concentration of 0.3 
parts-per-billion (0.0003 ppm), reflecting the “concentration at which adverse noncancer health 
effects would not be expected from continuous chronic exposure to 1-BP.”44  OEHHA calculated 
this toxicity value based on findings of neurotoxicity risks from 1-BP exposure that exceed the 
developmental risks EPA calculated in its risk evaluation.45  EPA’s decision to base its evaluation 
and proposed regulation of 1-BP on a less protective endpoint significantly understates 1-BP’s 
risks and leaves fenceline communities and other exposed populations (including workers) 
facing unacceptable risks.  
In 2022, EPA revised its ultimate risk determination for 1-BP, addressing some—but not 
all—of the flaws in the 2020 risk evaluation.  First, EPA abandoned its illegal use-by-use 
approach to risk evaluations and instead applied a framework that considered whether the 
chemical, as a whole, presents unreasonable risks.46  EPA explained that this “whole chemical 
approach is appropriate for 1-BP because there are benchmark exceedances for multiple 
conditions of use (spanning across most aspects of the chemical lifecycle . . . ) for health and the 
risk of health effects . . . associated with 1-BP exposures are irreversible.”47  Under this corrected 
framework, EPA determined that 1-BP “presents an unreasonable risk of injury to health under 
the conditions of use;”48 this determination was “driven by risks associated with [all but two] 
conditions of use, considered singularly or in combination with other exposures.”49 
Second, EPA stated that it would not rely on assumptions about the use of PPE in making 
its unreasonable risk determination for 1-BP.  EPA acknowledged that “unreasonable risk may 
exist for subpopulations of workers that may be highly exposed” because they are not protected

Finally, EPA announced that it was “conducting a screening approach to assess risks from 
pathways excluded from evaluation for several of the first 10 chemicals,” including 1-BP.51  As 
noted above, the 2020 1-BP risk evaluation did not evaluate the air exposure pathway.52  EPA 
claimed that the “goal” of this screening approach was to “remedy this exclusion and to 
determine if there may be risks that were unaccounted for in the 1-BP risk evaluation.”53  
Evaluating and addressing such risks is necessary to comply with TSCA; as EPA has explained, 
“[t]he approach to exclude certain exposure pathways . . . resulted in a failure to consistently and 
comprehensively address potential exposures to potentially exposed or susceptible 
subpopulations, including fenceline communities (i.e., communities near industrial facilities).”54  
Thus, while EPA did not name fenceline communities as a PESS in its revised risk determination, 
it has recognized that its risk evaluation may have disregarded relevant risks of injury posed by 
1-BP—including to fenceline communities that EPA has acknowledged are a relevant PESS—in 
violation of TSCA.  And indeed, EPA’s subsequent analysis confirmed that it disregarded 
relevant risks:  As explained below, EPA’s assessment found risks exceeding relevant 
benchmarks in the area around dozens of facilities emitting 1-BP. 
III. 
1-BP POSES UNREASONABLE RISK TO FENCELINE COMMUNITIES, 
AND EPA’S PROPOSED RULE FAILS TO ADDRESS THAT RISK 
Although EPA failed to consider the risks to fenceline communities in its initial risk 
evaluation or revised risk determination, EPA “recognizes there is exposure to the general 
population from the ambient air pathway for 1-BP, including fenceline communities.”55  In order 
to “determine if there is the potential for unreasonable risk to fenceline communities associated 
with air . . . exposures,”56 EPA conducted a “screening approach” that considered whether 
emissions of 1-BP posed risks to those in the vicinity of an emitting facility.57  EPA’s analysis 
was incomplete and understated 1-BP exposures and associated risks, but even with its flaws, it 
found that people in the vicinity of dozens of facilities faced risks far exceeding EPA’s 
benchmarks for unreasonable risk.  Instead of proposing risk management measures that would 
address this risk, EPA appears to dismiss the results of its analysis, stating: “While the use of this 
screening approach indicates that EPA is not able to quantify reduced risk or find that there are

no potential risks to fenceline communities, the screening approach was not designed to facilitate 
the making of an unreasonable risk determination for these communities.”58   
EPA’s approach violates TSCA.  EPA is required to eliminate the unreasonable risks 
posed by 1-BP as a whole chemical.  To do so, however, EPA must address all of the 
unreasonable risks that 1-BP presents, even if EPA ignored or understated those risks in its risk 
evaluation.  EPA’s Proposed Rule does not eliminate unreasonable risks to fenceline 
communities, as TSCA requires, and it cannot be finalized as proposed.59  
a. EPA Ignored Relevant Exposures in Calculating Risks to Fenceline 
Communities 
Following its risk evaluation, EPA conducted a fenceline screening analysis to determine 
whether 1-BP posed risks to fenceline communities, ultimately concluding that it does.  
However, that analysis understated the true extent of risks faced by fenceline communities 
because it failed to consider all of their relevant exposures to 1-BP.  EPA undercounted exposures 
by failing to consider combined exposures from multiple facilities or other sources of 1-BP, and 
it irrationally assumed that individuals are exposed to 1-BP for a maximum of 33 years. 
In 2022, EPA released a preliminary evaluation of the risks from 1-BP faced by fenceline 
communities.  EPA used a “screening level methodology” to evaluate the potential exposures and 
risks faced by people near facilities releasing chemicals undergoing risk evaluation to the 
ambient air.60  In it, EPA acknowledged that these fenceline communities—“[m]embers of the 
general population that are in proximity to air emitting facilities”—“may be disproportionately 
exposed to a chemical undergoing risk evaluation under TSCA section (6).”61  EPA preliminarily 
evaluated exposures and risks associated with 1-BP through the ambient airway pathway using 
2019 TRI data and calculating cancer risk with reference to a cancer benchmark of 1-in-
1,000,000, which EPA explained “is consistent with the cancer benchmark used for general 
population cancer risk in several other EPA programs and in previous risk evaluations.62  EPA 
found that risk was indicated for at least one facility for 13 out of 14 occupational exposure 
scenarios (“OES”),63, 64 and for 52 of the 64 real or surrogate facilities evaluated.65  EPA found

that fenceline community exposures were anticipated for 35 of the facilities for which risk was 
indicated and for which EPA had GIS locations,66 concluding that “facilities indicating risk are 
likely of concern to an expected fenceline community cohort.”67  
EPA’s screening analysis did not, however, fully account for fenceline communities’ 
potential exposures and risks.  For 1-BP specifically, EPA omitted major sources of 1-BP to air 
that were reported to the TRI in 2019.68  And as a general matter, EPA unrealistically assumed 
that community members were only exposed to a single source, via a single exposure route, 
without any exposures to other chemicals or non-chemical stressors that increase their 
susceptibility to harm.69  That is, the “screening approach” did not consider the risks that a 
community faces from being exposed to a chemical from multiple sources, including from 
multiple facilities emitting the same chemical.  Indeed, members of EPA’s Science Advisory 
Committee on Chemicals (“SACC”) warned that “the proposed screening methodology was not 
protective because of the lack of consideration for cumulative exposures, multiple source 
exposures, or additional risk factors such as stress, poverty, and/or diet that may interact to affect 
exposures.”70  They noted that a “lack of consideration[] for these factors may underestimate the 
adverse effects from environmental exposures in fenceline communities.”71  And the SACC, as a 
whole, criticized EPA’s use of a single year of release data in its exposure modeling.  The SACC 
recommended that EPA should instead use “[m]aximum or high centile values emission rates 
during the past few years (e.g., five years before the assessment) . . . in models.”72 
EPA addressed one of these flaws in a 2023 fenceline analysis that it conducted 
specifically for 1-BP in ambient air, but it failed to remedy the others.  Following the SACC’s 
recommendation, EPA evaluated risks from 1-BP releases over multiple years to account for the 
variation in emissions from one year to the next, in addition to a single-year analysis that

considered 1-BP emissions from only 2019 TRI data.73  As explained below, EPA’s multi-year 
fenceline analysis showed that communities surrounding many facilities faced increased cancer 
risks far exceeding EPA’s own standard cancer benchmark and the benchmark EPA chose to use 
“for cancer risk in fenceline communities.”74  This result is particularly concerning given that 
EPA found these risks while incorporating unrealistically low exposure assumptions in the 2023 
analysis. 
First, EPA analyzed the risk associated with exposures from a single facility in isolation.  
That is, EPA did not consider the exposures faced by communities that are exposed to 1-BP from 
multiple sources, such as: those who live near multiple facilities that emit 1-BP, those who are 
exposed to 1-BP at work and live near a facility that emits it, or those who live near an emitting 
facility and also use products that contain 1-BP.75  This failure to aggregate exposures violates 
TSCA, which requires EPA to regulate a chemical when there are unreasonable risks posed by 
“the manufacture, processing, distribution in commerce, use, or disposal of a chemical substance 
. . .  or . . . any combination of such activities.”76  It also violates TSCA’s mandate to evaluate 
and regulate chemicals “in a manner consistent with the best available science,”77 which requires 
consideration of exposures from multiple sources.78  And it means that EPA’s assessment of such 
risks does not reflect the real-world experience of fenceline communities. 
Indeed, EPA’s 2023 multi-year fenceline analysis calculated risks from 1-BP exceeding 
EPA’s cancer benchmarks from multiple facilities in close proximity to one another.  For 
example, two such facilities—Exotic Metals Forming Co. and Hytek Finishes Co.—are located 
in Kent, Washington.79  A third facility located in Kent, Protective Coatings Inc., has recently 
released over twenty thousand pounds of 1-BP in a single year.80  Because EPA did not aggregate 
exposures faced by communities in proximity to more than one of these facilities, it necessarily 
understated the risks it calculated to those communities.

Second, EPA failed to evaluate cumulative risks to people who are exposed to 1-BP in 
addition to other chemicals that cause similar health effects.  Communities near facilities that 
emit 1-BP are likely to also be exposed to toxic chemicals from other facilities given that 
“[f]acilities with 1-BP fenceline risks are often close to many other TRI-emitting facilities.”81  
And some of these communities also face higher-than-average National Air Toxics Assessment 
cancer risks.82  Communities that are exposed to multiple other carcinogens are more susceptible 
to harm from carcinogenic 1-BP than communities without those cumulative exposures.83 The

failure to consider co-exposures to other carcinogenic chemicals understates the true risk facing

Third, EPA unrealistically assumes that no one is exposed to 1-BP in the air for more than 
33 years,85 a figure presumably based on the estimate of “residential occupancy period”—the 
amount of time that people will remain in a given residence—in EPA’s Exposure Factors 
Handbook.86  This assumption significantly understates risks to the potentially exposed or 
susceptible subpopulations who spend their entire lives in the same community.  EPA’s 33-year 
estimate also improperly equates the time that someone lives in a given residence with the 
duration of their environmental exposure to a given chemical, as if everyone who moves within 
or from a fenceline community ends up in a pristine environment.  But nearly two-thirds of all 
moves occur within the same county,87 and many people may move within the same municipality 
or neighborhood.  EPA also ignores the racial, socio-economic, and cultural factors that limit 
geographic mobility, including familial or cultural ties to a particular community, as well as the 
effect of environmental contamination on property values, making it harder for many fenceline 
community residents to sell their homes and relocate.88  The SACC has previously advised EPA 
that its assumption of a 33-year lifetime exposure duration is “inadequate for PESS communities 
and arguably invalid for the general population as well.”89

b. Despite its Underinclusive Assessment, EPA Still Calculated Unreasonable 
Risks to Fenceline Communities 
EPA’s multi-year fenceline analysis found that there are dozens of facilities with expected 
exposures of 1-BP to fenceline communities and found severe risk in the areas surrounding many 
facilities.90  The analysis again evaluated the risks to fenceline communities with a reference to 
the standard cancer benchmark of 1-in-1,000,000 increased cancer risk and found that the areas 
surrounding 47 different facilities had risks above this benchmark at distances at least 100 meters 
from the facility.91  The areas around many of these facilities had risks far exceeding the 
benchmark,92 sometimes by orders of magnitude.93  Indeed, multiple facilities had years with 
emissions where the associated lifetime increased cancer risks were above 1-in-10,000.94  And as 
EPA recognized, exposures from many of the facilities with risks exceeding the benchmark were 
expected to reach fenceline communities that are at least one kilometer from the facility.95 
These findings compel a conclusion that 1-BP presents unreasonable risks to fenceline 
communities.  The 1-in-1,000,000 cancer risk benchmark EPA selected in its fenceline 
assessments has been endorsed by the SACC, who recommended that this benchmark “should be 
used in the fenceline assessments.”96  EPA admits that its fenceline analysis “indicates risk to 
fenceline communities,” yet claims that it is “unable to determine” whether these risks 
“contribute to the unreasonable risk” posed by 1-BP.97  But EPA cannot shirk its obligation under 
TSCA to “determine whether a chemical substance presents an unreasonable risk of injury to 
health or the environment . . . including an unreasonable risk to a potentially exposed or 
susceptible subpopulation.”98  And it cannot comply with TSCA’s mandate to regulate 1-BP “to 
the extent necessary so that [it] no longer presents [unreasonable] risk” if it leaves communities 
exposed to elevated cancer risks exceeding EPA’s longstanding unreasonable risk benchmark.99

c. EPA’s Proposed Rule Fails to Address Unreasonable Risks to Fenceline 
Communities 
As proposed, EPA’s rule does not eliminate the unreasonable risks 1-BP poses to 
fenceline communities, and it therefore does not comply with TSCA.100  TSCA requires EPA to 
adopt risk management measures “to the extent necessary so that [a chemical] no longer 
presents” unreasonable risk, including an “unreasonable risk to a potentially exposed or 
susceptible subpopulation.”101  Fenceline communities experience greater exposure to chemicals, 
including 1-BP, than the general population and therefore constitute a potentially exposed or 
susceptible subpopulation that EPA is statutorily mandated to protect.102  Indeed, in its Proposed 
Rule, EPA recognizes that “potentially exposed or susceptible subpopulation[s]” may include 
fenceline communities.103 
EPA appears to acknowledge that, despite this mandate, the risk management measures 
proposed will not eliminate the serious risks facing fenceline communities.104  EPA has not 
proposed emissions limits or other regulations of fenceline community exposures.  Instead, the 
Proposed Rule is almost entirely focused on the regulation of occupational and consumer 
exposures to 1-BP, with the bulk of the proposed measures involving a Workplace Chemical 
Protection Program (“WCPP”) that limits exposures occurring inside a facility.105  EPA does 
propose to require employers self-certify that the exposure controls selected as part of this WCPP 
“do not increase emissions of 1-bromopropane to ambient air outside of the workplace and 
whether additional equipment was installed to capture or otherwise prevent increased emissions 
of 1-bromopropane to ambient air.”106  But this directive to self-certify falls far short of what is 
needed to address the risks faced by fenceline communities.  EPA has not proposed any stack- or 
fenceline-monitoring requirements to enforce this provision, leaving no way to measure

101 Id. § 2605(a), (b)(4)(A).

using 1–BP who are exposed to 1–BP through the ambient air pathway to constitute a subset of

considered PESS.”).  Even if fenceline communities were not a potentially exposed or 
susceptible subpopulation, EPA would still be required to eliminate unreasonable risks facing 
them as a “subset of the general population.” Id.; see also 15 U.S.C. § 2605(a) (requiring EPA to 
regulate chemicals “to the extent necessary” so that they no longer pose unreasonable risk).

employers’ compliance.  Even if facilities did comply, this provision does not: (1) require any 
reductions in existing fenceline community exposures and risks, which already exceed EPA’s 
unreasonable risk benchmark, or (2) prevent 1-BP emissions from increasing for reasons other 
than compliance with workplace exposure limits, such as the projected increase in the 
manufacturing of 1-BP over the coming years. 
 
EPA does propose to prohibit certain conditions of use associated with facilities that emit 
dangerous amounts of 1-BP, and those prohibitions should be finalized.  However, as EPA 
acknowledges in the preamble to its Proposed Rule, these prohibitions affect only 3% of the 
current production volume of 1-BP and only two out of 47 facilities for which EPA found risk to 
fenceline communities.107  EPA cannot rely on this minimal and facially inadequate regulation to 
address the risks that it has found to fenceline communities. 
In addition to violating TSCA, EPA’s failure to address 1-BP’s risks to fenceline 
communities is also inconsistent with EPA’s past statements and current guidance concerning the 
evaluation of fenceline community risks.  As mentioned above, EPA relied on its TSCA 
Screening Level Approach for Assessing Ambient Air and Water Exposures to Fenceline 
Communities Version 1.0 (the “Fenceline Assessment Methodology”) to evaluate 1-BP’s risks to 
impacted communities.108  The purpose of that methodology is to determine whether “there may 
be unreasonable risk to [fenceline] communities” that require either mitigation under TSCA or 
further analysis.109  To make that determination, EPA “use[d] reasonably available data” to 
“evaluate exposures to fenceline communities and characterize risks” from “air and water 
pathways previously not evaluated in published risk evaluations.”110  “The calculated risks were 
then compared to the benchmark values,” including a “[1-in-1,000,000] . . . benchmark for cancer 
risk in fenceline communities.”111  
Where, as here, EPA calculates risk above that benchmark level, the Fenceline Assessment 
Methodology outlines several options for addressing those risks.  According to EPA, those options 
include: (1) issuing a risk management rule that “reduce[s] . . . releases to levels below which an 
unreasonable risk [to fenceline communities] is expected,”112 (2) “referring such risk findings to be 
managed under another EPA administered Federal law” that could eliminate the chemical’s 
unreasonable risks,113 or (3)  “undertak[ing] additional analysis” and “supplement[ing] the 
published risk evaluation” to refine its risk calculations and confirm whether the risks are 
unreasonable.114  Under no circumstance, however, does the Fenceline Assessment Methodology

permit EPA to leave fenceline communities exposed to elevated risks that EPA has neither further

Yet that is precisely what EPA has done in the proposed 1-BP rule.  As described above, 
EPA calculated extensive fenceline risks above its cancer risk benchmarks, triggering the need for 
mitigation or a supplemental risk evaluation under EPA’s Fenceline Assessment Methodology.115  
But EPA left the vast majority of those risks unaddressed,116 with no mandatory reductions in 1-BP 
releases, no supplemental analysis,117 and no finding that any other laws would eliminate the risks 
identified by EPA.118  In addition to violating TSCA, EPA’s “‘[u]nexplained inconsistency’ in 
agency policy” renders the proposed 1-BP rule arbitrary and capricious.119  
d. EPA Cannot Rely on Future Clean Air Act Rulemakings in Lieu of Issuing a 
TSCA Risk Management Rule to Eliminate Risk to Fenceline Communities 
EPA cannot rely on the potential for future regulation of 1-BP under the Clean Air Act to 
satisfy its obligations in this rulemaking to eliminate unreasonable risk faced by fenceline 
communities.  EPA states that it “expects that this proposed action, in combination with the 
emissions standards resulting from anticipated revisions to [National Emissions Standards for 
Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP)] requirements following the designation of 1–BP as a 
[hazardous air pollutant], would reduce risk sufficiently to the general population and fenceline 
communities.”120  Accordingly, EPA “seek[s] comment on its conclusions, and whether, 
consistent with TSCA section 9(b), any other statutory authorities administered by EPA should 
be used to take additional regulatory action identified as necessary to protect against such 
risk.”121 
EPA cannot rely on future Clean Air Act rulemakings to avoid its duties here.  First, EPA 
has not made the requisite determinations under section 9(b) that would allow it to use the Clean 
Air Act to address the risk it has found.  Second, any such determinations would be contrary to 
TSCA, arbitrary, and unsupported by substantial evidence.  Finally, relying on the mere prospect 
of future discretionary rulemakings would violate section 9(b)’s mandate that EPA shall take 
action under another statute to address risk.

considering, EPA will conduct a more comprehensive exposure assessment of fenceline 
communities and supplement the risk evaluation for that chemical with the new information.”).

1. EPA has not made the requisite 9(b) determinations 
Section 9(b) of TSCA authorizes EPA to “coordinate” its actions under TSCA, including 
risk management, “with actions taken under other Federal laws administered in whole or in part 
by the Administrator.”122  EPA must employ TSCA unless EPA determines under section 9(b) 
that the unreasonable risk associated with a chemical “could be eliminated or reduced to a 
sufficient extent by actions taken” under another environmental statute.123  “Sufficient” here 
means that the actions taken under another act accomplish TSCA’s mandate to eliminate 
unreasonable risk.  Additionally, as TSCA requires compliance deadlines for risk management 
rules that eliminate unreasonable risk “as soon as practicable but not later than 5 years after the 
date of promulgation of the rule,”124 an action taken under another statute must similarly 
eliminate the unreasonable risk within the same timeframes to be “sufficient.”  Even if EPA 
makes the formal determination that another EPA-administered statute “could” eliminate the 
unreasonable risk posed by 1-BP, EPA must nonetheless use TSCA to manage risks where “it is 
in the public interest to protect against such risks” by acting under TSCA.125  Thus, even if 
another statute, such as the Clean Air Act, “could” be used to sufficiently regulate the risks posed 
by 1-BP, EPA must also ascertain whether the public interest nonetheless requires EPA to 
regulate under TSCA. 
Here, EPA has not made the requisite determinations that future Clean Air Act regulation 
“could” sufficiently eliminate unreasonable risk and that it is not in the public interest to use 
TSCA, let alone supported such conclusions with substantial evidence.  Instead, the Proposed 
Rule simply contains EPA’s conjecture that it “expects” that Clean Air Act rulemakings can 
reduce risk.126  There is no evidence to support those conclusory statements, let alone substantial 
evidence.  Because EPA has not made the requisite section 9(b) determinations, EPA cannot rely 
on the prospect of future Clean Air Act regulations to avoid promulgating TSCA risk 
management regulations here. 
2. Future Clean Air Act rules do not lawfully meet the section 9(b)’s 
requirements 
Moreover, not only has EPA failed to make the requisite section 9(b) determinations, it 
could not rationally and lawfully do so on the basis of future Clean Air Act actions.  Here, two 
factors confirm that future actions under the Clean Air Act are not sufficient or in the public 
interest: (1) the numerous substantive differences between section 112 of the Clean Air Act and 
section 6 of TSCA; and (2) the practical realities of EPA’s implementation of section 112. 
Additionally, TSCA section 9(b) mandates that if EPA makes the predicate finding to use 
another EPA-administered statute to eliminate unreasonable risk, EPA “shall use such authorities 
to protect against such risk.”127  Thus, the statute imposes a mandatory obligation on EPA to use 
 
122 15 U.S.C. § 2608(b)(1). 
123 Id. 
124 15 U.S.C. § 2605(d)(1)(A). 
125 15 U.S.C. § 2608(b)(1). 
126 Proposed Rule at 65,103. 
127 15 U.S.C. § 2608(b)(1).

those other authorities and to do so in a manner that will sufficiently protect against risk.  EPA 
cannot rely on future, discretionary actions that, even if taken, are not required to eliminate the 
unreasonable risk EPA found under TSCA.  
First, the legal requirements for standard-setting under TSCA and the Clean Air Act 
differ in two key ways, meaning it is likely that future Clean Air Act standards issued will not be 
“sufficient” to eliminate unreasonable risk.  
1.  
Different Risk Benchmarks: Under EPA’s Clean Air Act framework, the level of risk 
that typically triggers regulation is less stringent than that which EPA has used under TSCA.  
Given those differences and EPA’s fenceline risk estimates for 1-BP, future Clean Air Act 
regulations are unlikely to eliminate the unreasonable risk posed by 1-BP. 
In its TSCA risk evaluation for 1-BP, EPA used a 1-in-1,000,000 cancer risk benchmark 
to identify unreasonable risks to consumers or the general population.128  In its Fenceline 
Assessment Methodology and subsequent multi-year analysis, EPA reaffirmed the use of that 
benchmark for fenceline communities under TSCA.129  
But EPA has not taken as stringent an approach under the Clean Air Act.  While EPA 
recognizes that the statute requires the Agency to conduct a rulemaking if cancer risk is above 1-
in-1,000,000, it treats this level as “an aspirational goal” to achieve.130  Instead, in the course of 
such a rulemaking, “EPA will generally presume that if the risk to [the maximum exposed] 
individual is no higher than approximately one in 10 thousand [i.e., 100-in-1,000,000], that risk 
level is considered acceptable.”131  That presumptive benchmark is 100 times less stringent than 
what EPA has used under TSCA.  Consequently, communities cannot count on EPA’s 
administration of Clean Air Act section 112(f) for assuring as much protection as TSCA would 
provide, because standards that EPA might deem legally sufficient for Clean Air Act purposes 
would not be sufficient to ensure that a chemical “no longer presents [unreasonable] risk” under 
the TSCA framework EPA has adopted.132

in the general population (e.g., fenceline communities).”).

In addition, there are prominent examples where EPA has refused to issue Clean Air Act 
rules to bring risk below even the 100-in-1,000,000 benchmark and has left communities subject 
to cancer risk above that extreme level.  This is because EPA has sometimes treated this 
benchmark as more discretionary than binding in its administration of section 112.133  For 
example, in a 2005 rulemaking for Coke Oven Batteries, EPA found that the maximum 
individual cancer risk was 300-in-1,000,000 but nonetheless found that level of risk to be 
acceptable.134  The final standards EPA adopted for that source category only reduced cancer risk 
to 270-in-1,000,000, nearly three times the Clean Air Act benchmark.135 And in a 2020 
rulemaking for Miscellaneous Organic Chemical Manufacturing, EPA applied the benchmark to 
find that the cancer risk due to allowable emissions was 800-in-1,000,000 and thus unacceptable, 
but it issued rules that still left some community members exposed to a cancer risk of 200-in-
1,000,000.136 Communities have been calling on EPA to reduce the unacceptability benchmark 
used under the Clean Air Act because it is outdated and does not protect children or fenceline 
communities exposed to multiple types of sources simultaneously, but so far EPA has not 
changed or updated this benchmark. 
 
For 1-BP, EPA’s fenceline analysis finds that facilities in a number of different industries 
exceed TSCA’s unreasonable risk benchmark of 1-in-1,000,000 but fall below EPA’s Clean Air 
Act benchmark of 100-in-1,000,000 for regulation under section 112(f).  Indeed, EPA found that 
only a handful of facilities had risks exceeding the 100-in-1,000,000 Clean Air Act 
benchmark.137  For the dozens of remaining facilities that fall under the 100-in-1,000,000 risk 
level but exceed TSCA’s 1-in-1,000,000 risk benchmark,138 EPA is unlikely to regulate the risks

2. 
Discretionary Standard Setting for Area Sources: Additionally, as interpreted by EPA, 
setting risk-based standards for area sources is largely discretionary under the Clean Air Act, 140 
and the Agency has largely declined to set risk-based standards for area sources.  Thus, for any 
facility where EPA found that the risk posed by 1-BP exceeds the benchmark for community 
risks but that is classified as an area source under the Clean Air Act, future section 112 
rulemakings are unlikely to sufficiently reduce the risk. 
For example, in setting standards for area source dry cleaners, EPA refused to reduce 
risks that significantly exceed the 100-in-1,000,000 benchmark.  In a 2006 rulemaking for this 
source category, the record demonstrated that the maximum individual cancer risk for ordinary 
area source dry cleaners was 220-in-1,000,000, and that for those dry cleaners co-located with 
other commercial businesses, the risk was 1,000-in-1,000,000.141  Despite these extremely high 
cancer risks, EPA declined to set standards in the 2006 rulemaking, and following a voluntary 
remand in which EPA promised to expeditiously reconsider this decision,142 it has recently 
reaffirmed that it does not intend to set standards designed to reduce these risks.143 
The vast majority of facilities which EPA’s fenceline analysis identifies as presenting 
unreasonable risk to the communities at the fenceline are area sources under the Clean Air Act. 
EPA’s ECHO database only identifies as major sources 14 of the facilities that EPA found to 
present unreasonable risk.144  In future Clean Air Act rulemakings for the facilities that are area

it is unlikely to do so, given its past practices of failing to conduct risk reviews or address risks 
posed by area sources. Thus, the evidence demonstrates that EPA cannot rely on the prospect of 
future Clean Air Act rulemakings to address the risks posed by dozens of area sources. 
Second, beyond the legal differences between the Clean Air Act and TSCA, the practical 
realities of Clean Air Act implementation establish that potential future NESHAP rulemakings 
are insufficient to address the risk 1-BP poses to fenceline communities.  EPA’s claim in this 
proposal that it can address risks through future NESHAP rulemakings is further undermined by 
the fact that EPA may never again conduct another Clean Air Act risk review for many of the 
sources that EPA found present unreasonable risk.  And even if EPA does conduct such reviews, 
they are certain to be so delayed that future EPA action will not sufficiently reduce the risks as 
required by section 9(b).  Given these practical and legal realities, it is plainly in the public 
interest to regulate the risks to fenceline communities under TSCA. 
1.  
EPA unlawfully refuses to do section 112(f) risk reviews for source categories where it 
has previously completed a risk review, and many of the sources that present risk to fenceline 
communities have already been subject to a section 112(f) risk review. Therefore, it is entirely 
speculative that EPA will conduct additional section 112(f) reviews for those sources, let alone 
that such reviews would lead to regulations that eliminate the unreasonable risk presented.  
In a recent Clean Air Act rulemaking for coke ovens, EPA refused to do a new risk 
review for the source category, taking the position that it only has an obligation to conduct one 
risk review per source category and it had previously conducted a risk review for the source 
category in question.145  While this position is unlawful, if applied here, it would mean EPA 
would not issue health protective standards under section 112(f) for many of the sources of 1-BP

145 EPA, Summary of Public Comments and Responses for Coke Ovens: Pushing, Quenching, 
and Battery Stacks Residual Risk and Technology Review, and Coke Oven Batteries Periodic 
Technology Review, Docket No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2002-0085 at 72 (May 2024), 
https://www.regulations.gov/document/EPA-HQ-OAR-2002-0085-1603 (click “Download”).  

22 
 
that EPA has concluded present risk to fenceline communities.  For example, EPA found that 
dozens of vapor degreasing facilities present significant risk to fenceline communities.146  But 
EPA has already completed a section 112(f) risk review for the source category that covers these 
vapor degreasing facilities.147  Thus, EPA’s position in the Proposed Rule—that a Clean Air Act 
rulemaking to address risks posed by vapor degreasing is “required” in the future148 —is contrary 
to the position it takes in administering the Clean Air Act.  
2. 
Even if rules under section 112 of the Clean Air Act could eliminate unreasonable risk 
posed by 1-BP, EPA will not complete the necessary rulemakings in time to “sufficient[ly]” 
eliminate the risk.149  As used in section 9(b), “sufficient” means that EPA must use its other 
statutory authorities to eliminate the unreasonable risk in the timeframes mandated by section 
6(a).  For risk management measures short of a ban, those timeframes require EPA to establish 
effective dates that are “as soon as practicable” and compliance dates that “as soon as 
practicable, but not later than 5 years after the date of promulgation of the [risk management] 
rule.”150  
Yet, EPA’s administration of the section 112 program demonstrates that EPA will not, 
consistent with TSCA’s section 6(d) timeframes, complete section 112(f) rulemakings and set 
compliance deadlines for all of the source categories for which EPA found unreasonable risk.  
Under Clean Air Act section 112, EPA must complete periodic reviews of source category 
standards on eight-year cycles.151  But EPA consistently fails to meet the deadlines.  To take 
EPA’s example of the vapor degreasing NESHAP, EPA last set standards for the Halogenated 
Solvent Cleaning source category in 2007, meaning that EPA was required to complete a review 
of the source category by 2015.152  That review is already nine years overdue, and there is no 
indication that EPA has even begun the rulemaking, which will be a multi-year process to 
complete.  Section 112 rulemakings routinely languish for years; indeed, commenters are 
unaware of any recent section 112 review that EPA has completed in the timeframe mandated by 
the Clean Air Act.  Instead, in order to get EPA to complete section 112 rules, EPA typically has 
to be sued by community groups and be placed on a court-ordered deadline.153 Given that there 
are many types of facilities that exceed the TSCA risk benchmark for 1-BP, EPA would have to

23 
 
complete Clean Air Act rulemakings for multiple source categories to address the risk.  EPA 
lacks substantial evidence that it could complete such rulemakings in a sufficiently timely 
manner; indeed, the evidence demonstrates EPA is unlikely to do so. 
 
There is a significant mismatch between EPA’s regulatory authority under TSCA and its 
administration of section 112 of the Clean Air Act, which precludes EPA from determining that 
future Clean Air Act rulemakings are sufficient to mitigate the risk posed by 1-BP or in the 
public interest.  This mismatch means that in circumstances where TSCA requires regulation to 
fully eliminate unreasonable risk, the prospect of future regulations under the Clean Air Act are 
uncertain at best and likely to be significantly less stringent than what TSCA requires; indeed, 
this mismatch may mean that EPA never regulates 1-BP emissions to protect fenceline 
communities under the Clean Air Act.  Thus, the Clean Air Act is insufficient to reduce the risks 
posed by 1-BP and, alternatively, it is in the public interest to regulate 1-BP under TSCA to 
eliminate the risks to fenceline communities. 
Finally, irrespective of whether actions under the Clean Air Act are sufficient and in the 
public interest for purposes of section 9(b), simply pointing to the possibility that EPA may take 
action someday under the Clean Air Act does not fulfill TSCA’s mandate that EPA “shall use” 
its other authorities to protect against unreasonable risk.154 Take EPA’s example of future Clean 
Air Act regulation of vapor degreasing.  There is no commitment or guarantee that EPA will ever 
conduct another section 112(f)(2) review for the Halogenated Solvent Cleaning source category 
because EPA believes it is under no legal obligation to do so.  Even if EPA did such a review, 
there is no guarantee or commitment that EPA would regulate the source category to reduce the 
risks for either major sources or area sources below TSCA’s unreasonable risk benchmark 
because EPA believes it is under no legal obligation to do so.  Finally, given that the standard for 
the source category is already years overdue and that EPA routinely delays standard setting, 
there is no guarantee or commitment as to when EPA would even undertake such a rulemaking 
let alone that it would do so sufficiently quickly.  This is just one example, and it applies with 
equal force to the many industries that EPA found pose unreasonable risk to fenceline 
communities.  EPA cannot rely on the mere possibility of future actions that EPA believes to be 
discretionary to satisfy section 9(b)’s mandate to take action to protect against unreasonable risk. 
IV. 
EPA MUST REVISE ITS PROPOSAL TO ENSURE IT ELIMINATES 
UNREASONABLE RISKS 
The Proposed Rule violates TSCA’s core requirement to regulate 1-BP “to the extent 
necessary so that the chemical substance . . . no longer presents [unreasonable] risk” to human 
health.155  The rule fails to protect the health of communities who, according to EPA’s own 
assessment, experience unacceptable cancer risks from their exposures to 1-BP.  And it would 
permit 97% of 1-BP production and use to continue, despite finding that 1-BP, as a whole 
chemical, poses unreasonable risk. 
 
154 15 U.S.C. § 2608(b)(1). 
155 15 U.S.C. § 2605(a). 

24 
 
TSCA authorizes, and in this case requires, a fundamentally different risk management 
approach.  Instead of permitting all active uses of 1-BP to continue in perpetuity, without 
meaningful limits on environmental releases or fenceline community exposures, EPA should 
phase out as many uses of 1-BP as possible.  To the extent there are critical uses that cannot be 
replaced in the near term, EPA can extend the phase-out period or grant time-limited exceptions 
pursuant to TSCA section 6(g).156  And for as long as 1-BP is permitted to be used, EPA must 
require fenceline monitoring at facilities emitting 1-BP, as well as corrective action should 
monitoring show higher-than-acceptable emissions.  This approach would result in a more 
health-protective, equitable, and legally defensible rule. 
a. EPA Should Phase out Ongoing Uses of 1-BP to Address Risks Faced by 
Fenceline Communities 
EPA has broad authority to regulate 1-BP based on its 2020 risk evaluation and 2022 
revised risk determination, which found unreasonable risk from 1-BP as a “whole chemical.”157  
As EPA explained in its revised risk determination, “under TSCA section 6(a), EPA is not limited 
to regulating the specific activities found to drive unreasonable risk and may select from among 
a suite of risk management requirements in section 6(a).”158  A total prohibition on the uses of 1-
BP—or a near-total prohibition, as EPA considered as a second alternative regulatory action159—
would best comply with TSCA’s mandate to eliminate unreasonable risks in that it would address 
the risks faced by fenceline communities.  To the extent that such a prohibition would phase out 
“critical” uses of 1-BP, EPA could adopt time-limited exemptions for those uses for which there 
are no feasible safer alternatives.160 
Having found unreasonable risks from 1-BP as a whole, EPA “shall consider” the factors 
enumerated in TSCA section 6(c)(2) when deciding how to manage those risks.161  Those 
statutory considerations include “the effects of the chemical substance . . . on health,” “the 
magnitude of the exposure of human beings to the chemical substances,” “the likely effect of the 
rule on . . . public health,” and “the costs and benefits of the proposed and final regulatory action 
and of the 1 or more primary alternative regulatory action.”162  TSCA does not permit EPA to 
exclude any reasonably available information about the magnitude of a chemical’s exposures or 
its effects on human health and the environment from the section 6(c)(2) analysis merely because 
the information was not quantified in EPA’s risk evaluation.  To the contrary, EPA has an 
independent obligation to consider those 6(c)(2) factors during the risk management process, 
including regulatory costs, benefits, and other “nonrisk factors” that EPA is precluded from 
considering during the risk evaluation process.163  And where there is insufficient information to 
 
156 Id. § 2605(g). 
157 Revised Risk Determination at 19. 
158 Id. at 2. 
159 See Proposed Rule at 65,094–95. 
160 See 15 U.S.C. § 2605(g). 
161 Id. § 2605(c)(2)(A). 
162 Id. 
163 See id. § 2605(b)(4)(A) 

25 
 
conduct quantitative analyses of those factors, TSCA section 6(c)(2) allows EPA to consider and 
rely on qualitative assessments as well.164   
When conducting the required section 6(c)(2) analysis for the Proposed Rule, therefore, 
EPA must consider not only the exposures and effects that it quantified in the 1-BP risk 
evaluation but also those that it unlawfully excluded from that evaluation.  Thus, EPA’s 6(c)(2) 
analysis must account for 1-BP’s adverse effects on fenceline communities, as well as the 
neurological risks identified by OEHHA that EPA understated in its risk evaluation and failed to 
adequately address in its Proposed Rule.165  EPA’s analysis of “effects of the chemical substance 
. . . on health” and “the magnitude of . . . exposure” must meaningfully include impacts on 
fenceline communities,166 and EPA’s consideration of the “the costs and benefits” of different 
regulatory options must consider the benefits of 1-BP reductions for fenceline communities.167  
While EPA alluded to the risks faced by members of fenceline communities in its discussion of 
the factors it analyzed under TSCA section 6(c)(2),168 it appears to have considered these 
individuals only “as a factor when proposing to prohibit increased releases of 1-BP to outdoor 
air” that result from a facility’s workplace controls.169  Moreover, EPA’s Economic Analysis 
omits from consideration benefits to fenceline communities from reductions in 1-BP 
exposures.170  EPA’s limited consideration of the risks faced by fenceline communities for only 
one regulatory option is insufficient under TSCA, and EPA must consider how the second 
alternative regulatory option would benefit fenceline communities. 
 
EPA can remedy this flaw in its final rule.  TSCA requires EPA to “factor in” the section 
6(c)(2) considerations “to the extent practicable” when “selecting among prohibitions and other 
restrictions,”171 but it does not specify how EPA must weigh those considerations.  Based on its 
existing unreasonable risk determination, EPA is free to determine that a broad phase-out is the 
best way of eliminating 1-BP’s unreasonable risks and recognize that it would have the added 
benefit of addressing the risks faced by fenceline communities.  Unlike the Proposed Rule, that 
approach would eliminate the unreasonable risks that EPA identified in its risk evaluation, while 
also rendering the gaps and deficiencies in that evaluation harmless. 
In addition to satisfying TSCA’s obligations, a broader phase-out of 1-BP is consistent 
with EPA’s longstanding policy objectives.  As the U.S. Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit 
 
164 Lab. Council for Latin Am. Advancement v. EPA, 12 F.4th 234, 250 (2d Cir. 2021) (upholding 
EPA ban on consumer uses of methylene chloride paint strippers based in part on EPA’s 
“qualitative assessments of the costs to retailers, distributors, and commercial end users” and 
stating that such assessments were “consistent with EPA’s obligation to consider ‘reasonably 
available information’”).

167 Id. § 2605(c)(2)(A)(iv)(II). 
168 Proposed Rule at 65,101–03. 
169 Proposed Rule at 65,098; see also id. at 65,103.  This risk-management option is insufficient 
to protect fenceline communities for the reasons explained in Part III, supra. 
170 See Economic Analysis at 8-1–8-22. 
171 15 U.S.C. § 2605(c)(2)(B).

recognized in upholding EPA’s 2019 ban of consumer uses of methylene chloride paint strippers, 
it is reasonable to expect that chemical phase-outs will promote the development and deployment 
of safer alternatives by “creat[ing] new business via alternative products and an evolving 
marketspace.”172  Upstream prohibitions are also “relatively easy to enforce because key 
requirements are directly placed on a small number of” manufacturers and suppliers, which 
“minimizes to the greatest extent the potential for [the chemical] to be intentionally or 
unintentionally misdirected into the prohibited uses.”173  EPA’s pollution prevention policies 
“make[] clear” that source reduction is EPA’s “preferred” strategy for reducing chemical risks.174  
Similarly, the hierarchy of controls that EPA endorses in the Proposed Rule prioritizes 
“elimination” and “substitution” as the most effective means of addressing occupational risks,175 
and these controls would have the additional co-benefit of reducing exposures to other groups, 
like fenceline communities. 
Industry has argued that prohibitions are not necessary to address uses of chemicals that 
can be managed through other, less restrictive means and are thus barred by TSCA’s requirement 
to regulate chemicals “to the extent necessary so that . . . [it] no longer presents [unreasonable] 
risk.”176  This argument misstates the facts and the law.  As explained above, a prohibition of 1-
BP would eliminate the chemical’s unreasonable risks to human health and the environment, 
while the Proposed Rule would not.  If any option is barred by section 6(a), it is the one that 
leaves unreasonable risks unaddressed. 
Industry’s interpretation of TSCA section 6(a) has been rejected by the sole court to 
consider it.  In 2019, the Halogenated Solvents Industry Alliance challenged EPA’s risk 
management rule banning consumer uses of methylene chloride paint strippers, claiming that 
rule was too broad, and thus in excess of what was necessary to protect consumers, because it 
swept in some commercial uses for which EPA had not made a finding of unreasonable risk.  The 
Second Circuit upheld EPA’s ban, explaining that the relevant inquiry under TSCA section 6(a) 
was not whether the rule impacted or restricted other uses but rather whether it was “a reasonable 
means, supported by substantial evidence, to ensure that the unreasonable risks of methylene

chloride paint removal products for consumer uses be ‘no longer present[ed].’”177  That is the 
appropriate standard for a risk management rule, and it is plainly satisfied by a prohibition of 
non-essential 1-BP uses.178 
b. EPA Should Require Fenceline Monitoring and Corrective Action  
Because the record demonstrates that there is unreasonable risk to fenceline communities 
from air emissions, EPA must issue a risk management rule that eliminates that risk.179 One 
option EPA must evaluate and should adopt is a fenceline monitoring and corrective action 
standard for any uses of 1-BP that cannot be phased out. 
Under such a standard, EPA would set an action level for the concentration of the 1-BP in 
the ambient air at which the chemical presents unreasonable risk to nearby communities.  
Facilities would then be required to set up air monitoring devices to measure ambient levels of 1-
BP at the perimeter of the facility—i.e., at the fenceline.  If the monitored levels of the 1-BP 
exceed the action level, the facility operator would be required to develop and implement a 
corrective action plan to reduce 1-BP emissions until the monitored levels of the pollutant come 
below the action level—i.e., until the unreasonable risk is eliminated. 
Such a standard is authorized under section 6(a).  Under its authority to “regulat[e] any 
manner or method of commercial use of such substance or mixture,”180 EPA may establish a 
fenceline monitoring and corrective action standard, just as it has utilized that authority to 
establish a WCPP, relying on an Existing Chemical Exposure Limit (“ECEL”).181  Additionally, 
EPA may establish such a standard utilizing its authority to “restrict[] the manufacturing[] [or] 
processing” of a chemical substance,182 or to require manufacturers and processors to “monitor 
or conduct tests which are reasonable and necessary to assure compliance” with other risk 
management measures.183  
Such a standard can be implemented here. EPA has already derived inhalation hazard 
values for 1-BP for both cancer and non-cancer endpoints.184 Based on those values, it can 
establish the exposure levels at which the chemical presents unreasonable risk for fenceline

communities.  And there are readily applicable monitoring technologies.  As EPA recognizes, 1-
BP air concentrations can be accurately monitored at very low concentrations of less than 0.0005 
ppm.185  
Thus, EPA can use validated monitoring methods such as TO-17 to conduct fenceline 
monitoring, as EPA itself recognized in this rulemaking.186 In addition, there are other air 
monitoring methods—such as TO-15, TO-15a, and Method 320—that are used for ambient 
monitoring of other volatile organic compounds (“VOCs”) that EPA could readily validate and 
then apply to 1-BP.187  Finally, there are commercial monitors that are used to detect and 
speciate VOCs that could detect 1-BP in the ambient air.188 
Failure to consider a fenceline monitoring standard would be arbitrary in light of EPA’s 
commitment to implementing Next Generation Compliance methods and its successful use of 
fenceline monitoring to control ambient air pollution at the fenceline in other contexts. Over a 
decade ago, EPA established the Next Generation Compliance initiative to identify techniques to 
“revolutionize environmental protection.”189 One such technique was fenceline monitoring.190 
EPA recognized that there is an ongoing “revolution” in the use of monitoring to reduce the 
emissions of toxic air pollutants because “[m]onitoring devices are becoming more accurate,

more mobile, and cheaper.”191  Fenceline monitoring “help[s] [companies] quickly identify and 
fix problems, saving money[] [and] reducing  pollution.”192  Additionally, it lets companies and 
communities know about pollution problems early, so that “prompt action can be taken to fix 
problems before they become serious health concerns.”193  
EPA has begun to successfully implement fenceline monitoring in a number of rules to 
control emissions to ambient air at the community fenceline.  In 2015, EPA established a 
fenceline monitoring and corrective action standard for the Petroleum Refinery categories, the 
first-of-its-kind implemented under section 7412(d)(6) of the Clean Air Act.194 As EPA 
recognized in that rulemaking, fenceline monitoring would enable quicker and more effective 
reductions of fugitive emissions.195  That fenceline monitoring standard has been a major 
success, significantly reducing ambient fenceline concentrations.196  Since that rulemaking, EPA 
has also required fenceline monitoring and corrective action standards in a number of other 
industries in order to protect fenceline communities from toxic air pollution.197  
Thus, EPA recognizes that fenceline monitoring and corrective action are critically 
important tools for protecting fenceline communities from toxic air pollution.  It would be 
arbitrary and contrary to the substantial evidence that fenceline monitoring can address risks to 
communities from ambient air emissions for EPA not to adopt fenceline monitoring and 
corrective action as a risk management measure to control 1-BP emissions. 
Indeed, deploying fenceline monitors is all the more important in light of the fact that 
EPA has likely underestimated the emissions and ambient fenceline concentrations of 1-BP, and 
thus underestimated the risk these facilities pose.  In estimating risk posed by 1-BP, EPA 
primarily relied on TRI data, which in turn is based on self-reported emissions estimates by 1-BP
