emitters, rather than monitoring data.198  Requiring fenceline monitoring has the potential to 
manage emissions and risks that EPA overlooked. 
As EPA has long recognized, monitoring often shows that companies “greatly 
underestimate[] their pollution, sometimes by an order of magnitude.”199  Thus, having “[a]ctual 
measurements, as opposed to estimates, often show far higher emissions than [EPA], or the 
company, thought.”200  Given that the vast majority of EPA’s estimates of 1-BP emissions are 
not based on monitors, there’s a significant likelihood that EPA’s current estimates of emissions 
and risks are too low.  Accordingly, requiring fenceline monitoring has the potential to be a 
particularly effective risk management technique here. 
Finally, even if EPA chooses other risk management measures to eliminate the risk 1-BP 
poses to fenceline communities, so long as EPA continues to allow the manufacture, processing, 
and commercial use of 1-BP, it should nonetheless require fenceline monitoring and corrective 
action as a complementary risk management measure.  As EPA has itself recognized, fenceline 
monitoring is an important tool for ensuring compliance with standards that are designed to 
reduce emissions.  In particular, fenceline monitoring enables companies to “identify ‘poor-
performing’ sources … using the fenceline monitoring data” and thereby take corrective action 
“before they would have otherwise been aware of the issue through existing inspection and 
enforcement measures.”201  
V. 
EPA SHOULD NOT INCLUDE A DE MINIMIS EXCEPTION TO ITS 1-BP 
PROHIBITIONS 
EPA solicits comments on “whether it should consider a de minimis level of 1–BP in 
formulations for certain continuing industrial and commercial uses to account for impurities 
(e.g., 0.1% or 0.5%) when finalizing these prohibitions, and, if so, what level should be 
considered de minimis.”202  EPA should not include any de minimis exception, which would be 
unsupported by the existing record and contrary to EPA’s obligations under TSCA. 
As described above, EPA’s overarching mandate under TSCA is to eliminate chemicals’ 
unreasonable risks.203  It makes no difference whether those risks are associated with the 
presence of the chemical as an impurity, a byproduct, or an intentional ingredient in a product or 
 
198 The TRI Form Rs show that only three of the facilities for which EPA found unreasonable 
risk—Mahle Engine Components, Elgiloy Specialty Metals, and Flint Hills Resources Corpus 
Christie West Plant—reported emissions based on monitored data. The remaining facilities relied 
on emissions factors, mass balance equations, or other emissions estimation methods.  
199 Cynthia Giles, Next Generation Compliance, The Environmental Forum at 24. 
200 Id. 
201 National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants for Coke Ovens: Pushing, 
Quenching, and Battery Stacks, and Coke Oven Batteries; Residual Risk and Technology 
Review, and Periodic Technology Review, 88 Fed. Reg. 55,858, 55,888 (Aug. 16, 2023); see also 
Cynthia Giles, Next Generation Compliance, The Environmental Forum at 24. 
202 Proposed Rule at 65,081. 
203 15 U.S.C. § 2605(a). 

31 
 
process;204 EPA “shall” regulate the chemical “to the extent necessary so that [it] no longer 
presents such risk.”205  Neither the 1-BP risk evaluation nor the risk management rule identify a 
threshold below which the presence of 1-BP in a product presents no unreasonable risk.  To the 
contrary, EPA found that 1-BP is highly toxic at low exposure levels and explained that “a single 
exposure during a critical window of development” can result in serious developmental harm.206  
The Proposed Rule floats two potential de minimis thresholds: 0.1% and 0.5%.207  To the best of 
our knowledge, EPA has not calculated the risks associated with 1-BP exposures at either of 
those levels. 
Moreover, even if EPA could identify a concentration at which the presence of 1-BP in a 
given product presents no unreasonable risk, a de minimis threshold would still be unlawful.  
When evaluating risks under TSCA, EPA must consider known, intended, and reasonably 
foreseen combinations of exposures from multiple products, pathways, and routes.208  Therefore, 
even if, say, EPA calculated no unreasonable risk from spot cleaners and stain removers 
containing 0.1% or less 1-BP, those exposures could still contribute to unreasonable risk when 
combined with 1-BP in other products (such as liquid or aerosol cleaners), in the environment, or 
in an occupational setting.  The Proposed Rule includes an ECEL of 0.5 ppm over an eight-hour 
time weighted average, which was calculated to keep workers’ cancer risks from 1-BP exposure 
risks below 1-in-10,000.209  But EPA did not account for any exposures workers face outside of 
the workplace when setting the ECEL.  Therefore, any additional exposure to 1-BP, even at so-
called “de minimis” levels, could leave workers exposed to unreasonable risk, in violation of 
TSCA. 
 
The de minimis thresholds included in prior risk management rules reinforce our 
concerns.  In its methylene chloride risk management rule, EPA allowed the continued 
production, sale, and use of otherwise prohibited products containing up to 0.1% methylene 
chloride by weight.210  EPA claimed that the presence of methylene chloride at that level “does 
not contribute to unreasonable risk under the conditions of use,”211 but EPA only analyzed the 
 
204 See EPA, 1,4-Dioxane Draft Revised Unreasonable Risk Determination at 22–23 (July 2023), 
https://www.epa.gov/system/files/documents/2023-
07/Draft%20Revised%20Risk%20Determination%2014-Dioxane-2023.pdf (finding that 1,4-
dioxane as a byproduct contributed to unreasonable risk) (attached as Exhibit F). 
205 15 U.S.C. § 2605(a).  
206 2020 Risk Evaluation at 238; see also id. at 297. 
207 Proposed Rule at 65,081. 
208  See notes 69–72 supra and accompanying text.  
209 Proposed Rule at 65,082.  We continue to disagree with EPA’s use of a 1-in-10,000 cancer risk 
threshold for workers for the reasons set forth in prior comments.  See, e.g., EDF, Comments on 
Chemicals Review of Risk Evaluation for 1-Bromopropane, Docket ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2019-
0235-0001 at 88–90 (Oct. 11, 2019), https://www.regulations.gov/comment/EPA-HQ-OPPT-
2019-0235-0047.  
210 Methylene Chloride; Regulation Under the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA), 89 Fed. 
Reg. 39,254, 39,268 (May 8, 2024).  
211 Id.  

32 
 
risks associated with that “de minimis” weight fraction for a single condition of use (aerosol 
degreasing).212  Aerosol degreasing is not even the highest risk use of methylene chloride; 
instead, it was selected because it was “the only exposure scenario for which weight fraction 
could be varied” in EPA’s model.213  Therefore, EPA’s analysis does not ensure that a 0.1% 
weight fraction would prevent unreasonable risks from all of methylene chloride’s conditions of 
use, much less from combinations of such uses. 
 
EPA’s other defenses of a de minimis threshold are no more availing.  In its methylene 
chloride rule, EPA states that the “[t]he OSHA Hazard Communication Standard . . . indicates 
that a substance is considered a health hazard if it includes greater than 0.1% of a . . . 
carcinogen,” and that “other EPA programs, such as the Toxics Release Inventory (TRI) program, 
have adopted a de minimis threshold of 0.1% for chemicals which are defined as carcinogens or 
as a potential carcinogen.”214  Setting aside the questionable legality of the TRI de minimis 
exemption,215 neither the Occupational Safety and Health Act nor the Emergency Planning and 
Community Right-to-Know Act mandate the elimination of unreasonable risks to potentially 
exposed or susceptible subpopulations, and those thresholds were not set with TSCA’s mandate 
in mind.  They have no bearing on whether EPA can or should establish a de minimis threshold 
for 1-BP under TSCA. 
* 
* 
* 
 
EPA’s Proposed Rule represents a missed opportunity to address the risks facing 
communities surrounding facilities that emit carcinogenic 1-BP.  While we support the limited 
ways that the Proposed Rule will reduce emissions of 1-BP—including by banning certain uses 
of the chemical—the rule falls far short of what is mandated under TSCA and would leave 
fenceline communities exposed to unsafe levels of 1-BP.  EPA has the authority to strengthen its 
risk management approach to 1-BP by phasing out the use of the chemical and mandating 
emissions monitoring and reductions while 1-BP is still in use.  TSCA requires that it do so. 
 
If you have any questions about these comments, please contact Kelly Lester and Tosh 
Sagar at Earthjustice (klester@earthjustice.org and tsagar@earthjustice.org).

--- COMMENT ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471-0127 AND DOCKET ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 ---
September 4, 2024 
 
Michael S. Regan 
Administrator 
United States Environmental Protection Agency 
 
Comments to the EPA on the Proposed Rule for 1-Bromopropane 
Agency/Docket Numbers: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471; FRL-8156-01-OCSPP 
RIN: 2070-AK73 
 
Dear Administrator Regan: 
Brittany Carroll, a practicing Industrial Hygienist who evaluates workplace and worker 
exposures to chemical and physical hazards, appreciates the opportunity to provide 
feedback on the United States Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA) proposed rule on 1-
Bromopropane (1-BP). I hope you find this feedback useful and am happy to answer any 
questions you may have. The questions I have provided responses to (below) relate to my 
professional expertise; all other questions did not pertain to Industrial Hygiene-related 
issues. 
 
 
1. EPA is requesting public comment on all elements of the proposed regulatory 
action and the alternative regulatory actions. 
In the “Other monitoring requirements” section I do not agree with the proposed 
requirement to “…require owners and operators to re-monitor within 15 working days after 
receipt of results of any exposure monitoring when results indicate non-detect or air 
monitoring equipment malfunction, unless an Environmental Professional as defined at 40 
CFR 312.10 or a Certified Industrial Hygienist reviews the monitoring results and 
determines re-monitoring is not necessary.” 
 
First, I do not agree with the re-monitoring requirement because it does not make technical 
sense to re-sample a process that has been demonstrated to be well-controlled through 
valid data collection (i.e., a non-detect result which is below the ECEL Action Level). 
Similarly, the rationale to establish a regulated area where a non-detection result has been 
collected does not make technical sense if the result proves that an exposure is well-

controlled. However, if a non-detection result is due to not enough air volume collection 
whereas the non-detection result exceeds the ECEL (for example, a laboratory result: <0.1 
ppm), then re-monitoring should be required. This needs to be clarified in the final 
regulation to prevent excessive personal monitoring for processes that are well-controlled. 
 
Second, I disagree with the proposed requirement to re-monitor within 15 working days. I 
disagree with this proposed requirement because it may be exceedingly difficult to 
coordinate a sample event so quickly. Many businesses (especially smaller industry 
businesses) do not have an industrial hygienist on-site. Often, small businesses may have a 
safety professional on staff, but this person may or may not be qualified to perform 
exposure monitoring. In these instances, these businesses have to locate a contractor, 
negotiate pricing, establish a contract, schedule an on-site visit, etc. Typically, a visit such as 
this is negotiated to be a one-time visit with specifically budgeted hours. If the contractor 
has to return to re-sample it is unlikely that sampling event can be scheduled in such a 
tight deadline either due to scheduling constraints, budgeting issues (having to issue a 
change order), or not being aware that the re-sample event is required by TSCA regulation. 
In the case where a non-detect result exceeds the ECEL, re-monitoring should be required. 
However, the timeline has to be more lenient. I would recommend allowing 30-60 days 
following the initial event. Additionally, it should be considered that in some operations it 
may be unlikely to re-monitor if the specific task that involved 1-BP is very infrequent and 
does not occur in the timeline proposed in the regulation (i.e., only conducted quarterly or 
biannually or annually).  
 
Third, if air monitoring equipment malfunctions during sampling and air volume collected 
cannot be determined, the technical procedure for sample handling is to void that specific 
sample and not send it for laboratory analysis. If air monitoring equipment malfunctions, 
but the air volume can be determined and it is sufficient volume to collect a result below 
the ECEL Action Level, that sample may be saved and sent to laboratory analysis. 
 
9.  EPA is soliciting comment regarding an ECEL action level that is lower than the 
ECEL and any associated provisions related to the ECEL action level. 
I agree with having an action level established for inhalation exposures, similar to OSHA 
chemical-specific standards (i.e., vinyl chloride, formaldehyde, hexavalent chromium, 
benzene, etc.). However, compliance to monitor to the ECEL action level may be difficult. 
Currently the only feasible method to monitor to such a low compliance limit is via a small 
charcoal tube (passive monitoring badges do not have a high enough air uptake rate). 
Using a small charcoal tube, 1-BP can be detected down to 20% of the proposed ECEL over 
a full 8-hour workshift. Unfortunately, cheaper and easier-to-use passive badges are 
unable to detect to a low enough air concentration over the same sampling period. Thus, 
due to the extremely low ECEL action level, options for exposure monitoring are severely 
restricted. 
 

10. EPA requests comment on the feasibility of complying with and monitoring for 
an ECEL of 0.05 ppm and an ECEL action level of 0.03 ppm, including occupational 
exposure monitoring and associated analytical methods. In particular, EPA 
requests comment on changes that may be needed in order to meet such a 
standard, for example changes related to elimination or substitution of 1–BP, 
engineering controls, process changes, or monitoring frequency. EPA is also 
interested in the information on the availability of laboratory capacity needed to 
meet the proposed standard, and the costs associated with such testing.  
It is currently feasible to perform employee personal exposure monitoring to comply with 
the ECEL of 0.05 ppm. Utilizing a small charcoal tube (such as SKC 226-01 media) with a 
detection limit of 2.7 micrograms and a flow rate of 0.2 liters per minute, down to 20% of 
the ECEL can be detected on the sample media over the course of a work shift. However, it 
would not be possible to detect 10% of the ECEL over an 8-hour work shift, as the time 
required would exceed 480 minutes. The method described above is NIOSH 
1500/1501(GC). 
 
Notably, Industrial Hygiene professionals often use 10% of the occupational exposure limit 
(OEL) as an important risk-ranking criteria to professionally judge if an exposure is “well 
controlled.” If IHs are unable to establish if a sampling result is specifically <10% the OEL 
with the proposed sampling method, it will become more difficult for IHs (and other safety 
professionals) to provide certainty in their professional evaluations.   
 
11. EPA is soliciting comments regarding the timing of the initial exposure 
monitoring so that it would be representative of all tasks involving 1– BP where 
exposures may approach the ECEL. 
The timelines proposed for initial exposure monitoring seem to be reasonable for industry 
and federal agencies/contractors working on behalf of a federal agency. However, I am 
concerned about wide publication to industry alerting them of these regulations under 
TSCA. If industry/small businesses are unaware of workplace exposure limits and 
requirements via TSCA regulations (as opposed to OSHA), then they are unlikely to meet 
the required timelines for initial exposure monitoring and subsequent requirements 
established by TSCA regulations.  
 
12. EPA requests comment on the timeframes for periodic monitoring outlined in 
Table 1 of Unit IV.A.2. 
The timelines proposed for periodic monitoring are reasonable and appropriate, in my 
opinion, due to the similarity with the OSHA hexavalent chromium standard. However, I 
disagree with the proposed requirement “if the most recent (non-initial) exposure 
monitoring indicates that airborne exposure is below the ECEL action level, the owner or 
operator must repeat such monitoring within 6 months of the most recent monitoring until 
two consecutive monitoring measurements, taken at least seven days apart, are below the 
ECEL action level (<0.03 ppb 8-hour TWA), at which time the owner or operator must repeat 
the periodic exposure monitoring at least once every 5 years.”  

 
Firstly, the 0.03 ppb needs to be corrected to 0.03 ppm. Secondly, I believe the requirement 
should only require one additional sample taken within 6 months. The reasoning for this is 
because it is already confirmed that the sample is below the ECEL action level. One 
additional sample is sufficient to re-confirm that the operation is not exposing employees 
to 1-BP. 
 
Additionally, there appears to be a typographical error in the periodic monitoring section – 
“If the most recent exposure monitoring indicates that airborne exposure is at or above the 
ECEL action level (≥0.03 ppm 8-hour TWA) but at or below the ECEL (≤0.05 ppb 8-hour TWA), 
the owner or operator must repeat the periodic exposure monitoring within 6 months of 
the most recent exposure monitoring.” The “≤0.05 ppb” needs to be corrected to “≤0.05 
ppm”. 
 
13. EPA requests comment on workplace monitoring for implementation of an 
ECEL. EPA is soliciting information related to the frequency of monitoring, initial 
monitoring, and periodic monitoring that would be needed to demonstrate 
workplace exposure levels. Specifically, when this may impact the frequency of 
periodic monitoring where initial monitoring shows that employee exposures are 
above the level that would initiate requirements for compliance with the ECEL. 
The proposed requirements are appropriate, the only edit would be the one suggested in 
the answer to Question 12 above. 
 
14. EPA is requesting comment on the proposed timeframe of within 30 days to 
conduct additional exposure monitoring after there has been a change in the 
production, process, control equipment, personnel or work practices that may 
reasonably be expected to result in new or additional exposures at or above the 
ECEL action level, or when the owner or operator has any reason to believe that 
new or additional exposures at or above the ECEL action level have occurred.  
EPA should consider lengthening the proposed timeframe to conduct additional exposure 
monitoring following a change in production, process, control equipment, personnel or 
work practices to 45 – 60 days. The reasoning for lengthening the timeframe is because a 
majority of businesses do not have staff on-site who are capable of performing industrial 
hygiene exposure monitoring. These businesses have to locate a contractor, negotiate 
pricing, establish a contract, schedule an on-site visit, etc. This process is lengthy, so a 
longer grace period would be appreciated. 
 
17. EPA is soliciting comment on requiring warning signs to demarcate regulated 
areas, such as the requirements found in OSHA’s General Industry Standard for 
Beryllium. 
The proposed requirement to require warning signs to demarcate 1-BP regulated areas, 
similar to OSHA’s General Industry Standard for Beryllium is appropriate. However, like 
OSHA’s General Industry Standard for Beryllium, EPA should provide language in the final 

regulation that it would require to be posted on the signage. Below is the sign specification 
that OSHA requires for Beryllium:  
 
1910.1024(m)(2)(ii)(B)  
The employer must ensure each warning sign required by paragraph (m)(2)(i) of this standard 
bears the following legend: 
DANGER 
REGULATED AREA 
BERYLLIUM 
MAY CAUSE CANCER 
CAUSES DAMAGE TO LUNGS 
AUTHORIZED PERSONNEL ONLY 
WEAR RESPIRATORY PROTECTION AND PERSONAL PROTECTIVE CLOTHING AND 
EQUIPMENT IN THIS AREA 
 
18. EPA is requesting comment on whether there should be a requirement to 
replace cartridges or canisters after a certain number of hours, such as the 
requirements found in OSHA’s General Industry Standard for 1,3-Butadiene, or 
a requirement for a minimum service life of non-powered air-purifying 
respirators such as the requirements found in OSHA’s General Industry Standard 
for Benzene. 
The EPA could feasibly establish a requirement for respirator cartridge change-out 
schedule similar to the OSHA General Industry Standard for 1,3-Butadiene and use the 
ECEL as the comparison tool and a multiplication factor. However, research would be 
needed to determine if the respirator cartridge change-out requirements are satisfactory 
(too lenient where an individual is not protected from 1-BP by using an expired cartridge, 
or too conservative where cartridges are being changed too frequently and creating 
additional waste). The development of a 1-BP service life indicator for cartridges would be 
preferred, due to the visual component of seeing when a cartridge needs to be changed 
out, instead of having to keep track of the amount of time a cartridge has been used. 
 
19. EPA is requesting comment on how owners and operators can engage with 
potentially exposed persons on the development and implementation of an 
exposure control plan and PPE program. EPA is also requesting comment on 
whether EPA should include designated representatives who can also be 

permitted to observe exposure monitoring and have regular access to exposure-
related information at the request of potentially exposed persons. 
Owners and operators can engage with potentially exposed persons to develop exposure 
control plans by collecting and documenting the information with the potentially exposed 
group that is performing the work.  
 
At my place of work, we are considering requiring the people who handle and perform 
work with Methylene Chloride to document their exposure control plans themselves in 
conjunction with safety personnel (i.e., an Industrial Hygienist). That way the process is 
documented by the expert(s) of the process. Our PPE program is already established and 
documented previous to TSCA regulations. 
 
20. EPA requests comment relative to the ability of owners or operators to 
conduct initial monitoring within the timeframes identified in this proposed rule, 
and anticipated timelines for any procedural adjustments needed to 
comply with the requirements in this proposed rule, including establishment of a 
respiratory protection program and development of an exposure control plan. 
The proposed timelines are appropriate to conduct initial monitoring outlined in the 
proposed rule. However, I work at a company that has many industrial hygiene staff and an 
already established respiratory program. 
 
21. EPA also requests comment on whether additional time is needed to 
implement all aspects of the WCPP or if there are available substitutes for these 
applications. 
Additional time is not needed to implement a WCPP for my place of work due to it being a 
federal contractor working on behalf of a federal agency. I believe 3 years is sufficient in 
order to establish the additional elements of chemical control that will be required. 
 
22. EPA is soliciting comments on the requirements proposed for appropriate PPE 
selection. In addition, EPA understands that some workplaces rinse and reuse PPE 
after minimal use and is therefore soliciting comments on the impact on 
effectiveness of rinsing and reusing certain types of PPE, either gloves or 
protective clothing and gear. EPA also requests comment on the degree to which 
additional guidance related to use of PPE might be appropriate. 
The EPA is proposing to require the use of polyvinyl alcohol (PVA) or multiple-layer laminate 
glove materials. However, it is unclear if the use of these specific glove materials is required 
for the listed occupational uses [manufacturing (import), processing as a reactant, 
processing as incorporation into articles, processing by repackaging, recycling, and 
disposal], or if the glove requirements are for all those potentially exposed persons via the 
dermal route. This requirement needs further clarification. 
 
As an industrial hygienist who makes glove requirements/recommendations, I believe 
leaving this decision for PPE should be left up to a trained safety personnel. While PVA and 

multiple-layer laminate gloves can withstand exposure to 1-BP for 480 minutes according 
to testing, this amount of chemical resistance is not necessary for all applications/uses of 1-
BP. Working in academia or in a research and development setting, most contact with 
chemicals is incidental and brief. Over prescription of a glove material that reduces 
dexterity may make certain activities more hazardous. Prescribing a less chemically-
resistant glove is a benefit in certain situations where contact is not expected to be more 
than incidental or due to an accident (i.e. very unlikely to occur). 
 
Additionally, the rinse and reuse of PPE is not encouraged. PPE is one-time use, especially 
in instances where the PPE has been exposed to a chemical. PPE can and does degrade 
over time. 
 
The EPA should provide general guidance for PPE use; specific PPE evaluations should be 
conducted by trained and qualified safety professionals given that not all workplaces are 
the same, handle chemicals in the same way or manner, or have the same work practices 
and procedures. Further, as PPE research evolves, initial requirements made by the EPA 
may become outdated (e.g., another material provides adequate or excellent protection at 
a fraction of the cost). 
 
26. EPA requests comment on the primary alternative regulatory action (a 
combination of prohibitions, requirements for a WCPP, prescriptive controls, self-
certification, and glove use) and whether any elements of this primary 
alternative regulatory action described in this proposed rule should be 
considered as EPA develops the final regulatory action. In particular, EPA is 
soliciting comment on prescribing specific engineering or administrative controls 
that would reduce inhalation exposures enough to address the unreasonable risk 
across all workplaces engaged in a condition of use. EPA also requests comment 
on any advantages or drawbacks for the timelines outlined in Unit IV.B. compared 
to the timelines identified for the proposed regulatory action in Unit IV.A. 
The EPA prescribing specific engineering and/or administrative controls may contribute 
positively in compliance as industries and especially small businesses have a better idea of 
how to comply with TSCA regulation when engaging in regulated processes. However, if the 
controls being prescribed are exceptionally burdensome, this may lead to businesses being 
forced to shut down. It may be better to suggest or highly encourage the use of certain 
controls, but to allow for a certain amount of professional judgement as long as personal 
exposures remain below the ECEL or Action Level. 
 
The timeline proposed in the primary alternative regulation would be preferred by industry 
because they may also be subject to other TSCA regulations. Industry having to work to 
comply with multiple new regulations simultaneously can become overburdensome 
enough where compliance isn’t feasible. In most cases, there are limited safety personnel 
in industry sectors, and working to establish multiple new programs at once can be a 

struggle. Longer compliance timelines are always appreciated when new regulations are 
implemented. 
 
35. EPA is requesting comment on specific controls that would mitigate the 
unreasonable risk from 1–BP and that could be included as part of a prescriptive 
workplace controls requirement, which could be considered as EPA develops the 
final regulatory action. Specifically, EPA is soliciting comment on combinations of 
specific engineering controls, administrative controls, and PPE that would reduce 
inhalation exposures to at or below the ECEL of 0.05 ppm as an 8-hour TWA for all 
workplaces where such controls would be required. EPA also is soliciting 
comment on the extent to which such requirements could reduce inhalation 
exposures to at or below the ECEL of 0.05 ppm as an 8-hour TWA. EPA is 
requesting comment on the compliance timeframe needed to implement 
engineering controls, administrative controls, and PPE that reduce inhalation 
exposures to at below the ECEL of 0.05 ppm as an 8- hour TWA for all regulated 
entities. Additionally, EPA is requesting any existing monitoring data that could 
inform whether a WCPP or prescriptive controls with a PPE program is a more 
appropriate regulatory action for industrial and commercial use of 1–BP in batch 
vapor degreasing. 
It is difficult to recommend specific controls for general operations that could be widely 
applicable or used due to each workplace having different processes, work practices, 
operational set-ups, etc. If a process can be enclosed or contained, that is typically the 
preferred engineering control recommended by IH.  
 
If work operations are/were able to be conducted remotely, this is another a great 
engineering control. However, not all businesses have the budget to be able to implement 
such costly controls and remain operational. 
 
A suggestion for a potential administrative control is to recommend for processes to be 
performed unattended (if possible); this typically entails starting a work process at the end 
of a workshift and letting it operate overnight or over the weekend. However, this is not 
going to be possible in all/many situations as processes are often required to be attended 
or monitored.  
 
If a business is pursuing implementing a new Industrial Hygiene control that completely 
changes a work process, the implementation timeline of such control could range up to a 
year or more. Engineering control implementation requires input from engineers, 
budgeting, safety personnel, in addition to the actual installation of the control, 
commissioning of the control, etc. All of these steps can be very time and budget 
consuming. This is also true of administrative controls such as developing trainings and 
new procedural documents. PPE is generally the easiest control to implement.  
 

42. EPA requests comment on whether owners and operators should be required 
to attest to whether and why the exposure controls they have selected would not 
result in increased releases of 1–BP to ambient air from the workplace, and keep 
records of that statement as part of the WCPP exposure control plan. 
An attestation statement regarding whether and why exposure controls selected would not 
result in increased releases of 1-BP to ambient air from the workplace does not seem 
especially effective as it is likely to be viewed as  an unnecessary add-on to administrative 
burden by stakeholders. It would be more effective to require environmental air (emission) 
data that proves 1-BP is not being released to ambient air from outside of local exhaust 
stacks. 
 
If you have any questions about my comments on EPA’s proposed rule on NMP, please 
contact me at carrollbm96@outlook.com. Thank you for your time and consideration. 
 
Sincerely, 
 
Brittany Carroll, MPH 
Industrial Hygienist

--- COMMENT ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471-0146 AND DOCKET ID: EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 ---
September 23, 2024 
 
U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 
Attention: Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471 
The Honorable Michal I. Freedhoff, Ph.D. 
Assistant Administrator 
Office of Chemical Safety and Pollution Prevention 
1200 Pennsylvania Avenue, N.W., 
Washington, D.C. 20460 
 
RE:  
1-Bromopropane (1–BP); Regulation Under the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA); 89 Fed. 
Reg. 65,066 (August 8, 2024).  
 
Dear Dr. Freedhoff: 
The Alliance for Chemical Distribution (ACD) submits the following comments in response to the 
proposed risk management actions published by the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) in the 
August 8, 2024, Federal Register regarding Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OPPT-2020-0471, 89 Fed. Reg. 65,066, 
titled, “1-Bromopropane (1–BP); Regulation Under the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA).” We 
appreciate this opportunity to comment. 
About ACD 
The Alliance for Chemical Distribution supports and champions the chemical distribution experts the 
world depends on to safely, reliably, responsibly, and sustainably move the chemical products essential 
to our daily lives. ACD’s more than 400 chemical distribution industry members are primarily small, 
multi-generational family-owned businesses. They provide critical chemical products used in medicine 
and health care, food and agriculture, clean water and sanitation, energy production, electronics, 
communication, and more to over 750,000 end users.  
As leaders of the over $27 billion chemical distribution industry, ACD member companies have the 
extensive expertise, commitment to safety and sustainability, and access to a deep well of resources 
needed to ensure chemicals are moved safely and responsibly when and where they are needed. ACD 
owners and operators have a personal stake in the health, safety, and security of their employees, 
companies, and communities. They demonstrate their commitment through strict adherence to the 
highest standards in quality, safety, sustainability, and performance through compliance with ACD 
Responsible DistributionTM.  
The comprehensive Code of Management Practice, a condition of Responsible Distribution, requires 
each member company to have an active program designed to continuously improve safety and reduce 
incidents. This dedication, in addition to relationships with employees, involvement in local 

 
 
communities, including participation in Local Emergency Planning Committees, and careful compliance 
with numerous regulations on the federal, state, and local levels, empowers ACD members to navigate 
the ever-changing complexities of moving essential chemicals around the nation and the globe. 
ACD worked closely with Congress for over a decade to revise the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA) 
and supported the passage of the Frank R. Lautenberg Chemical Safety for the 21st Century Act (LCSA). 
ACD remains engaged in implementing the LCSA and has submitted comments to the EPA on several of 
the Agency’s proposed rules under the new TSCA.  
ACD shares the EPA’s goals of eliminating the unreasonable risk of exposure to toxic chemicals, practicing 
environmental and product stewardship, providing the public with meaningful information about 
exposure risks, preventing chemical accidents, improving preparedness, and safeguarding public health. 
Additionally, ACD appreciates the agency’s hard work completing the required risk management rules 
consistent with the Lautenberg Amendments to TSCA. Though these efforts will protect workers and the 
public from risks to their health and safety, they must be built upon the best available science, informed 
by current industrial practices, and informed with input from all stakeholders. It is with these goals and 
efforts in mind that ACD urges the EPA to allow for the continued responsible manufacture, import, 
distribution, and use of 1-bromopropane (1-BP) in all industrial settings.  
EPA Should Utilize Workplace Chemical Protection Programs (WCPP) and Prescriptive Controls 
ACD was pleased that the EPA has proposed to allow the continued use (including manufacture, import, 
and distribution) of 1-BP in most industrial settings when there are prescriptive controls and/or 
workplace controls to mitigate exposures. ACD believes that the EPA’s decision to permit the use of 1-BP 
in these scenarios is justified, considering the low level of risk 1-BP poses to employees in these 
circumstances.  
For this reason, ACD urges the EPA to reconsider its proposed ban on certain industrial uses and instead 
require WCPPs for all industrial uses. The prescriptive requirements necessary to establish a WCPP that 
meets the requirements outlined in this rule would effectively prevent unreasonable exposure risks to 1-
BP. Additionally, ACD recommends that the EPA implement more reasonable exposure levels.  
The Small Business Advocacy Review (SBAR) panel report includes similar opinions. First, the EPA 
reported that small entity representatives (SER) “did not think 1-BP should be used in consumer and 
small-scale commercial products.”  Furthermore, one SER “described how, as required by the 
Occupational Safety and Health Administration (OSHA) and other regulatory entities, formulators use 
various techniques such as shipping labels; Globally Harmonized System (GHS) information; [personal 
protective equipment] PPE (including respirators and dermal protection); engineering controls; 
ventilation systems; and enclosed mixing systems” to manage any exposure risks of 1-BP. Additionally, 
several times, SERs described in detail the investments they and their customers have made in exposure 
reduction technologies. They also expressed concerns about being able to achieve the proposed 0.05 
ppm existing chemical exposure limit (ECEL). One SER stated that “a requirement to meet the ECEL 
would be a de facto ban on [the] use of 1-BP by small businesses and other vapor degreasing operations 

 
 
for many critical applications in the surface finishing industry, which could lead facilities to choose a 
substitute chemical with health or environmental risks.”1 
ACD was pleased to see that the EPA considered the SERs’ feedback and allowed for the continued use of 
1-BP in vapor degreasing applications. Facility owners and operators are capable of incorporating WCPPs 
that have the controls necessary to ensure there is no unreasonable risk to exposure of 1-BP to 
employees, effectively ensuring no unreasonable risks are occurring in their operations. For this reason, 
ACD believes it is reasonable to instead require WCPPs for all industrial uses and an achievable ECEL that 
is based on the best available science and weight of scientific evidence. 
Lack of Adequate Alternatives 
The EPA lacks comprehensive hazard data for alternatives even though the agency is proposing to ban 
conditions of use (COUs). The lack of data potentially puts users at risk of unknown or even more 
significant hazards without required workplace protections. Instead of prohibiting COUs, the EPA should 
require WCPPs that protect users. Again, similar concerns were raised during the SBAR, with one SER 
that “described [the need for] consideration for substitute chemicals or processes, including efficacy and 
safety concerns associated with the use of alternative chemicals.”2 
For example, the EPA included 227 products in the alternatives assessment for industrial and commercial 
use in adhesive chemical – spray adhesive for foam cushion manufacturing and other uses. In the 227 
alternative products evaluated, the EPA only found 165 unique chemical substances with associated 
Chemical Abstract Service Registry Numbers (CASRNs). Of those unique chemicals, reasonably available 
hazard information is available for 64 percent, and only 42 percent have at least one authoritative data 
source. Moreover, 39 percent of those chemicals are flammable. In the assessment for this COU, the EPA 
identified acetone, dimethyl ether, and methylene chloride as the most common chemical ingredients.3 
It should be noted that methylene chloride underwent its final risk management rulemaking under TSCA 
earlier this year. While the final rule was less stringent than initially proposed, it still places significant 
restraints on the supply chain, and affected entities are working to comply with the separate 
requirements of this regulation. 
ACD is concerned that if the EPA severely restricts the use of 1-BP through unachievable exposure levels, 
stakeholders will turn to alternatives with unknown and potentially worse hazards. The EPA must require 
WCPPs instead of prohibitions due to the absence of adequate and safe alternatives. ACD member 
companies and SERs have reported that identifying and adopting alternatives for 1-BP is not always 
practicable. In the EPA’s Final SBAR Report, the EPA summarizes that “[d]uring the Pre-Panel and Panel 
outreach meetings, as well as in SER comments, SERs noted the benefits to their business of using 1-BP 
and their concerns with potential substitutes and alternative methods such as aqueous cleaning. SERs

cited various chemical and economic properties that make 1-BP a good choice, including low

Inhalation Exposure and EPA’s ECEL 
For inhalation exposures to workers, EPA derived an ECEL of 0.05 ppm based on the incidence of 
alveolar/bronchiolar adenoma or carcinoma (combined) in female B6C3F1 mice from a two-year 
inhalation toxicity study in mice and rats performed by the U.S. National Toxicology Program (NTP).5,6 In 
those studies, NTP concluded the following:7 
 
Under the conditions of these 2-year inhalation studies, there was some 
evidence of carcinogenic activity [asterisk omitted] of 1-bromopropane 
in male F344/N rats based on the occurrence of rare adenomas of the 
large intestine and increased incidences of epithelial neoplasms of the 
skin (keratoacanthoma, squamous cell carcinoma, and basal cell 
neoplasms). Increased incidences of malignant mesothelioma and 
pancreatic islet adenoma and carcinoma (combined) may also have 
been related to 1-bromopropane exposure. There was clear evidence of 
carcinogenic activity of 1-bromopropane in female F344/N rats based on 
increased incidences of adenoma of the large intestine. Increased 
incidences of skin neoplasms may also have been related to 1-
bromopropane exposure. There was no evidence of carcinogenic activity 
of 1-bromopropane in male B6C3F1 mice exposed to concentrations of 
62.5, 125, or 250 ppm 1-bromopropane. There was clear evidence of 
carcinogenic activity of 1-bromopropane in female B6C3F1 mice based 
on increased incidences of alveolar/bronchiolar neoplasms. 
EPA did not, however, perform a framework analysis of the carcinogenic mode of action for the identified 
tumors, per its 2005 “Guidelines for Carcinogen Risk Assessment” (the 2005 Guidelines) and as required 
under TSCA Sections 26(h)-(i).8 Instead, EPA provided an approximate one-page narrative on the 
“Possible Mode of Action for 1-BP Toxicity” in the final risk evaluation for 1-BP.9 Though beyond the scope 
of these comments, ACD notes some obvious conclusions from the NTP report. For example, there was 
some evidence and clear evidence of carcinogenic activity in male and female rats, respectively, for the 
occurrence of rare adenomas of the large intestines. In comparison, there was no evidence of 
carcinogenic activity in male mice and clear evidence of carcinogenic activity in female mice for lung

to humans. As shown below, if EPA had based its ECEL on either the keratoacanthoma/squamous cell 
carcinoma (combined) in male rats or the large intestine adenomas in female rats, the respective ECELs 
would be approximately four or six times higher.

The ECELs based on the rat carcinogenicity data support that EPA’s acute non-cancer exposure limit 
(ELacute) of 0.17 ppm (i.e., 0.86 mg/m3) should serve as the regulatory limit required as part of EPA’s 
proposed WCPPs. There are, however, caveats to consider with this value. ACD notes that the ELacute is a 
conservative value that is 3× lower than if EPA had followed its 1994 Guidance titled “Methods for 
derivation of inhalation reference concentrations and application of inhalation dosimetry”. If EPA had 
done so, it would have calculated an ELacute of 0.57 ppm (i.e., 2.85 mg/m3), thereby justifying use of the 
 
10  
EPA (2005) supra note 8, at 2-47. 
11  
EPA (2014) “Summary Report, State-of-the-Science Workshop on Chemically-Induced Mouse Lung Tumors: Applications to Human

https://ordspub.epa.gov/ords/eims/eimscomm.getfile?p_download_id=521522  

 
 
ECEL of 0.3 ppm (i.e., 1.5 mg/m3), based on the female rat data for large intestine adenoma. EPA 
provided the following justification for not following the 1994 Guidance:12 
[A] portion of the toxicokinetic uncertainty may be accounted for by the 
calculation of an HEC accounting for the relative blood/air partition 
coefficients across species and application of a dosimetric adjustment 
factor [DAF] as outlined in the RfC methodology [citation to 1994 
Guidance omitted]; however, an UFA of 10 is retained to account for 
additional toxicokinetic differences that remain unaccounted for. 1-BP is 
irritating to the respiratory tract and rodents exhibit physiological 
responses (such as reflex bradypnea) that differ from humans and may 
alter uptake due to hyper- or hypoventilation, resulting in decreased 
internal dose in rodents relative to the applied concentration. Therefore, 
a UFA of 10 is retained to account for toxicokinetic differences [citation 
omitted]. 
EPA did not, however, provide similar justifications in its other final risk evaluations for chemical 
substances that are irritating to the respiratory tract. For example, in the final risk evaluation for 1,4-
dioxane, EPA applied a reduced value of 3× for UFA after the application of a DAF.13 ACD notes that 
hypoventilation would also be a potential concern in this example, yet EPA followed the 1994 Guidance. 
Dermal Exposure 
For the dermal exposure assessment for workers, the EPA incorporated a fraction absorbed parameter 
as a modification to its “EPA/OPPT 2-Hand Dermal Exposure to Liquids Model.” This parameter was used 
to account for the volatility of 1-BP. The EPA also used, however, surrogate data on cooking oil, a highly 
viscous substance, as default values to estimate the amount of 1-BP remaining on the skin. The EPA’s use 
of the surrogate cooking oil data is the subject of a differing scientific opinion among the EPA scientists. 
On July 1, 2024, the EPA released its draft risk evaluation for 1,1-dichloroethane (1,1-DCE) and included a 
charge question to the Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA) Science Advisory Committee on Chemicals 
(SACC) about the appropriateness of using the surrogate cooking oil data to inform the amount of 1,1-
DCE remaining on the skin.  
The EPA’s use of these data in the 1-BP final risk evaluation to quantify unreasonable dermal risks to 
workers is, therefore, inconsistent with the scientific standards under TSCA Section 26(h)-(i). EPA must, 
therefore, hold off on issuing the 1-BP proposed rule as final, until it receives the TSCA SACC’s 
recommendations on whether the EPA was justified in using the surrogate cooking oil data on 1,1-DCE 
and other low-viscosity substances like 1-BP. If the TSCA SACC determines that the EPA inappropriately 
used the surrogate cooking oil data, then the EPA must revise its final risk evaluation for 1-BP and re-
issue its proposed 1-BP rule based on the revised unreasonable risk determinations.

EPA (2020a) supra note 9, at 214-215.

See, e.g., EPA (2020b) “Final Risk Evaluation for 1,4-Dioxane CASRN: 123-91-1, EPA-740-R1-8007, U.S. Environmental Protection

Need for Collaboration to Adopt Successful WCPP Requirements 
TSCA is intended to supplement, not replace, other established regulatory requirements. Additionally, 
because the actions of this rule are limited to TSCA-regulated uses, ACD recommends that the EPA work 
with the Occupational Safety and Health Administration (OSHA) to align on a WCPP. This coordination 
and partnership with OSHA would, to the fullest extent possible, protect the broadest population from 
unreasonable exposure risks through robust industrial hygiene practices and controls, including proper 
use of PPE. 
Furthermore, allowing entities to demonstrate that they can meet the requirements of the WCPP, versus 
outright prohibition of certain uses, would enable businesses — especially small businesses — that rely 
on the use of 1-BP to remain in operation while also protecting their workers. A WCPP is a practical and 
reasonable way for EPA to address exposure risks of certain toxic chemicals without interfering with 
national security or critical infrastructure or putting livelihoods at risk. 
ACD also recommends that the EPA engage with recognized industrial hygiene and safety experts, such 
as the American Industrial Hygiene Association (AIHA), when developing the WCPP. Other stakeholders, 
including those who meet the rigorous standards of programs like ACD’s Responsible DistributionTM and 
the American Chemistry Council’s Responsible Care®, should also be included in the conversation as they 
are recognized experts who can competently address safety measures already in place at their facilities.  
De Minimis Exemption 
The EPA requests comment on the inclusion of a de minimis level of 1-BP in formulations where it is an 
impurity at concentrations (i.e., less than 0.1% or 0.5% by weight) when finalizing COU prohibitions. 
Products containing 1-BP below this threshold would not be subject to the requirements in the proposal. 
ACD supports this inclusion of a de minimis level and applauds EPA’s willingness to include the exemption 
in the proposal.  
ACD supports including a de minimis exemption as there are no threats of an unreasonable risk when the 
presence of 1-BP is trivial. Allowing this de minimis exemption gives regulated entities important 
operational flexibility. Without this de minimis threshold, manufacturers and importers of TSCA-
regulated chemicals would have to declare and prove through documentation that no 1-BP was present 
in those products. This would subject manufacturers and importers to unrealistic reporting 
requirements. 
Chemical Distribution Industry Impact  
ACD members provide essential chemical products that support critical processes in every sector of the 
economy. Members sell imported and domestically sourced 1-BP and mixtures where 1-BP is present as 
an intentional component, by-product, or impurity. Some ACD members physically handle and distribute 
these products, while under other ACD members’ business models, they serve as brokers. In these 
scenarios, they source these substances for customers and never come into physical contact with the 
products.  

 
 
ACD members sell essential products nationwide to large corporations, small businesses, municipalities, 
states, and the federal government. Every other year, ACD members self-report 14 the sectors into which 
they sell and the following table outlines ACD member companies’ presence in markets relevant to these 
proposed risk management actions.

If the EPA prohibits certain uses and sets unachievable exposure limits, it risks severely restricting the 
manufacture, import, distribution, and use of 1-BP as a solvent. This chemistry is essential to the 
chemical distribution industry because it is used as a viable alternative to the other halogenated solvents 
being regulated.  
One ACD member, classified as a small business, has 85 active accounts using this product. They sell into 
a wide range of industrial applications, including aerospace, automotive, and electric vehicles and 
batteries, military suppliers, durable goods, medical devices, and several other uses. The solvent is used 
downstream by their customers primarily in vapor degreasers, in both vacuum and open-top machines. 
It is also used to clean metallic substrates to remove difficult contaminants in extremely tight spaces and 
blind holes.  
While alternatives are feasible, some caveats must be considered. Other than switching to another 
chlorinated solvent, most end users who want to continue to vapor degrease are looking into the use of 
fluorinated blends, hydrocarbons, or modified alcohols. Aqueous alternatives are also an option. The first 
issue with an alternative is determining whether it will clean the substrate. If an alternative solvent can 
 
14  
Alliance for Chemical Distribution. “ACD Annual Report 2023.” ACD. Accessed September 10, 2024. https://www.acd-
chem.com/nacdcom/assets/file/public/2023annualreport/.  
15  
Coatings, Adhesives, Sealants, and Elastomers (CASE) 

 
 
be used, then the next challenge is to determine whether the machine needs to be modified. If the 
machine cannot be modified, purchasing new equipment is required, which can be very expensive. 
Moreover, identifying and sourcing new equipment can have lead times of up to 12 months. If aqueous 
chemistry can work, the footprint of the equipment needs to be considered, as well as the cost of energy 
to heat the chemistry and dry the parts. There is also a concern with treating the wastewater from the 
system. As mentioned previously, many of these concerns, including the lead times, costs, energy 
consumption, and the importance of 1-BP to small businesses, were reiterated in the EPA’s SBAR Panel. 16 
Finally, as spelled out in the proposed WCPP, there must be the use of proper PPE and exhaust of the 
equipment. All users of the solvents should be required to record and monitor vapors. ACD’s member, as 
a significant distributor of these chemicals, recommends and assists the end users in the proper use of 
the solvents and testing protocols. 
Prohibitions on certain uses of 1-BP would cause devastating effects that would reverberate throughout 
the value chain. These regulatory actions would eliminate the ability of ACD members to supply their 
customers with chemicals critical to product formulation, manufacturing, or application. This rule, 
coupled with the EPA actions on Trichloroethylene (TCE), perchloroethylene (PCE), and methylene 
chloride are making it very difficult for the downstream industries into which ACD members sell, 
especially the industrial surface finishing industry, to economically adjust to the rapid changes, not to 
mention the lead time and expense of new equipment. This change has been anticipated for several 
years, and most end users have updated their equipment to limit exposure, reduce waste, and control 
costs. However, eliminating these infinitely recyclable solvents will force users to either switch to much 
more expensive alternatives and require them to make enormous equipment investments or shut down 
their production altogether and move their process out of the U.S. to a more favorable location.  
The EPA should consider extending the compliance timelines for the final rule to enable businesses, 
especially small entities, to adjust their operations to meet the frequent and significant changes required 
under the EPA’s recent TSCA Section 6 actions. The EPA must remember that the chemical distribution 
industry does not handle singular chemicals; their operations manage hundreds of chemistries. ACD has 
numerous members impacted by several of the Agency’s risk management actions. Expecting businesses 
to adapt to a dozen new exposure standards without encountering significant and burdensome 
operational and economic effects is unrealistic. The EPA must align its TSCA Section 6 changes with other 
regulatory standards and requirements as much as possible. More uniform standards reduce costs and 
streamline transition periods. Importantly, the EPA acknowledges that some COUs and some activities or 
facilities may require additional compliance timeframes to transition appropriately. For example, those 
instances where PCE – which is undergoing its own TSCA Section 6 rulemaking – and 1-BP are desired 
substances because they are non-flammable solvents used as cleaning agents for vapor degreasing. The 
EPA recognizes that additional time is needed to identify an alternative chemical or process to avoid 
flammability concerns in these instances.17

Economic Impact 
To demonstrate the impacts that this rulemaking has and how it will directly impact the chemical 
distribution industry, the ACD employed the services of John Dunham & Associates (JDA) to conduct an 
economic analysis of this proposed rule along with other recent TSCA Section 6 rulemakings to evaluate 
the cumulative economic impact that these regulatory actions could have on the chemical distribution 
industry. 
When analyzing this cost’s impact at the distribution level, JDA estimates the overall cost of the rule 
would be over $28.3 million for the chemical distribution sector. This would have an adverse economic 
impact. 
Based on a model developed for the ACD by JDA, the annualized cost of the rule on the chemical 
distribution industry alone would be equal to $0.002 per ton, resulting in a 0.001 percent increase in the 
cost of chemicals.18   
Also based on the JDA model, this additional fee will result in 940 tons of reduced chemical sales.19  This 
is a decrease of about 0.0015 percent of the current volume.20 Lower sales volumes will result in reduced 
jobs as distributors need fewer truck drivers, clerks, and warehouse staff.

Around 1 FTE chemical distributor jobs could be lost due to the higher prices under the proposed rule. 
Including businesses that supply chemical distributors, and those that depend on re-spending by direct 
and supplier firm employees, the rule would lead to a total of about 3 fewer FTE jobs and over $200,000 
in lost wages and benefits. On top of this, the American economy would be reduced by just under 
$700,000.   
This is just the impact on the chemical distribution industry. Higher prices for chemicals will flow through 
nearly every other sector of the economy, leading to more job losses. Also, while the economic impact 
numbers may seem relatively small for this rule, when combined with the EPA’s other recent risk 
management actions, the cumulative cost is substantial. The economic report on the cumulative impact 
of the EPA’s recent risk management actions and the JDA demand model methodology are included in 
full in these comments as Appendix A and B, respectively.

Conclusion 
Every chemical that has undergone the TSCA Section 6 chemical risk evaluation process has received an 
unreasonable risk determination that has led to bans and/or unachievable and indefensible workplace 
standards, with time-limited exemptions granted where the EPA deemed appropriate. Chemicals are 
essential to daily life, including those that undergo risk evaluation and risk management, and if the EPA 
unnecessarily restricts and bans substances that have no or limited viable alternatives, there will be 
severe unintended consequences for the health, safety, and well-being of the public. Congress did not 
intend this result in amending TSCA eight years ago. 
For all the above reasons, ACD strongly urges the EPA to reconsider its proposed regulatory actions for 1-
BP. The proposed requirements are overly prescriptive and unnecessarily prohibit several uses. Instead, 
ACD urges the EPA to focus on establishing workable WCPPs for each condition of use to ensure workers 
and the general public are safe while allowing critical uses of 1-BP to continue.  
Thank you for your consideration. If you have questions or require additional information, please do not 
hesitate to contact me.  
 
Sincerely, 
 
 
Jennifer C. Gibson 
Senior Vice President, Regulatory Affairs 
 
 

 
 
APPENDIX A 
Cumulative Economic Analysis  
 
 
According to the General Accounting Office, the current Administration has been enacting new rules at a 
rapid pace.  In 2023, the Biden Administration enacted 1,629 new rules (4.5 new rules every single day), 
of which 81 were major rules, leading to significant economic costs and business disruptions.  The pace 
has quickened in 2024, with 77 new rules, of which 63 were major, enacted so far.  That means that 2 
major rules are being enacted every 3 days. And this is just the rules being submitted to the Government 
Accountability Office (GAO) for Congressional Review.21 
The typical alphabet soup of federal regulatory agencies (EPA, FDA, OSHA, SEC, FCC, etc.) only reported 
397 final rules to the GAO, of which 275 (69.3 percent) were from the EPA.  Considering that a total of 
2,681 final rules were published in the Federal Register in 2023, it is likely that these regulatory agencies 
have been slow to inform the GAO.22  
Each of these rules adds costs to the operations of chemical distributors, which are passed on to their 
customers through higher prices.  In turn, these higher prices impact the demand for chemical products, 
not only the ones being regulated but all products sold through distributors.   
JDA has provided analysis of the cost to the industry of a number of these rules.  While individually, the 
costs may seem small, in aggregate they can be extremely expensive. 
Looking at just four of the rules examined by JDA, all of which have been proffered by the EPA under the 
Toxic Substances Control Act (TSCA) the overall cost to the chemical distribution industry totals $48.2 
million per year.  The rules are: 
1. Additional regulations on the use of 1-bromopropane (1–BP), a widely used solvent in a variety 
of occupational and consumer applications, including vapor degreasing, aerosol degreasing, 
adhesives and sealants, and insulation.  
 
2. New rules that would ostensibly ban the production, importation, and use of trichloroethylene 
(TCE) in the United States.  This is a widely used chemical in the production of products such as 
degreasers, lubricants, paints and coatings, adhesives, batteries, and spot removers. 
 
3. A rule essentially banning the use and production of perchloroethylene (PCE) a chemical used for 
the production of fluorinated compounds, as a solvent for dry cleaning and vapor degreasing, in 
 
21  
Congressional Review Act, US Government Accountability Office, https://www.gao.gov/legal/other-legal-work/congressional-review-
act#s-skipLinkTargetForFilterOptions, accessed May 8, 2024.  Note all rules are required to be submitted to the GAO but agencies do 
not always do so.  According to the GAO, The Congressional Review Act (CRA) requires agencies to submit rules to Congress and to 
GAO, as well as to delay the effective date of certain rules to provide Congress an opportunity to review and possibly disapprove of 
rules before they become effective. Twenty-eight percent of the final economically significant rules issued during the Trump 
Administration (37 of 131) did not meet at least one timing requirement of the CRA or were not submitted to GAO or to Congress. 
Similar data for the Biden Administration are not yet available. 
22  
Federal Register, National Archives and Records Administration at: https://www.federalregister.gov/.  Accessed May 9, 2024 

 
 
catalyst regeneration in petrochemical manufacturing.  It is also a component of a variety of 
commercial and consumer applications including adhesives, paints and coatings, aerosol 
degreasers, brake cleaners, aerosol lubricants, sealants, stone polish, stainless steel polish, and 
wipe cleaners.  
 
4. A rule essentially banning the use and production of Methylene Chloride, a chemical that is 
widely used solvent in a variety of consumer and commercial applications including adhesives 
and sealants, automotive products, and paint and coating removers.  
Each of these rules impose specific costs on chemical manufacturers (which are then passed on to 
distributors), as well as on distributors themselves.  The cost and the impact of each rule is shown in 
Table 1 below.

APPENDIX B 
Demand Model Methodology 
JDA’s Regulatory Assessment Model (RAM) is an updated version of a multi-market demand model first 
developed by the American Economics Group (AEG) under contract with Philip Morris. It was completely 
rebuilt by Dr. Hyeyeon Park in 2001, and its structure was updated by JDA in 2019. The model was 
presented to the National Conference of State Legislatures, Senior Fiscal Analysts Seminar in Portland, 
Maine, on September 4, 1999, where it was well received. In fact, at that time, many state fiscal analysts 
asked if the model could be made available to them as a forecasting tool. The results from the model 
were also presented to the Tax Foundation Excise Tax Seminar, held in Jacksonville, Florida, on January 
12, 2001, as part of a larger discussion on the economic impact of tobacco taxes. 
Since then, the RAM model has been modified to work with nearly any product or market. It is designed 
to measure product sales in a multi-state market structure with differential pricing. The general 
methodology is a two-stage estimation of the demand equation linked to a non-linear programming 
model of import and export patterns. Data for the model comes from the 2023 Economic Impact Model 
of the Chemical Distribution Industry, as well as from the US Census Bureau, the Bureau of Economic 
Analysis, the Bureau of Transportation Statistics Commodity Flow Survey, and JDA research. Caliper 
Corporation was used to estimate distances between states.  
Estimates on what sales should be in each state are developed first. In this case, both demand and prices 
come directly from the Impact model. If cross-border sales were observable, the calculations would be 
complete; however, since they are not, the model must estimate them through non-linear programming 
techniques that solve the 51 demand functions simultaneously. The model adjusts the cross-price 
elasticities between states to balance the actual sales with expected demand. 
Demand elasticities are calculated using a logarithmic demand curve with a base of -0.805, which is an 
average for chemical products.23 
Once the linear program model balances, the model can be shocked with either new prices or demand 
values. By rebalancing the model following the shock, it is possible to calculate demand response 
estimates across all states (as well as cross-border sales changes).  
Revenue and job impacts can then be estimated through linear extrapolation. 
 
 
23  
See Gallaway, Michael, et. al., Short-run and long-run industry-level estimates of US Armington elasticities, North American Journal 
of Economics and Finance, March 2003.

Dear Ms. Masten: 
 
Albemarle Corporation (Albemarle) is a global manufacturer of specialty chemicals, among whose 
product line includes 1-bromopropane (1-BP) as an important part of our portfolio. As a 
manufacturer and in conjunction with leading experts and key users of our products, Albemarle has 
developed considerable expertise with respect to 1-BP. Albemarle appreciates the opportunity to 
provide comments on the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA) proposed risk 
management rule for 1-BP (CAS RN®106-94-5), issued under the Toxic Substances Control Act 
(TSCA).1  
 
Albemarle has actively engaged in the administrative process leading up to EPA’s proposed risk 
management rule on 1-BP and appreciates the opportunity to comment on this proposal.2 
Albemarle’s comments focus on the following aspects of the proposal: 
- 
The science that serves as the basis for EPA’s proposed risk management rule for 1-BP (i.e., 
systematic review and EPA’s evaluation of the carcinogenic mode of action [MOA] for 1-BP).  
- 
EPA’s existing chemical exposure limit (ECEL) and the surrogate data EPA used for informing 
its dermal exposure estimates to workers.  
- 
EPA’s unreasonable cancer risk determinations, as informed by its inhalation unit risk (IUR) 
derived from lung tumors in female mice, with a focus on vapor degreasing operations.  
- 
A request to reconsider the proposed ECEL of 0.05 ppm (0.25 mg/m3) using data that are not of 
questionable relevance to humans.

Systematic Review 
 
EPA completed a TSCA Section 6 risk evaluation on 1-BP (the 2020 1-BP RE) in August 2020.3 
EPA stated in the 2020 1-BP RE that “To meet [the] TSCA § 26 science standards, EPA used the 
TSCA systematic review process described in the Application of Systematic Review in TSCA 
Risk Evaluations document [the 2018 SR Document] [citation omitted].”4 The 2018 SR 
Document was subsequently peer reviewed by the U.S. National Academies of Sciences, 
Engineering, and Medicine (NASEM), which concluded that the approach did not meet the 
criteria of “comprehensive, workable, objective, and transparent” and that “The OPPT approach 
to systematic review does not adequately meet the state-of-practice.”5 In response to the NASEM 
review, EPA revised its systematic review method. On December 20, 2021, EPA released the 
2021 Draft Systematic Review Protocol Supporting TSCA Risk Evaluations for Chemical 
Substances Version 1.0 (the 2021 Draft SR Protocol) for public comment.6 EPA acknowledged 
in the 2021 Draft SR Protocol that:7 
 
Previously [in the 2018 SR Document], EPA did not have a complete clear and 
documented TSCA systematic review (SR) Protocol. EPA is addressing this lack of a 
priori protocol by releasing [the 2021 Draft Protocol]. 
 
EPA’s acknowledgment supports that it did not comply with the scientific standards under TSCA 
Section 26 and the implementing regulations at that time under 40 C.F.R. Part 702, which 
stated:8 
 
Weight of scientific evidence means a systematic review method, applied in a manner 
suited to the nature of the evidence or decision, that uses a pre-established protocol 
to comprehensively, objectively, transparently, and consistently, identify and evaluate 
each stream of evidence, including strengths, limitations, and relevance of each study 
and to integrate evidence as necessary and appropriate based upon strengths, 
limitations, and relevance. [emphasis added]

Carcinogenic MOA and Human Relevance of Female Mouse 
Lung Tumors 
 
EPA derived a lifetime cancer existing chemical exposure limit (ECELcancer) of 0.05 ppm (0.25 
mg/m3), based on the incidence of alveolar/bronchiolar adenoma or carcinoma in female mice 
from two-year carcinogenicity studies performed on male and female mice and rats.14,15 EPA did 
not, however, provide a scientific justification for the selection of these data over the incidence 
data in male rats (e.g., keratoacanthoma squamous cell carcinoma) or female rats (e.g., rare large 
intestine adenoma).16 Albemarle acknowledges that uncertainty in the human relevance of a 
carcinogenic MOA may be an appropriate reason for selecting a particular data set from which to 
evaluate unreasonable risks. There are, however, procedural requirements for making such a 
determination, which EPA did not comply with. 
 
EPA stated the following in the 2020 1-BP RE under the section header titled “Weight of the 
Scientific Evidence for Cancer”:17  
 
The exact mechanism/mode of action of 1-BP carcinogenesis is not established. 
Evidence for a [mutagenic mode of action] MMOA is suggestive but inconclusive. 
Other potential mechanisms that may contribute to the multi-stage process of 
carcinogenesis by 1-BP include oxidative stress, immunosuppression, and cell 
proliferation. Overall, there is evidence in high-quality animal studies to support 
carcinogenicity following 1-BP exposure. Therefore, these endpoints were carried 
forward for dose-response analysis. 
 
EPA did not, however, perform an evaluation of the carcinogenic MOA(s) for these data, 
pursuant to its 2005 “Guidelines for Carcinogen Risk Assessment” (the 2005 Guidelines).18 
Instead, EPA prepared an approximate one-page summary on the carcinogenic MOA for 1-BP 
under a section header titled “Possible Mode of Action for 1-BP Toxicity”.19 This does not, 
however, satisfy the scientific standards under TSCA Sections 26(h)-(i), which require an 
evaluation consistent with the carcinogenic framework analysis as described in the 2005 
Guidelines.20

EPA also did not cite to or discuss reasonably available information on the carcinogenic MOA(s)

Section 26(k), which states: 
In carrying out sections 4, 5, and 6, the Administrator shall take into consideration 
information relating to a chemical substance or mixture, including hazard and 
exposure information, under the conditions of use, that is reasonably available to the

For example, the human relevance of chemically-induced mouse lung tumors has been a topic of 
scientific discussion for many years, including by EPA scientists, which held a workshop on this

more recent evaluations on the human relevance of chemically-induced mouse lung tumors,

nor peer-reviewed investigations on this topic that pre-dated the 2020 1-BP RE. 
Albemarle acknowledges that EPA may rightfully have a different scientific opinion (DSO) on 
the human relevance of chemically-induced lung tumors in female mice versus the 
interpretations of other scientists. DSOs are, after all, part of the scientific process. Albemarle’s 
concern is that EPA did not provide its DSO, if that is the case, in the 2020 1-BP RE. Rather, 
EPA chose not to discuss scientific interpretations of carcinogenic MOAs in the 2020 1-BP RE, 
which conflicts with the scientific standards under TSCA Section 26 and the transparency 
requirements of EPA’s scientific integrity policy.27

Existing Chemical Exposure Limit 
 
EPA stated in the 2024 Proposed Rule that it “is proposing an ECEL of 0.05 parts per million 
(ppm) (0.25 mg/ m3) for inhalation exposures to 1-BP as an 8-hour TWA. This ECEL is based on 
the cancer inhalation unit risk (IUR) at a risk level of 1 × 10-4, which is the most sensitive 
hazard value across acute, chronic non-cancer, and cancer endpoints. [emphasis added]”28 
Albemarle agrees with EPA that its IUR is the most sensitive hazard value. There is, however, no 
requirement under TSCA for EPA to use the most sensitive hazard value, rather EPA is required 
to use the best available science and weight of scientific evidence, per TSCA Sections 26(h)-(i). 
EPA is also required to conduct risk evaluations and to make risk determinations “without 
consideration of costs or other nonrisk factors”, per TSCA Section 6(b)(4)(A). EPA’s decision to 
regulate from the most sensitive hazard value conflicts with the scientific standards under TSCA 
Section 26 and implicitly incorporates a precautionary approach (i.e., a nonrisk factor) into its 
decision making. 
 
As discussed in the previous section, EPA did not perform an evaluation of the carcinogenic 
MOA(s) for the available carcinogenicity data in rats and mice, per the requirements under 
TSCA Sections 26(h)-(i). The implications of EPA’s failure to satisfy these legal requirements 
are discussed below. 
 
EPA calculated human equivalent concentrations (HECs) for the carcinogenicity data on rats and 
mice, as shown in Table 1.29

EPA also derived an acute non-cancer exposure limit (ELacute) of 0.17 ppm (0.86 mg/m3).32 EPA 
did not include this limit as part of its proposed risk management rule on 1-BP, noting that the 
ECEL of 0.05 ppm would “address unreasonable risks for chronic cancer and non-cancer and 
acute non-cancer inhalation endpoints.”33 The ELacute does, however, warrant discussion, as it 
exemplifies Albemarle’s previous statements on EPA’s decision making in the 2020 1-BP RE 
that conflicts with the scientific standards under TSCA Section 26. 
 
For the ELacute, EPA applied a composite uncertainty factor of 100×, which included a factor of 
10× for intraspecies uncertainty (i.e., human-to-human variability) and 10× for interspecies 
uncertainty (i.e., uncertainty with extrapolating from animals to humans). EPA derived the 
ELacute from an HEC, which included application of a dosimetric adjustment factor (DAF). The 
interspecies uncertainty factor is composed of two subcomponents (i.e., toxicokinetics [TK] ≈ 3× 
and toxicodynamics [TD] ≈ 3×). When a DAF is applied for deriving an HEC, the TK 
subcomponent of the interspecies uncertainty factor is reduced from 3× to 1×. The remaining 
value of 3× accounts for the TD subcomponent. Therefore, EPA should have applied a composite 
uncertainty factor of 30×, not 100×, when deriving the ELacute, per its 1994 guidance titled 
 
30  Id. at 226. 
31  EPA (2021b), supra note 14. 
32  EPA (2021b), supra note 14. 
33  89 Fed. Reg. 65066, at 65072-65073. 

 
7 
 
Methods for Derivation of Inhalation Reference Concentrations and Application of Inhalation 
Dosimetry (the 1994 RFC Guidance).34 EPA provided the following justification for its departure 
from the 1994 RFC Guidance:35 
 
[A] portion of the toxicokinetic uncertainty may be accounted for by the calculation 
of an HEC accounting for the relative blood/air partition coefficients across species 
and application of a dosimetric adjustment factor [DAF] as outlined in the RfC 
methodology [citation to 1994 RFC Guidance omitted]; however, an UFA of 10 is 
retained to account for additional toxicokinetic differences that remain unaccounted 
for. 1-BP is irritating to the respiratory tract and rodents exhibit physiological 
responses (such as reflex bradypnea) that differ from humans and may alter uptake 
due to hyper- or hypoventilation, resulting in decreased internal dose in rodents 
relative to the applied concentration. Therefore, an UFA of 10 is retained to account 
for toxicokinetic differences [citation omitted]. 
 
Albemarle notes that the harmonized (i.e., legally binding) classification for 1-BP in the 
European Union (EU) includes the hazard class and category code for STOT SE 3, H335, “May 
cause respiratory irritation”.36 Albemarle’s concern is not, however, based on EPA’s 
determination that 1-BP may cause respiratory tract irritation. Its concern is that EPA selectively 
applied the above justification to 1-BP yet followed the 1994 RFC Guidance in its final risk 
evaluations on other chemical substances with this same hazard concern. For example, the 
harmonized classification for 1,4-dioxane in the EU includes the hazard class and category code 
for STOT SE 3, H335, “May cause respiratory irritation.”37 In the final risk evaluation for 1,4-
dioxane, EPA applied a reduced value of 3× for UFA, after application of a DAF.38 EPA does 
not, however, provide a scientific justification why hypo- or hyperventilation would not also be a 
potential concern in this example, thereby warranting application of a 10× value for UFA, as it 
applied to 1-BP. 
 
Albemarle is concerned that EPA’s proposed risk management rule on 1-BP is based on a 
fundamentally flawed risk evaluation (i.e., the 2020 1-BP RE) that does not satisfy the scientific 
standards under TSCA Sections 26(h)-(i). Albemarle believes that the above examples support 
these concerns.
